Learn English/Parts of speech/06-prepositions/catalog

Dependent prepositions: adjective patterns#

The prepositions you do not get to choose — the ones an adjective drags along behind it, so that good takes at, afraid takes of, and married takes to, for no reason anyone can give.

← The grammar of prepositions · All categories · Verb patterns

Everywhere else in this dictionary a preposition means something, and picking the right one is a matter of seeing the relationship clearly: in encloses, after follows, despite concedes. This file is the exception, because here the preposition is fixed by the word in front of it — the only reason good takes at while ready takes for and aware takes of is that four hundred years of use settled it that way, and the pairs have to be learned as units, the way irregular verbs are learned. What can be learned systematically is what may follow the preposition: since these are real prepositions, a verb after one has to be a gerund and never an infinitive (accustomed to driving, not ✗ accustomed to drive), and that single rule is the highest-value thing in the file. The Contrast line in each entry does the other half of the work, because a great many of these adjectives take two or three prepositions with genuinely different meanings — good at / good for / good with, concerned about / concerned with, familiar with / familiar to.

What lives here, and what lives next door. This file holds the adjective patterns, plus the handful of verb phrases caught in the same fixed to — look forward to above all. The verb patterns, including the sets where swapping the preposition changes the meaning outright, are catalogued in Dependent prepositions: verb patterns rather than repeated here: hear about / from / of, agree with / to / on, look at / for / after / into, think about / of, talk to / with / about, wait for / on, and care about / for. The plain prepositions themselves — at, of, to, for, with, about — are explained in the core files and are only linked to from here, never re-explained.

The two traps this file is built around#

The gerund rule, and the to that is not an infinitive#

The word to has two jobs in English, and they look identical on the page. In I want to leave, to is the marker of an infinitive and a bare verb has to follow it. In I object to leaving, to is a preposition and a noun-like thing has to follow it — a noun, a pronoun, or a gerund. Nothing in the spelling tells you which one you have; you have to know it about the word in front.

The test is simple and it never fails: put a noun after the to. If the result is grammatical, the to is a preposition and a verb in that slot must be -ing.

PhraseNoun testVerdict
accustomed toaccustomed to the noise ✓preposition → accustomed to working nights
used to (adjective)used to the noise ✓preposition → used to working nights
look forward tolook forward to the weekend ✓preposition → look forward to hearing from you
opposed toopposed to the plan ✓preposition → opposed to raising rates
committed tocommitted to the project ✓preposition → committed to finishing it
dedicated todedicated to her students ✓preposition → dedicated to teaching
close toclose to the end ✓preposition → close to quitting
subject tosubject to review ✓preposition → subject to being audited
want to✗ want to the noiseinfinitive marker → want to work
able to✗ able to the noiseinfinitive marker → able to work
ready to✗ ready to the noiseinfinitive marker → ready to work

The errors this prevents are the standard ones in advanced English, and they survive in the writing of people who are otherwise fluent:

  • ✗ I look forward to hear from you. → ✓ I look forward to hearing from you.
  • ✗ She isn't accustomed to drive on the left. → ✓ accustomed to driving on the left.
  • ✗ We're committed to improve service. → ✓ committed to improving service.
  • ✗ He's opposed to change the schedule. → ✓ opposed to changing the schedule.
  • ✗ I'm interested to buy your car. → ✓ interested in buying your car.

The same rule governs every other preposition in the file, but to is the only one that can be mistaken for something else, which is why nearly all the damage happens there. After of, at, for, in, on, with, and about, no one is tempted: nobody writes ✗ capable of do it or ✗ good at cook.

The used to ambiguity#

Used to is two unrelated constructions that are spelled the same and, in most American speech, pronounced the same — /ˈjustə/, with the s of loose, not the z of lose.

1. The habitual past — a semi-modal, followed by a bare infinitive. I used to smoke. This is about a discontinued habit, it has no present tense, and its subject cannot be be: ✗ I am used to smoke. Its negative and question forms drop the d in American writing — didn't use to, did she use to — and never used to is the commoner negative in speech.

2. The adjective used, followed by the preposition to. I'm used to the noise. I'm used to working nights. This is about familiarity, it appears with be, get, and become, and a verb after it must be a gerund. It is a plain synonym of accustomed to.

The two are told apart by what sits in front and what sits behind:

Habitual pastAdjective (= accustomed)
Frontsubject alone: I used tobe / get / become: I'm used to, I got used to
Behindbare verb: used to smokenoun or -ing: used to the smoke, used to smoking
Tensepast onlyany tense
Negativedidn't use to, never used toisn't used to, wasn't used to

So I used to drive to work means the speaker no longer does; I'm used to driving to work means the speaker finds it normal; and I got used to driving to work means the speaker came, over time, to find it normal. Both entries are in the A–Z below, at used to and accustomed to.

Different from, different than, different to#

The one place in this file where a usage question is live rather than settled, and where the honest American answer has three parts.

  • Different from is the standard American choice and is never wrong. Before a noun or pronoun it is what careful writing uses: Her account is different from his.
  • Different than is common, respectable American English, and it is the better choice before a clause, where from forces a clumsy what: The job turned out different than I expected beats different from what I expected in speech and is defensible in print. Before a bare noun (different than his) some editors still object, so from is the safer default there.
  • Different to is British. It is not an error in London and it is not used in the United States; American readers hear it as foreign.

Differ takes from only — ✗ differs than. And the comparative-conjunction than is treated as a word in its own right at than. See the full entry at different from.

A–Z: adjective + preposition#

absent from#

Pronunciation
/ˈæbsənt frʌm/ · AB-sunt from · weak /ˈæbsənt frəm/
Русский
отсутствующий где-л. — не быть там, где ожидалось; не встречаться в тексте или списке
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral, tilting formal; the everyday word is missing
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming a place, an occasion, or a body of work (absent from school, absent from the record) — never a gerund and never a clause. There is no ✗ absent from being there.
Pattern
be absent from [place/event] · [noun] absent from [noun] · conspicuously / notably / entirely absent from [noun]
Variants
missing from is the neutral everyday equivalent and takes the same complement. Away from covers only the physical sense.
Contrast
✗ absent of and ✗ absent in are errors here — the fixed preposition is from. Do not confuse the adjective with the marginal preposition absent meaning "in the absence of" (absent a court order, the file stays sealed), which takes a noun with no from at all and is a different word; see absent. Against away from, which reports a body somewhere else, absent from reports a gap where something was expected.

Not present where it was supposed to be — and, in the extended use, not to be found in a text, a list, or an argument.

  1. Two jurors were absent from the courtroom when the verdict came in.
  2. Any mention of the earlier settlement is absent from the report.
  3. She has been absent from work since Thursday.
  4. Warmth was conspicuously absent from his reply.
  5. That name is absent from every version of the guest list I've seen.
See also
free of, short of, lacking in, from

accountable to, accountable for#

Pronunciation
/əˈkaʊntəbəl tu, əˈkaʊntəbəl fɔr/ · uh-KOWN-tuh-bul too, uh-KOWN-tuh-bul for · weak /əˈkaʊntəbəl tə/ and /əˈkaʊntəbəl fər/ — in running speech the whole tail flattens to "uh-KOWN-tuh-bul-tuh" and "uh-KOWN-tuh-bul-fur"
Русский
подотчётный кому-л. (to), ответственный за что-л. (for)
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the standard word in management, government, and journalism about both
Complement
after to, a person or body that can demand the explanation — a noun or pronoun only, never a gerund (✗ accountable to hiring); after for, the thing you must explain — a noun phrase or a gerund (accountable for keeping the receipts), and never a to-infinitive (✗ accountable for to keep the receipts). No that-clause attaches to either.
Pattern
be accountable to [person / body] · be accountable for [thing / verb-ing] · hold [someone] accountable for [thing] · accountable to [person] for [thing] — both slots at once · directly / ultimately / personally accountable
Variants
answerable to and answerable for split exactly the same way and are the slightly more formal, British-leaning twins. Responsible to / responsible for is the everyday pair and divides identically.
Contrast
To names the one who can demand an accounting; for names the thing accounted for. She is accountable to the board* says who can call her in; She is accountable for the shortfall says what she will be asked about. The two slots can be filled at once — accountable to Congress for every dollar — which is the clearest proof they are not alternatives. Against responsible for, which can mean merely "caused it," accountable for always implies somebody is entitled to an explanation. ✗ accountable of and ✗ accountable with are errors.

Obliged to explain and justify — to whoever may demand it, and for whatever falls inside your charge.

  1. The commissioner is accountable to a board of five elected members.
  2. Every site manager is accountable for the safety of the crew on shift.
  3. Somebody is going to be held accountable for losing the original file.
  4. In a co-op, the staff are accountable to the residents, not to a landlord.
  5. She is accountable to the state for every dollar of the grant.
See also
answerable to, responsible for, liable for, liable to, subject to, to

accustomed to#

Pronunciation
/əˈkʌstəmd tu/ · uh-KUSS-tumd too · weak /əˈkʌstəmd tə/
Русский
привыкший к — после to герундий, не инфинитив
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; used to is the everyday version
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (accustomed to working late) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ accustomed to work late is the classic error; the to here is a preposition, not an infinitive marker, which the noun test proves: accustomed to the work ✓. A that-clause is impossible.
Pattern
be accustomed to [noun / verb-ing] · become / grow / get accustomed to [noun / verb-ing] · accustom [someone] to [noun / verb-ing]
Variants
used to — identical in meaning, far commoner, informal to neutral. Habituated to and inured to are the clinical and the grim versions; inured to implies the thing endured is unpleasant.
Contrast
Accustomed to + -ing versus the habitual past used to + bare verb is the pair to keep straight: I'm accustomed to driving (I find it normal) is not I used to drive (I no longer do). An older accustomed to do survives in nineteenth-century prose and in a few frozen legal phrases, but in modern American English it reads as a mistake — write the gerund. Note also that the adjective describes a settled state while get accustomed to describes the process of arriving at it.

Used to something by repetition, so that it no longer registers as strange or difficult.

  1. She's accustomed to the noise from the freeway and sleeps right through it.
  2. Crews here are accustomed to working in twenty below.
  3. It took a month to get accustomed to the new software.
  4. He is not accustomed to being interrupted.
  5. Passengers accustomed to the old schedule kept showing up an hour early.
See also
used to, familiar with, comfortable with, look forward to, to

acquainted with#

Pronunciation
/əˈkweɪntɪd wɪθ/ · uh-KWAYN-tid with · weak /əˈkweɪntəd wəθ/, the final consonant often voiced to /wəð/ before a vowel
Русский
знакомый с — с человеком или с предметом, темой
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal-leaning; in speech Americans say know or familiar with and save acquainted with for introductions and legal statements
Complement
a noun phrase or pronoun naming a person, a subject, a document, or a place. A gerund is grammatical but rare and stiff (acquainted with running a floor crew), and a to-infinitive is impossible — ✗ acquainted with to run the crew. The noun test settles it: acquainted with the crew ✓, so with is doing ordinary prepositional work.
Pattern
be acquainted with [person / subject] · get / become acquainted with · acquaint [someone] with [noun] · be well / slightly / only vaguely acquainted with · make the acquaintance of — the noun frame, with of
Variants
familiar with is the neutral everyday synonym; conversant with is the formal one for a body of knowledge; know is the plain verb and takes a direct object with no preposition at all.
Contrast
Against familiar with, which reports how much you know, acquainted with keeps a trace of the introduction — you become acquainted with a person by meeting them, and the phrase is faintly cool about how well you know them. ✗ acquainted to is an error in every frame, including the causative: you acquaint someone with the rules, never ✗ to the rules. Note that the related noun changes the preposition twice over: an acquaintance with the material, but an acquaintance of mine.

Introduced to someone, or informed enough about a subject to find your way around it.

  1. I'm only slightly acquainted with her husband.
  2. Anyone acquainted with the case knows the timeline doesn't work.
  3. It took the new hire a week to get acquainted with the filing system.
  4. The judge is thoroughly acquainted with the record and won't need the summary.
  5. We became acquainted with the neighbors over a fence repair neither of us wanted.
See also
familiar with, conversant with, cognizant of, aware of, with

addicted to#

Pronunciation
/əˈdɪktɪd tu/ · uh-DIK-tid too · weak /əˈdɪktɪd tə/
Русский
зависимый от, подсевший на — о наркотике или, шутливо, о привычке
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral in the clinical sense; casually hyperbolic in the other (addicted to that show)
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (addicted to checking her phone) — never a to-infinitive (✗ addicted to check) and never a clause.
Pattern
be / become / get addicted to [noun / verb-ing] · addict [someone] to [noun] · addicted to [substance]
Variants
hooked on is the informal equivalent and takes on, not to. Dependent on is the medical term for the physical half of addiction.
Contrast
✗ addicted with and ✗ addicted of are errors. Against hooked on, which is slang and almost always light-hearted, addicted to carries the clinical sense unless the context is plainly joking. Note that the noun keeps the preposition — an addiction to painkillers — while the everyday alternative changes it: a dependence on painkillers.

Unable to stop taking or doing something, whether that is meant literally or as a joke about a habit.

  1. He was addicted to prescription painkillers for two years.
  2. Half the office is addicted to that cold brew from downstairs.
  3. Kids who get addicted to scrolling sleep about an hour less.
  4. She's addicted to buying supplies she already owns.
  5. The clinic treats patients addicted to opioids and alcohol alike.
See also
hooked on, dependent on, obsessed with, crazy about, to

adjacent to#

Pronunciation
/əˈdʒeɪsənt tu/ · uh-JAY-sunt too · weak /əˈdʒeɪsənt tə/
Русский
прилегающий к, соседний с — граничащий вплотную
Type
adjective + preposition, working as a complex preposition of place
Register
formal to technical — zoning ordinances, real-estate listings, radiology reports, geometry; conversation says next to
Complement
a noun phrase or pronoun naming a place or a physical thing — never a gerund (✗ adjacent to parking) and never a clause. Used attributively with no complement at all (the adjacent room), it is a plain adjective and not a preposition; that use is outside this entry.
Pattern
be adjacent to [place] · [noun] adjacent to [noun] · immediately / directly / physically adjacent to · the adjacent [noun] — attributive, no preposition
Variants
next to is the everyday equivalent; abutting and contiguous with are the surveyor's and the statute's words; bordering on adds a shared edge and carries a figurative use (bordering on rude) that adjacent to does not have.
Contrast
Adjacent to claims a shared boundary; close to claims only nearness. Two lots adjacent to each other touch; two lots close to each other need not. In American zoning and title work that difference decides cases, which is why the formal word survives. ✗ adjacent with* and ✗ adjacent of are errors. The purely spatial prepositions this competes with — next to, beside, by — are catalogued in place and position.

Sharing a border or a wall with something — touching it, or as near as makes no difference.

  1. The lot adjacent to the school was rezoned for townhouses.
  2. Park in the garage adjacent to the courthouse, not the lot across the street.
  3. The mass is adjacent to the artery, which is why they won't operate.
  4. Two rooms adjacent to the kitchen were knocked together into an office.
  5. In the figure, the shaded square is adjacent to the right angle.
See also
close to, parallel to, perpendicular to, proximal to, distal to, to

adverse to#

Pronunciation
/ædˈvɜrs tu/ · ad-VURSS too; also AD-vurss attributively · weak /ædˈvɜrs tə/
Русский
противоречащий интересам, неблагоприятный для — юр./книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; an American meets it in legal filings (a position adverse to the trustee), in medical reporting (adverse event), and in finance, essentially never in conversation
Complement
a noun phrase naming an interest, a party, or a position — never a gerund (✗ adverse to signing) and never a to-infinitive. The word is also common attributively with no complement (adverse weather, an adverse ruling, adverse possession), which is the adjective alone.
Pattern
[noun] adverse to [interest / party] · take a position adverse to [noun] · adverse [noun] — attributive · adversely affect [noun] — the adverb, with no preposition
Variants
contrary to and opposed to cover the general sense in plain English; hostile to covers the personal one; unfavorable to is the neutral choice for rulings and conditions.
Contrast
Adverse is not averse, and this is the single commonest educated mix-up in American English. Averse to* means personally disinclined and is what you want after I'm not: ✓ I'm not averse to a second cup, ✗ I'm not adverse to a second cup. Adverse to means working against someone's interest, and its subject is a condition, a ruling, or a party — not a feeling. Note also the frozen legal compounds, where no to follows at all: adverse party, adverse witness, adverse possession.

Running against someone's interest — of a ruling, a fact, a condition, or an opposing party.

  1. Counsel disclosed a client whose interests are adverse to the estate's.
  2. The ruling was adverse to every party except the insurer.
  3. Testimony adverse to the defense came from its own expert.
  4. A finding adverse to the agency would cost it the contract.
  5. Anything adverse to the bondholders has to appear in the filing.
See also
opposed to, prejudicial to, detrimental to, repugnant to, to

afraid of#

Pronunciation
/əˈfreɪd ʌv/ · uh-FRAYD uv · weak /əˈfreɪd əv/, and before a consonant often /əˈfreɪd ə/
Русский
боящийся чего-л. — в русском «бояться» + родительный падеж
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (afraid of falling) — never a to-infinitive after of. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition (afraid to ask) and a that-clause (afraid that we're too late), but those are different frames, not alternatives inside this one.
Pattern
be afraid of [noun / verb-ing] · be afraid to [verb] · be afraid (that) [clause] · be afraid for [someone] · afraid of [someone] -ing (afraid of the roof collapsing)
Variants
scared of is the everyday synonym, frightened of the slightly more formal one, terrified of the intensified one — all three take of.
Contrast
Afraid of doing versus afraid to do is a real difference. Afraid of falling names a fear of something that might happen to you; afraid to jump names a reluctance to act. I was afraid of waking her is about the risk; I was afraid to wake her is about hesitating. And afraid for changes the target entirely — afraid for the children means you fear on their behalf, not that you fear them. ✗ afraid from is an error.

Frightened of something, or unwilling to do something because of what might follow.

  1. She's been afraid of dogs since she was bitten at six.
  2. He was afraid of saying the wrong thing in front of the whole department.
  3. I'm not afraid of hard work — I'm afraid of pointless work.
  4. They were afraid for their parents, who lived two blocks from the fire.
  5. Don't be afraid of asking; the worst they can say is no.
See also
scared of, frightened of, nervous about, worried about, of

amenable to#

Pronunciation
/əˈminəbəl tu/ · uh-MEE-nuh-bul too; the older uh-MEN-uh-bul survives in law · weak /əˈminəbəl tə/
Русский
готовый согласиться на; о вещи — поддающийся (лечению, анализу); книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; the "willing" sense is ordinary business English, the "yields to" sense belongs to science, medicine, and law
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund — amenable to moving the date ✓ — and never a to-infinitive: ✗ amenable to move the date. The noun test proves the point: amenable to the change ✓, so the to here is a preposition and the -ing form is obligatory.
Pattern
be amenable to [proposal / verb-ing] · be amenable to [treatment / analysis / suit] · find [someone] amenable to [noun] · perfectly / entirely / not especially amenable to
Variants
open to and receptive to are the everyday equivalents in the willingness sense; susceptible to and susceptible of cover the "yields to" sense; tractable covers it with no preposition at all.
Contrast
The subject decides the sense. With a person, amenable to means willing to go along: she's amenable to a later start. With a thing, it means it yields to some treatment or procedure: amenable to surgery, amenable to statistical analysis, and in law amenable to suit — subject to a court's power. Against opposed to, which is a settled position, amenable to reports a door left open. ✗ amenable with is an error.

Willing to go along with something, when the subject is a person; open to being treated, analyzed, or sued, when it is a thing.

  1. The seller turned out to be amenable to closing two weeks early.
  2. Nobody on the committee is amenable to another round of edits.
  3. The infection proved amenable to a two-week course of antibiotics.
  4. That question isn't amenable to a yes-or-no answer.
  5. A foreign parent may still be amenable to suit in the state where it sells.
See also
susceptible to, susceptible of, conducive to, opposed to, subject to, to

analogous to#

Pronunciation
/əˈnæləɡəs tu/ · uh-NAL-uh-gus too · weak /əˈnæləɡəs tə/. The g is hard /ɡ/, unlike the /dʒ/ of the noun analogy — ✗ "uh-NAL-uh-jus" is a common misreading
Русский
аналогичный чему-л. — сходный по функции или по структуре отношений
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal and scholarly — scientific writing, legal reasoning from precedent, academic prose; conversation says like
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (analogous to running the trial twice) — never a to-infinitive (✗ analogous to run the trial twice) and never a that-clause. A wh-clause is fine: analogous to what happens in the second reactor.
Pattern
be analogous to [noun / verb-ing] · a case analogous to [noun] · closely / roughly / only loosely analogous to · by analogy to [noun] — the noun frame
Variants
analogous with turns up but is not the standard American choice; comparable to and parallel to are the neutral alternatives; the noun goes both ways — an analogy to or an analogy with, catalogued in noun patterns.
Contrast
Analogous to claims that two things stand in the same relation, not that they look alike — a heart is analogous to a pump because of what it does, not what it resembles, while similar to claims plain likeness. In biology the word is a term of art opposed to homologous*: a bird's wing and an insect's wing are analogous (same function, separate origins) and not homologous (same origin). ✗ analogous of and ✗ analogous from are errors.

Corresponding in function or in structure of relations, so that reasoning about the one carries over to the other.

  1. The heart is analogous to a pump, though the metaphor gives out quickly.
  2. Her situation is closely analogous to the one the court decided in 2019.
  3. A bird's wing is analogous to an insect's wing but not homologous with it.
  4. Billing by the hour here is analogous to metering electricity.
  5. The claim is roughly analogous to arguing that umbrellas cause rain.
See also
similar to, equivalent to, parallel to, isomorphic to, to

ancillary to#

Pronunciation
/ˈænsəˌlɛri tu/ · AN-suh-lair-ee too · weak /ˈænsəˌlɛri tə/. British English stresses the second syllable, an-SIL-uh-ree; American English does not
Русский
вспомогательный по отношению к, служебный при чём-л. — книжн./юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — hospital billing (ancillary services), contracts (ancillary agreement), and federal courts (ancillary jurisdiction); it is an office and a filing word, not a spoken one
Complement
a noun phrase naming the main thing supported. A gerund is possible in careful writing (ancillary to running the main plant) but rare, and a to-infinitive is impossible: ✗ ancillary to run the plant.
Pattern
be ancillary to [main activity] · [service / agreement] ancillary to [noun] · ancillary [noun] — attributive · merely / purely ancillary to
Variants
incidental to and collateral to are the near twins in legal drafting; subordinate to and secondary to are the plainer versions; auxiliary to is the older and slightly more mechanical one.
Contrast
Ancillary to says a thing exists to support the main thing; incidental to says it merely comes along with it. A hospital's lab is ancillary to patient care because it serves it; the parking fee is incidental to the visit because it just happens alongside. Against subordinate to, which ranks two things, ancillary to* describes a supporting function and implies no chain of command. ✗ ancillary of and ✗ ancillary with are errors.

Supporting the main thing rather than being it — a service, a document, or a proceeding that exists to make the principal one work.

  1. Imaging and lab work are billed as services ancillary to the procedure.
  2. The confidentiality letter is ancillary to the purchase agreement and expires with it.
  3. Storage is ancillary to the shipping business, not a line of its own.
  4. The court kept the counterclaim as a matter ancillary to the main action.
  5. Everything else in the budget is ancillary to payroll.
See also
incidental to, incident to, subordinate to, appurtenant to, to

angry with#

Pronunciation
/ˈæŋɡri wɪθ/ · ANG-gree with · weak /ˈæŋɡri wəθ/
Русский
сердитый на — with на человека, at на вещь, about из-за положения дел
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; mad at is the American conversational default
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun after with and at; noun phrase or gerund after about (angry about being passed over) — never a to-infinitive after any of the three. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: angry that no one told her.
Pattern
be angry with [person] · be angry at [person or thing] · be angry about [situation] · be angry (that) [clause] · get angry with [person] about [thing]
Variants
angry at and angry with are both standard American English before a person; older handbooks insist on with for people and at for things, and that distribution still reads as slightly more careful, but angry at her is unremarkable in American print.
Contrast
The three prepositions split by target. With (and, in American usage, at) takes the person; about takes the situation; at alone is what you use for an inanimate cause (angry at the delay). You can be angry with your brother about the money — both slots filled at once — but ✗ angry with the money is wrong. Compare annoyed with, which follows the same split at lower intensity, and furious with, at higher.

Feeling strong displeasure — with a person, at a thing, or about a state of affairs.

  1. She was angry with her brother for weeks after the funeral.
  2. Don't get angry at the referee; get better at defense.
  3. Everyone in the room was angry about how the vote had been scheduled.
  4. He's still angry with himself for not asking more questions.
  5. I was angry that nobody had bothered to call.
See also
mad at, annoyed with, furious with, upset about, with

annoyed with#

Pronunciation
/əˈnɔɪd wɪθ/ · uh-NOYD with · weak /əˈnɔɪd wəθ/
Русский
раздражённый, недовольный кем-л. — слабее, чем angry
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun after with and at; noun phrase or gerund after about and by (annoyed by having to ask twice) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: annoyed that it took three calls.
Pattern
be annoyed with [person] · be annoyed at / about [thing or situation] · be annoyed by [the thing doing the annoying] · be annoyed (that) [clause] · annoyed with [person] for [verb-ing]
Variants
irritated with/by, irked by, and put out with all sit in the same range; bugged by is the casual American version.
Contrast
Three prepositions, three roles. With takes the person you are annoyed at; about or at takes the situation; by takes the cause acting on you and is the passive-flavored one — annoyed by the noise treats the noise as the agent, while annoyed about the noise treats it as the topic of your complaint. Use for + -ing to name what the person did: annoyed with him for forgetting. ✗ annoyed of is an error.

Mildly angry — the low end of the same scale that runs up through angry to furious.

  1. I'm annoyed with my landlord for ignoring three emails.
  2. She looked annoyed at the interruption but waved him in.
  3. Passengers were annoyed by the constant announcements.
  4. He's annoyed about having to redo the entire form.
  5. Try not to get annoyed with her; she's had a week of this.
See also
angry with, upset about, fed up with, patient with, by

answerable to#

Pronunciation
/ˈænsərəbəl tu/ · AN-sur-uh-bul too · weak /ˈænsərəbəl tə/ — and note the silent w, as in the verb
Русский
подотчётный кому-л., обязанный отчитываться за что-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal, with a British tilt; American English usually reaches for accountable to or the plain reports to, and keeps answerable for rhetorical weight (answerable to no one)
Complement
after to, a noun or pronoun naming the person or body owed the explanation — never a gerund; after for, a noun phrase or a gerund (answerable for having signed it), never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
be answerable to [person / body] · be answerable for [thing / verb-ing] · answerable to [person] for [thing] · answerable to no one — the fixed rhetorical frame
Variants
accountable to / accountable for is the ordinary American equivalent and splits identically. Responsible to / responsible for is the everyday pair. The verb answer itself takes the same two prepositions in the same two senses, with no adjective in between.
Contrast
The split matches accountable to, accountable for exactly: to takes the one who may demand an answer, for takes the matter. What separates the two adjectives is tone — answerable is the older and more moralized word, at home in answerable to history and answerable to the voters, while accountable is the neutral organizational term. Note the trap of the other answerable, meaning "able to be answered" (an answerable charge), which takes no preposition at all. See also the zero-preposition verb entry at answer: you answer to a supervisor but answer a question, with nothing in between.

Obliged to give an account of yourself to someone, or for something.

  1. The inspector general is answerable to Congress, not to the agency she audits.
  2. Elected officials are answerable to the people who put them there.
  3. He behaved as if he were answerable to nobody at all.
  4. The contractor is answerable for every subcontractor on the site.
  5. She is answerable to the trustees for how the endowment is invested.
See also
accountable to, accountable for, responsible for, liable for, liable to, answer, to

antecedent to#

Pronunciation
/ˌæntəˈsidənt tu/ · an-tuh-SEE-dunt too · weak /ˌæntəˈsidənt tə/
Русский
предшествующий чему-л. — во времени или в порядке рассуждения; книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition, often working as a complex preposition
Register
formal and dated — nineteenth-century prose, older judicial opinions, philosophy; an American meets it in quoted authority, not in speech, where the words are before and prior to
Complement
a noun phrase, or a gerund in careful writing (antecedent to filing suit) — never a to-infinitive (✗ antecedent to file suit) and never a clause.
Pattern
[noun] antecedent to [event] · be antecedent to [noun] · logically / temporally antecedent to · the antecedent — the noun, in grammar the word a pronoun points back to
Variants
prior to is the living formal equivalent, previous to the slightly stiffer one, anterior to the exact synonym in the same dated register, and before the plain word that beats all of them.
Contrast
Antecedent to covers both coming earlier in time and coming first in logic, and the second is what keeps it alive in philosophical writing: a duty antecedent to any promise* is not merely earlier, it is more basic. Against previous to and prior to, which are purely temporal, that logical use is the only reason to prefer it. Do not confuse the adjective with the grammatical noun antecedent, which takes of (the antecedent of "which"). For the plain time prepositions see time.

Coming before something else, either in time or in the order of reasoning.

  1. Every condition antecedent to closing had been satisfied by Friday.
  2. The right of way was antecedent to the subdivision and survived it.
  3. Hume's question is whether any idea is antecedent to experience.
  4. Facts antecedent to the merger are outside the scope of the audit.
  5. A duty of care antecedent to the contract may still be owed.
See also
anterior to, posterior to, previous to, preparatory to, subject to, to

anterior to, posterior to#

Pronunciation
/ænˈtɪriər tu, pɑˈstɪriər tu/ · an-TEER-ee-ur too, pah-STEER-ee-ur too · weak /ænˈtɪriər tə/ and /pɑˈstɪriər tə/
Русский
расположенный спереди / сзади от — анат., относительно другой структуры
Type
paired adjectives + preposition
Register
technical — anatomy, radiology, and dentistry, where they are the everyday professional words; plus an archaic formal use for time, found in old legal and scholarly prose and nowhere else
Complement
a noun phrase naming a body structure, or, in the dated temporal use, a date or an event — never a gerund and never a clause. Both words also stand attributively with no complement (the anterior wall, the posterior fossa), which is the plain adjective.
Pattern
[structure] anterior to [structure] · [structure] posterior to [structure] · just / immediately anterior to · anteroposterior — the compound, as in an AP film
Variants
in front of and behind are the plain equivalents; ventral to and dorsal to are the comparative-anatomy pair, which for a four-legged animal is not the same axis; prior to and subsequent to replace the dated temporal use.
Contrast
In American clinical use these are strictly spatial, and the temporal sense is dead outside quotation. Write the incision is anterior to the ear, but not ✗ anterior to the surgery for "before it" unless you are imitating older prose. The pair is also relative, not absolute: a structure is anterior to something, so the object is obligatory — the anterior surface alone is the attributive adjective, not this pattern. In dentistry the plural nouns anteriors and posteriors name the front and back teeth and take no preposition.

Situated toward the front of the body, or toward the back of it, relative to some other structure.

  1. The sternum is anterior to the heart.
  2. The kidneys lie posterior to the intestines, against the back wall.
  3. The lesion sits just anterior to the ear canal.
  4. The esophagus runs posterior to the trachea.
  5. In the older reports, any conveyance anterior to the mortgage took priority — the archaic temporal use.
See also
antecedent to, proximal to, distal to, adjacent to, previous to, to

anxious about#

Pronunciation
/ˈæŋkʃəs əˈbaʊt/ · ANK-shuss uh-BOWT · weak /ˈæŋkʃəs əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈæŋkʃəs ˈbaʊt/
Русский
тревожащийся о чём-л.; с инфинитивом (anxious to) — страстно желающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; slightly more formal than nervous about
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about (anxious about flying) — never a to-infinitive after about. The adjective separately takes a bare to-infinitive (anxious to help), for + noun (anxious for news), and a that-clause (anxious that nothing goes wrong, with an optional subjunctive in formal writing).
Pattern
be anxious about [noun / verb-ing] · be anxious to [verb] · be anxious for [noun] · be anxious (that) [clause]
Variants
nervous about and worried about are the everyday equivalents; apprehensive about is the formal one.
Contrast
Anxious about is worry; anxious to is eagerness. Anxious about starting means dreading it; anxious to start means impatient to begin — the same adjective, opposite emotional loads, decided entirely by the preposition versus the infinitive. Purists once objected to the eager sense altogether, but it is standard American English now. Anxious for splits the difference: anxious for the results is wanting them badly, usually with an edge of worry.

Uneasy about something ahead — and, in the infinitive frame, keen for it to happen.

  1. He's anxious about the biopsy results.
  2. She was anxious about moving so far from her sister.
  3. Parents are anxious about the new attendance policy.
  4. I'm anxious to get started before the weather turns.
  5. Everyone in the waiting room was anxious for news.
See also
nervous about, worried about, eager for, afraid of, about

apprised of#

Pronunciation
/əˈpraɪzd ʌv/ · uh-PRIZED uv · weak /əˈpraɪzd əv/, and before a consonant often /əˈpraɪzd ə/
Русский
поставленный в известность о — офиц.
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
formal — police reports, corporate memoranda, and court filings, where keep the court apprised is a fixed phrase; conversation says told or kept posted
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (apprised of being under investigation) — never a to-infinitive (✗ apprised of to change the date). A that-clause attaches to the verb, not to this frame: the clerk apprised him that the hearing had moved, but ✗ apprised of that the hearing had moved.
Pattern
be apprised of [noun / verb-ing] · keep [someone] apprised of [noun] · apprise [someone] of [noun] — the active verb · fully / duly / promptly apprised of
Variants
informed of and notified of are the plain equivalents and take the same of; advised of is the police and banking version; made aware of is the everyday paraphrase.
Contrast
Apprised is not appraised. To apprise is to inform; to appraise is to value or assess, and it takes a direct object with no preposition: ✓ the jeweler appraised the ring*, ✗ I was appraised of the situation. This misspelling is common enough in American business writing to be worth a deliberate check. Against aware of, which reports a state of knowledge however acquired, apprised of reports that somebody actively told you.

Informed of something by someone whose job it was to tell you.

  1. Keep me apprised of any change in her condition.
  2. The board was never apprised of the second offer.
  3. Counsel will keep the court apprised of the settlement talks.
  4. Residents were apprised of the shutoff two days in advance.
  5. He claims he was not apprised of being named in the complaint.
See also
aware of, cognizant of, conscious of, mindful of, of

appurtenant to#

Pronunciation
/əˈpɜrtənənt tu/ · uh-PUR-tuh-nunt too · weak /əˈpɜrtənənt tə/
Русский
принадлежащий земельному участку — юр., переходит вместе с землёй
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal, and narrowly so — real-property law, deeds, title reports, and condominium declarations; outside a closing binder an American will never meet it
Complement
a noun phrase naming land, an estate, or a right attached to land — never a gerund, never a clause. The word is also used attributively in the frozen term an easement appurtenant, where the noun comes first and no complement follows.
Pattern
an easement appurtenant to [parcel] · [right] appurtenant to [estate] · rights and privileges appurtenant to [noun] · the appurtenances — the noun, in the deed formula together with all appurtenances
Variants
incident to is the broader legal synonym; attached to and belonging to are the plain-English versions; pertaining to covers the looser sense of "having to do with."
Contrast
The point of the word is that the right travels with the land. An easement appurtenant to a parcel passes automatically to the next owner; an easement in gross belongs to a person and does not. Against incident to, which is general, appurtenant to is specific to real property. Do not confuse it with pertinent to, which means relevant: ✗ the question is appurtenant to the issue is not English. ✗ appurtenant of and ✗ appurtenant with are errors.

Attached by law to a piece of land, so that whoever takes the land takes the right along with it.

  1. The driveway easement is appurtenant to the back parcel and passes with the deed.
  2. Parking space 14 is appurtenant to the unit, not separately salable.
  3. Water rights appurtenant to the ranch were valued separately in the estate.
  4. The declaration lists every right appurtenant to ownership of a unit.
  5. A dock permit appurtenant to the lot cannot be transferred to a neighbor.
See also
incident to, ancillary to, incidental to, pertinent to, to

ashamed of#

Pronunciation
/əˈʃeɪmd ʌv/ · uh-SHAYMD uv · weak /əˈʃeɪmd əv/
Русский
стыдящийся чего-л. — «мне стыдно за»
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (ashamed of lying) — never a to-infinitive after of. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive (ashamed to admit it) and a that-clause (ashamed that he'd said nothing).
Pattern
be ashamed of [noun / verb-ing] · be ashamed of [someone] for [verb-ing] · be ashamed to [verb] · be ashamed (that) [clause] · ashamed of oneself
Variants
embarrassed about / by covers the mild social version; ashamed is the moral one. Mortified by is the intensified form.
Contrast
Ashamed of doing versus ashamed to do — the first looks back at something already done, the second hesitates before doing it. I'm ashamed of asking means the asking happened and I regret it; I'm ashamed to ask means I have not asked and would rather not. ✗ ashamed about is not idiomatic for the moral sense — that is embarrassed about's territory — and ✗ ashamed for works only when someone else's conduct is meant (I'm ashamed for you, feeling shame on your behalf).

Feeling guilt or dishonor about something you have done, or about someone connected to you.

  1. She was ashamed of the way she'd spoken to the clerk.
  2. Nobody should be ashamed of needing help.
  3. He was ashamed of himself before the front door closed.
  4. I'm ashamed to say I never opened the envelope.
  5. They were ashamed of their father for the first time in their lives.
See also

astonished at#

Pronunciation
/əˈstɑnɪʃt æt/ · uh-STON-isht at · weak /əˈstɑnɪʃt ət/
Русский
поражённый, изумлённый чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to literary; stronger and rarer than surprised
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at and by (astonished at finding the door open) — never a to-infinitive after either. A bare to-infinitive attaches to the adjective directly (astonished to learn), as does a that-clause (astonished that anyone showed up).
Pattern
be astonished at [noun / verb-ing] · be astonished by [noun] · be astonished to [verb] · be astonished (that) [clause]
Variants
astonished at and astonished by are both standard; at leans toward the reaction, by toward the cause. Amazed at/by, astounded by, and stunned by are the near-neighbors.
Contrast
Same split as surprised and shocked: at frames the thing as what you are reacting to; by frames it as what acted on you — astonished at the price is your reaction to a number, astonished by the news is the news doing something to you. With a verb, the two available frames mean different things: astonished at hearing it reports the moment of hearing, astonished to hear it reports the reaction on hearing. ✗ astonished of and ✗ astonished from are errors.

Struck by something so unexpected that it briefly stops you.

  1. I was astonished at how quickly the line moved.
  2. She was astonished at the size of the bill.
  3. Visitors are always astonished by the acoustics in that room.
  4. He was astonished to find his name on the list.
  5. They were astonished at finding the cabin exactly as they'd left it.
See also
surprised at, shocked at, impressed with, at

available to#

Pronunciation
/əˈveɪləbəl tu/ · uh-VAY-luh-bull too · weak /əˈveɪləbəl tə/
Русский
доступный кому-л. — который можно получить, использовать или к кому можно обратиться
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming a person or group after to; noun phrase or gerund after for (available for hire, available for booking) — never a gerund after to. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive meaning "free to" (available to talk at four), which is a different frame from available to + person.
Pattern
be available to [person] · be available for [purpose / verb-ing] · be available to [verb] · make [something] available to [person] · available from [source] · available at [place]
Variants
open to covers the "not restricted" sense; accessible to the "reachable" one.
Contrast
Available to names who can get it; available for names what it is for. The report is available to members (who may have it) versus The room is available for meetings (what it may be used for). The infinitive frame is a third thing entirely: She's available to answer questions is about her free time, not about who has access to her. ✗ available with is an error, though available in is correct for formats and sizes (available in blue).

Able to be had, used, reached, or spoken to.

  1. Transcripts are available to any student who requests one.
  2. The gym is available for private events on Sundays.
  3. Dr. Alvarez is available to meet on Thursday morning.
  4. That grant is only available to first-time applicants.
  5. Two of the three units are still available for rent.

aware of#

Pronunciation
/əˈwɛr ʌv/ · uh-WAIR uv · weak /əˈwɛr əv/
Русский
осознающий что-л., знающий о — «в курсе»
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, gerund (aware of being watched), or a wh-clause (aware of what it would cost) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective with no of: aware that the deadline had moved, and ✗ aware of that the deadline had moved is an error.
Pattern
be aware of [noun / verb-ing / wh-clause] · be aware (that) [clause] · become / make [someone] aware of [noun] · acutely / dimly / painfully aware of [noun]
Variants
conscious of is the close synonym and behaves identically; cognizant of is the bureaucratic version.
Contrast
The of drops before a that-clause. This is the single error worth watching: ✓ aware of the problem, ✓ aware that there's a problem, ✗ aware of that there's a problem. Against conscious of, which leans toward felt, bodily awareness (conscious of a draft), aware of leans toward knowledge. And note aware has no comparative with more aware of as a fixed pattern — it takes degree adverbs instead: fully, keenly, vaguely aware of.

Knowing that something exists or is the case.

  1. Were you aware of the change in the return policy?
  2. She's aware of how it looks and doesn't care.
  3. He became aware of someone standing behind him.
  4. The board was fully aware that the numbers wouldn't hold.
  5. I'm painfully aware of having missed the obvious answer.
See also

bad at#

Pronunciation
/bæd æt/ · BAD at · weak /bæd ət/
Русский
неумелый в чём-л. — bad at неумелый, bad for вредный, bad with не умеющий обращаться с
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (bad at remembering names) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ bad at remember names and ✗ bad at to remember are both wrong. After for the complement is a person or thing affected, and a gerund is possible (bad for keeping food fresh is odd; bad for your teeth is the norm).
Pattern
be bad at [noun / verb-ing] · be bad for [person / thing] · be bad with [category of thing one handles badly] · be bad about [verb-ing] (habitually negligent)
Variants
poor at is the formal version, terrible at and awful at the emphatic ones, hopeless at and useless at the self-deprecating ones, slow at the specific one. All take at.
Contrast
Three prepositions, three meanings, and they are not interchangeable. Bad at math = unskilled. Bad for you = harmful. Bad with money = unable to handle a whole category well — with is the one used for people, animals, money, and tools (bad with kids, bad with numbers). A fourth frame, bad about + -ing, means chronically failing to do something: I'm bad about returning calls. ✗ bad in math is an error in American English, though bad in bed and bad in a crisis are fixed idioms where in names a situation.

Not good at something, or harmful to something — the split depends entirely on the preposition.

  1. I'm hopelessly bad at spelling anyone's name right the first time.
  2. Standing all day is bad for your back.
  3. He's bad with directions and always has been.
  4. She's bad about answering email between semesters.
  5. Our whole family is bad at small talk.
See also
good at, poor at, terrible at, hopeless at, harmful to, at

based on#

Pronunciation
/beɪst ɑn/ · BAYST on · weak /beɪst ən/
Русский
основанный на — на источнике, фактах или принципе
Type
participial adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (based on assuming the worst) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. ✗ based on that the market recovers must be based on the assumption that the market recovers.
Pattern
be based on [noun / verb-ing] · [noun] based on [noun] · base [something] on [something] · based on [noun], [clause] — the dangling sentence-adverbial use
Variants
based upon is the formal equivalent, common in contracts and older prose. Based in is a different word combination meaning headquartered, not derived: a firm based in Denver.
Contrast
Based on versus based in is the trap: The movie is based on a true story (derived from) versus The company is based in Seattle (located at). ✗ based off of is widespread in American speech and still nonstandard in writing; editors change it to based on. The floating sentence-opener Based on the survey, we cut the budget is criticized as a dangling participle — the budget is not based on the survey, the decision is — and careful writing prefers On the basis of or Judging by. See on the basis of.

Built or derived from a source, a set of facts, or a principle.

  1. The screenplay is based on a magazine article from 1974.
  2. Rates are based on your driving record, not your zip code.
  3. She made the call based on twenty years of doing this.
  4. The whole argument is based on assuming that nobody moves.
  5. Their pricing is based upon volume, tier by tier.
See also
dependent on, related to, subject to, on

beholden to#

Pronunciation
/bɪˈhoʊldən tu/ · bih-HOHL-dun too · weak /bəˈhoʊldən tə/ — the d is usually swallowed into the n, giving "bih-HOHL-un-tuh"
Русский
обязанный кому-л. за услугу и связанный этим — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to literary, and a standing word in American political journalism (beholden to donors); it survives almost nowhere else
Complement
a noun or pronoun naming the person, group, or interest you owe — never a gerund (✗ beholden to giving) and never an infinitive. A second slot with for names the debt: beholden to her for the introduction.
Pattern
be beholden to [person / group] · beholden to [person] for [favor] · beholden to no one — by far the commonest frame · feel / remain beholden to
Variants
indebted to is the neutral equivalent and takes for the same way; obligated to covers the duty; in someone's debt is the everyday paraphrase; under obligation to is the formal noun frame.
Contrast
Beholden to is about owing a favor that constrains you, which is why it is nearly always negative in American use: to say a senator is beholden to* an industry is to say the industry owns the vote. Against obligated to, obliged to, which names a duty that may be perfectly honorable, beholden to names a dependence somebody would rather not admit. Note that the word is archaic outside this frame — beholden is not a participle of behold in modern English, whatever it looks like. ✗ beholden of and ✗ beholden by are errors.

Under an obligation to someone who did you a favor, and constrained by it.

  1. He ran without taking corporate money so he'd be beholden to nobody.
  2. The board is beholden to two donors who between them fund the building.
  3. I don't want to be beholden to my brother-in-law for a job.
  4. Editors who are beholden to advertisers stop printing certain stories.
  5. She remained beholden to the professor who had vouched for her.
See also
obligated to, obliged to, grateful to, answerable to, dependent on, to

binding on, binding upon#

Pronunciation
/ˈbaɪndɪŋ ɑn, ˈbaɪndɪŋ əˈpɑn/ · BYNE-ding on, BYNE-ding uh-PON · weak /ˈbaɪndɪŋ ən/ and /ˈbaɪndɪŋ əˈpɒn/
Русский
обязательный для кого-л. — юр., имеющий обязательную силу
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal and formal; the natural home is a contract, an arbitration clause, or an appellate opinion, and binding upon is the drafter's variant of the same phrase
Complement
a noun phrase naming the person or entity bound — a party, a successor, an agency, a court — and never a gerund (✗ binding on signing) and never a clause. What the obligation is does not go in this slot; that goes in the sentence around it.
Pattern
be binding on [party] · binding upon [party] and [party's successors] — the standard drafting formula · legally / contractually binding on · a binding agreement — attributive, no complement
Variants
binding upon is stylistically identical and commoner in drafted documents; enforceable against is the sharper term for the same idea; bind as a verb takes a direct object with no preposition (the agreement binds the parties).
Contrast
Binding on takes the person bound, while bound by takes the thing that binds — the same fact from the two ends: the arbitration clause is binding on the parties* = the parties are bound by the arbitration clause. Do not confuse either with the temporal upon: binding upon execution means "binding as soon as it is signed," and there the upon is a time preposition with a different job. ✗ binding to and ✗ binding for are errors in this sense.

Having legal force against someone, so that they cannot walk away from it.

  1. The settlement is binding on both parties and their successors.
  2. A ruling from the Ninth Circuit is binding on district courts in that circuit.
  3. The letter of intent is binding upon the seller only as to confidentiality.
  4. Arbitration under this section is final and binding on the employer.
  5. Nothing said in mediation is binding on anyone unless it is written down.
See also
bound by, enforceable against, subject to, incumbent on, incumbent upon, on

bored with#

Pronunciation
/bɔrd wɪθ/ · BORD with · weak /bɔrd wəθ/
Русский
которому скучно от чего-л., наскучивший
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (bored with waiting) — never a to-infinitive. Bored to exists only in the fixed hyperboles bored to death and bored to tears, which are not this pattern.
Pattern
be bored with [noun / verb-ing] · be bored by [the thing that bores you] · get bored with [noun / verb-ing] · bored out of one's mind
Variants
bored with and bored by are both standard American English. Bored of is now extremely common in speech on both sides of the Atlantic and still marked in careful American writing — it began as a British colloquialism, modeled on tired of, and editors change it to with. If you want a safe choice, use with.
Contrast
With takes the thing you have had enough of; by takes the thing actively boring you — bored with the job is your state, bored by his lecture points at the cause, exactly as annoyed with / annoyed by divides. Against tired of, which can mean either weary or fed up, bored with is specifically about lack of interest, and against fed up with, which carries irritation, bored with carries only flatness.

Tired of something because it holds no interest.

  1. The kids got bored with the museum after twenty minutes.
  2. She's bored with explaining the same policy every August.
  3. I was bored by the first hour and asleep by the second.
  4. He gets bored with any project once the hard part is over.
  5. Nobody is bored with the paycheck, just with the work.
See also
tired of, fed up with, sick of, interested in, with

bound by#

Pronunciation
/baʊnd baɪ/ · BOWND by · weak /baʊnd bə/, and the d usually drops before a consonant: "BOWN-buh"
Русский
связанный чем-л. — правилом, обещанием, документом
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; ordinary in contracts, oaths, and journalism, and common in everyday speech about rules
Complement
a noun phrase naming the rule, promise, or document that constrains — never a gerund (✗ bound by keeping quiet) and never a to-infinitive. The other bound patterns take entirely different complements and are listed under Contrast.
Pattern
be bound by [contract / rule / oath] · feel bound by [noun] · legally / morally / contractually bound by · bound by law to [verb] — the two frames stacked
Variants
subject to covers the same ground more loosely; governed by is the neutral drafting word; tied to is the informal one; bound to + noun survives in bound to secrecy and bound to an apprenticeship.
Contrast
Three different bounds live behind this word, and only one of them is this entry. Bound by + noun means constrained by a rule (bound by the NDA). Bound to + bare verb means certain to happen and has nothing to do with obligation (it's bound to rain) — the to there is an infinitive marker, not a preposition. Bound for + place names a destination (bound for Denver), a use catalogued in participial prepositions. Keeping the three apart is worth the trouble, because ✗ bound by to rain and ✗ bound to the NDA are both wrong.

Legally or morally tied to a rule, a promise, or a document, and unable to disregard it.

  1. Everyone who signs is bound by the confidentiality clause for five years.
  2. The court is bound by precedent it may privately dislike.
  3. I'm bound by what I told them last week, whether or not it was smart.
  4. Employees are bound by the handbook only where it tracks the contract.
  5. She didn't feel bound by an agreement she'd never seen.
See also
binding on, binding upon, subject to, obligated to, obliged to, committed to, by

busy with#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɪzi wɪθ/ · BIZ-ee with · weak /ˈbɪzi wəθ/ — note the spelling trap: busy is pronounced with /ɪ/, not the /u/ the u suggests
Русский
занятый чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral and everyday
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (busy with getting the truck loaded) — never a to-infinitive after with: ✗ busy with to load the truck. The adjective's other frame takes a bare -ing with no preposition at all (busy loading the truck), which is the commoner construction in speech.
Pattern
be busy with [noun / verb-ing] · be busy [verb-ing] — no preposition · keep [someone] busy with [noun] · too busy for [noun] · too busy to [verb] — infinitive after too
Variants
occupied with and tied up with are the near equivalents; swamped with and buried in are the informal intensifiers; engaged in is the formal one.
Contrast
Busy with + noun, busy + -ing with nothing between, and too busy to + infinitive are three separate frames, and mixing them produces the standard errors: ✗ busy with cook dinner*, ✗ busy to cook dinner. Note also the difference between busy with the kids (they are what occupies you) and busy for the kids (which is not English) — the "on behalf of" idea needs a rewrite. ✗ busy in cooking is an Indian-English pattern that American readers hear as foreign, though it is centuries old.

Occupied by something that is taking up your time.

  1. She's busy with the year-end close until the fifteenth.
  2. He was busy with getting the storm windows up before the rain.
  3. The whole crew is busy with a rush order and nobody can take the call.
  4. Don't bother him — he's busy fixing the dishwasher.
  5. I'm too busy this week to take on anything else.
See also
involved in, engaged to, committed to, content with, with

capable of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkeɪpəbəl ʌv/ · KAY-puh-bull uv · weak /ˈkeɪpəbəl əv/
Русский
способный на что-л., умеющий — после of герундий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (capable of running the whole shift alone) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ capable to do it is one of the most common errors in this file; the correct forms are capable of doing it or able to do it, and the two adjectives are not interchangeable in structure.
Pattern
be capable of [noun / verb-ing] · more than capable of [verb-ing] · capable of [noun] (capable of great cruelty)
Variants
able to + infinitive is the everyday equivalent with the opposite structure. Up to + -ing is the informal one (up to running it alone).
Contrast
Capable of + -ing versus able to + verb is the pair to memorize, because they mean nearly the same thing and take opposite complements: ✓ capable of finishing, ✓ able to finish, ✗ capable to finish, ✗ able of finishing. Note also the flavor difference: capable of often implies latent capacity — including the capacity to do harm (capable of murder) — while able to reports plain ability in a situation. Incapable of is the negative and behaves identically.

Having the power, skill, or disposition to do something.

  1. She's capable of running the department without any help.
  2. The engine is capable of two hundred thousand miles with care.
  3. I didn't think he was capable of that kind of patience.
  4. Anyone is capable of making that mistake at four in the morning.
  5. The new scanner is capable of reading damaged plates.
See also
incapable of, expert at, skilled at, good at, of

careful with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɛrfəl wɪθ/ · KAIR-full with · weak /ˈkɛrfəl wəθ/
Русский
осторожный с — with с вещью, about в решении, of остерегаться, to не забыть сделать
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase after with and of; noun phrase or gerund after about (careful about choosing a contractor) — never a to-infinitive after any preposition, though the adjective takes a bare infinitive directly (careful to lock up), which is a separate frame and a very common one.
Pattern
be careful with [thing handled] · be careful about [matter / verb-ing] · be careful of [hazard] · be careful to [verb] · be careful (that) [clause] · Careful! (imperative alone)
Variants
cautious about, wary of, and particular about cover neighboring ground; watch out for is the informal equivalent of careful of.
Contrast
Four frames, four jobs. Careful with the glasses = handle the thing gently. Careful about what you promise = exercise judgment in a matter. Careful of the step = watch out for a hazard, slightly formal in American English, where watch out for or mind is commoner. Careful to sign both copies = don't forget to do it. The infinitive frame is the one that means "make sure you do"; ✗ careful of signing would not carry that meaning at all.

Taking pains — with a thing, about a decision, of a danger, or to do something right.

  1. Be careful with that box; the bottom is soft.
  2. She's careful about who she lends money to.
  3. Careful of the loose railing on the second landing.
  4. He was careful to copy the whole team on the reply.
  5. You have to be careful with dogs you don't know.
See also
suspicious of, sensitive to, serious about, with

certain of#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɜrtn ʌv/ · SUR-tun uv · weak /ˈsɜrtn əv/
Русский
уверенный в чём-л.; с инфинитивом — непременно случится
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; sure is the everyday word
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, gerund (certain of winning), or a wh-clause (certain about what she saw) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective (certain that he'd locked it), and a bare to-infinitive attaches directly with a different meaning (certain to happen = bound to happen).
Pattern
be certain of [noun / verb-ing] · be certain about [matter] · be certain (that) [clause] · be certain to [verb] · make certain of [noun] · for certain
Variants
sure of / sure about is the everyday twin and follows every one of these frames. Positive about is the emphatic conversational version.
Contrast
Certain of versus certain to is a genuine ambiguity trap: He's certain of promotion means he feels sure; He's certain to be promoted means it is bound to happen and says nothing about his feelings. Of leans toward a fact you are confident in (certain of the date); about leans toward a matter you have considered (certain about the decision), and about is the one that takes a wh-clause comfortably. ✗ certain for is an error.

Free of doubt — or, in the infinitive frame, guaranteed to happen.

  1. I'm certain of the date because it was my mother's birthday.
  2. She wasn't certain about signing a two-year lease.
  3. Make certain of the spelling before it goes to print.
  4. Rain is certain to ruin the Saturday game.
  5. Nobody was certain that the vote would even be held.
See also
sure of, confident of, confused about, aware of, of

characteristic of#

Pronunciation
/ˌkɛrəktəˈrɪstɪk ʌv/ · kair-uk-tuh-RISS-tik uv · weak /ˌkɛrəktəˈrɪstɪk əv/
Русский
характерный для, свойственный кому-л. или чему-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; common in academic and critical writing
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun, and a gerund where the thing described is an action (characteristic of dismissing critics out of hand is possible but heavy) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be characteristic of [person / group / period] · [noun] characteristic of [noun] · it is characteristic of [someone] to [verb]
Variants
typical of is the everyday equivalent and can be mildly disapproving; distinctive of stresses what sets something apart; peculiar to means found in that one place only.
Contrast
Characteristic of is neutral; typical of often is not. That's typical of him usually means he has disappointed you again, while That's characteristic of him is a description. Note the frame It's characteristic of her to volunteer first, where the infinitive belongs to the it-construction and not to the preposition. ✗ characteristic for and ✗ characteristic to are errors, though peculiar to and unique to correctly take to.

Belonging to the recognizable pattern of a person, group, style, or period.

  1. The long opening sentence is characteristic of her later essays.
  2. Fog like this is characteristic of the coast in June.
  3. Restraint was characteristic of the whole administration.
  4. That kind of joinery is characteristic of Shaker furniture.
  5. It's characteristic of him to send a note the next morning.
See also
typical of, similar to, known for, of

close to#

Pronunciation
/kloʊs tu/ · KLOHSS too · weak /kloʊs tə/ — note the adjective is /kloʊs/ with an s, not the verb /kloʊz/
Русский
близкий к — в пространстве, в отношениях, по количеству или во времени
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (close to giving up) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ close to give up is the error the noun test catches: close to the end ✓, so the to is a preposition.
Pattern
be close to [place / person / amount / verb-ing] · come close to [verb-ing] · close to [number] · nowhere close to [noun / verb-ing]
Variants
near to is possible but near alone is the American default before a place (near the airport). On the verge of + -ing is the formal equivalent of the "about to" sense.
Contrast
Three senses, one preposition. Physical (close to the station), emotional (close to her grandmother), and quantitative or imminent (close to sixty, close to quitting). The last of these competes with about to, which takes a bare infinitive: ✓ close to quitting, ✓ about to quit, ✗ close to quit. Note that close in the "nearly" sense also works adverbially with no to at all (close on a hundred people), and that the neighboring spatial preposition near is treated in its own right.

Near — in space, in relationship, in quantity, or in time.

  1. Their new place is close to the water treatment plant, unfortunately.
  2. She's very close to her older sister.
  3. Repairs came close to four thousand dollars.
  4. He was close to walking out in the middle of the meeting.
  5. We're close to having enough signatures.
See also
related to, similar to, ready for, near, to

coextensive with#

Pronunciation
/ˌkoʊɪkˈstɛnsɪv wɪθ/ · koh-ik-STEN-siv with · weak /ˌkoʊɪkˈstɛnsɪv wəθ/
Русский
совпадающий по объёму с — книжн./юр., покрывающий ровно ту же область
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal and technical — legal opinions, philosophy, and logic, where it is a precise word for "covering exactly the same ground"; never conversational
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other extent, scope, or set — never a gerund and never a clause. The two things compared must be of the same kind: a duty with a duty, a set with a set, a term with a term.
Pattern
be coextensive with [scope / term / set] · exactly / not quite / roughly coextensive with · coextensive in scope with [noun]
Variants
coterminous with is the near twin and is commoner for periods of time and for boundaries; congruent with and identical to are the plainer words; conterminous with is the geographer's spelling for shared borders.
Contrast
Coextensive with says two things cover the same range; coterminous with says they begin and end together. A duty coextensive with a risk is exactly as broad as the risk; a lease coterminous with the loan expires the day the loan does. Against equivalent to, which claims equal value, coextensive with* claims equal extent and nothing more. ✗ coextensive to is an error.

Covering exactly the same range or scope as something else, with no part of either falling outside the other.

  1. The insurer's duty to defend is not coextensive with its duty to indemnify.
  2. The statute's reach is coextensive with the commerce power, and no wider.
  3. The city's water district is roughly coextensive with the county line.
  4. The class of people harmed is not coextensive with the class who filed.
  5. He argued that the moral rule is coextensive with the legal one here.
See also
coterminous with, concurrent with, congruent to, congruent with, equivalent to, with

cognizant of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑɡnəzənt ʌv/ · KOG-nuh-zunt uv · weak /ˈkɑɡnəzənt əv/; the older KON-uh-zunt, with a silent g, still turns up in law
Русский
осведомлённый о — книжн./юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — memos, opinions, and official statements; in speech it sounds like a person reaching for a bigger word than aware
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (cognizant of being the only one there) — never a to-infinitive (✗ cognizant of to be there). A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective with no preposition: cognizant that the deadline had passed ✓, but ✗ cognizant of that the deadline had passed.
Pattern
be cognizant of [noun / verb-ing] · be cognizant (that) [clause] · fully / well / painfully cognizant of · take cognizance of [noun] — the noun frame, standard in law
Variants
aware of is the neutral everyday word; conscious of stresses the noticing; mindful of adds that you are letting the fact guide you; apprised of means somebody told you.
Contrast
Against aware of, which is the plain word, cognizant of is a register choice and nothing more — it means the same thing at a higher altitude, and American editors often cut it back. The legal noun phrase take cognizance of is different in kind: a court that takes cognizance of a matter assumes jurisdiction over it. ✗ cognizant about, ✗ cognizant to, and ✗ cognizant with are errors.

Aware of something, and aware of it in a way you are prepared to be held to.

  1. The committee was fully cognizant of the risk when it approved the loan.
  2. Officers must be cognizant of the policy before they use the device.
  3. She was cognizant of being the only person in the room who had read it.
  4. The opinion is cognizant of the burden this puts on small filers.
  5. We are cognizant of the deadline and are working through the weekend.
See also
aware of, conscious of, mindful of, apprised of, of

comfortable with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkʌmftərbəl wɪθ/ · KUMF-tur-bull with · weak /ˈkʌmftərbəl wəθ/; also four syllables /ˈkʌmfərtəbəl/
Русский
спокойно относящийся к чему-л., которого что-л. устраивает
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; heavily used in American workplace English
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (comfortable with saying no) — never a to-infinitive after with. The adjective separately takes a bare to-infinitive (comfortable to sit in, describing the chair, not the sitter), which is a different construction.
Pattern
be comfortable with [noun / verb-ing] · be comfortable (that) [clause] · feel comfortable with [noun] · comfortable in [a role or setting]
Variants
at ease with is the formal version; okay with and fine with are the conversational ones and take the same complements.
Contrast
Comfortable with is about the person's ease; comfortable to + infinitive is about the object's comfort. I'm comfortable with the chair means I accept it; The chair is comfortable to sit in means it feels good. Against used to, which reports habituation, comfortable with reports acceptance — you can be used to something without being comfortable with it, which is exactly why both phrases exist. ✗ comfortable of is an error.

At ease with a thing, an arrangement, or a course of action — willing to accept it.

  1. Are you comfortable with the numbers as they stand?
  2. She's not comfortable with speaking in front of the board.
  3. I'd be more comfortable with a written agreement.
  4. He's comfortable with silence in a way most people aren't.
  5. Nobody was comfortable with how fast the vote was scheduled.
See also
used to, accustomed to, content with, familiar with, with

committed to#

Pronunciation
/kəˈmɪtɪd tu/ · kuh-MIT-id too · weak /kəˈmɪtɪd tə/
Русский
приверженный чему-л., связавший себя обязательством — после to герундий
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral; institutional in tone when used of organizations
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (committed to reducing emissions) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ committed to reduce emissions is among the most frequent errors in corporate and academic English. The noun test settles it: committed to the reduction ✓, so to is a preposition.
Pattern
be committed to [noun / verb-ing] · commit [oneself] to [noun / verb-ing] · deeply / firmly / fully committed to [noun]
Variants
dedicated to and devoted to are the close synonyms, both with the same gerund requirement. Pledged to takes an infinitive instead (pledged to cut rates), which is why the two get confused.
Contrast
Committed to + -ing versus pledged to / vowed to + infinitive. Same meaning, opposite structures: ✓ committed to cutting rates, ✓ pledged to cut rates, ✗ committed to cut rates. Note the unrelated sense of the verb commit with no preposition (commit a crime) and the passive committed to meaning institutionalized (committed to a state hospital), which uses the same string for something else entirely.

Bound by conviction or promise to a cause, a course of action, or a relationship.

  1. The district is committed to keeping class sizes under twenty-five.
  2. She's committed to the neighborhood in a way newcomers rarely are.
  3. We are fully committed to finishing before the frost.
  4. He wasn't ready to be committed to anyone.
  5. Both parties say they're committed to reopening the talks.
See also
devoted to, dedicated to, opposed to, intent on, to

common to#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑmən tu/ · KOM-un too · weak /ˈkɑmən tə/
Русский
общий для — присущий каждому из названных
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; ordinary in scientific and analytical writing (a feature common to both groups)
Complement
a noun phrase naming two or more things, or a plural or collective noun — common to both drafts, common to mammals, common to the whole family. A gerund is impossible here (✗ common to running), and so is a clause. The complement must be plural or collective in sense, because the word means "shared by."
Pattern
[feature] common to [group] · common to both / all / every [noun] · have [something] in common with [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to with
Variants
shared by is the plain equivalent; universal to and characteristic of cover neighboring ideas; in common with is the phrase you need when the subject is one of the sharers.
Contrast
Common to takes the sharers; in common with takes the other party. A trait common to* both species is shared by them; in common with most birds, it molts in fall puts the subject alongside the group. Against characteristic of, which says a trait is typical of one thing, common to says a trait is present in several. Watch the everyday sense of common meaning "frequent," which takes no preposition (a common mistake) and, in the phrase common in, takes a place or population: the fungus is common in the Southeast — frequent there, not shared by it.

Shared by two or more things, so that each of them has it.

  1. A dislike of paperwork is common to every trade I've worked in.
  2. The bug is common to both the desktop and the phone builds.
  3. Sickle cell trait is common to populations where malaria was endemic.
