  4. What's common to all four accounts is the missing half hour.
  5. Roof access is common to the units and maintained by the association.
See also
characteristic of, peculiar to, typical of, specific to, to

compatible with#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈpætəbəl wɪθ/ · kum-PAT-uh-bull with · weak /kəmˈpætəbəl wəθ/
Русский
совместимый с
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; technical in computing, formal in argument
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun, and a gerund where an activity is meant (compatible with running two shifts) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be compatible with [noun / verb-ing] · [noun] compatible with [noun] · not compatible with [noun] · backward compatible with [noun]
Variants
consistent with is the argument-and-evidence version; suited to the person-and-role version. Incompatible with is the direct negative.
Contrast
Compatible with asks whether two things can coexist; consistent with asks whether they contradict each other. A finding is consistent with a theory (it does not disprove it); a printer is compatible with a laptop (they work together). ✗ compatible to is an error, which is worth flagging because similar to, identical to, and equal to all take to and pull compatible toward it. See consistent with.

Able to exist or work together without conflict.

  1. The new cable isn't compatible with anything we already own.
  2. That schedule isn't compatible with having a family.
  3. Their goals turned out to be compatible with ours after all.
  4. The format is compatible with every reader we tested.
  5. His account is not compatible with the timeline in the report.
See also
consistent with, suitable for, similar to, with

concerned about#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsɜrnd əˈbaʊt/ · kun-SURND uh-BOWT · weak /kənˈsɜrnd əˈbaʊt/, casually /kənˈsɜrnd ˈbaʊt/
Русский
обеспокоенный чем-л.; concerned with — занимающийся чем-л., касающийся чего-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about, with, and for (concerned about losing the contract) — never a to-infinitive after any of them. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: concerned that the numbers were revised twice.
Pattern
be concerned about [noun / verb-ing] · be concerned with [subject matter] · be concerned for [someone's welfare] · be concerned (that) [clause] · as far as [X] is concerned
Variants
worried about is the everyday equivalent of the about sense only. Deal with and be about replace the with sense in plainer prose.
Contrast
This is one of the sharpest three-way splits in the file. Concerned about = worried. Concerned with = having to do with, dealing with — no worry at all: Chapter 4 is concerned with tax policy means it is about tax policy. Concerned for = anxious on someone's behalf: concerned for her safety. Substituting one for another changes the sentence completely: a report concerned with air quality studies it, while a report concerned about air quality is alarmed by it. The frozen as far as I'm concerned uses yet another sense — "in my judgment."

Worried about something, dealing with something, or anxious on someone's behalf.

  1. Residents are concerned about the noise from the new terminal.
  2. The second half of the book is concerned with labor law.
  3. She was concerned for her father, alone in the house all winter.
  4. I'm concerned about signing anything before the audit closes.
  5. As far as the bank is concerned, the loan is already closed.
See also
worried about, anxious about, involved in, about

concomitant with#

Pronunciation
/kənˈkɑmɪtənt wɪθ/ · kun-KOM-uh-tunt with · weak /kənˈkɑmətənt wəθ/
Русский
сопутствующий чему-л. — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal and technical — clinical writing above all (concomitant medications on any American intake form), plus economics and academic prose
Complement
a noun phrase naming what accompanies — never a gerund in normal use and never a clause. The word is also common attributively with no complement at all: concomitant therapy, the concomitant rise in costs.
Pattern
[thing] concomitant with [thing] · concomitant [noun] — attributive · with the concomitant [noun] — the fixed prepositional phrase · concomitantly with — the adverb
Variants
accompanying and attendant on are the near synonyms; coincident with stresses the timing; along with is the plain preposition; simultaneous with is the neutral technical word.
Contrast
Concomitant with claims that two things go together, not that one causes the other — which is exactly why medical and economic writing likes it: a concomitant rise in costs accompanies the change without being blamed on it. Against concurrent with, which is strictly about running at the same time, concomitant with* implies the two belong to one picture. In clinical use the attributive form has nearly swallowed the prepositional one: a chart lists concomitant medications, not drugs concomitant with the trial drug. ✗ concomitant to is occasionally seen and is not the standard choice.

Occurring alongside something else, as part of the same situation.

  1. Fatigue is concomitant with nearly every drug in this class.
  2. The layoffs were concomitant with a slump nobody had forecast.
  3. Rising rents, concomitant with flat wages, pushed the vacancy rate down.
  4. List any medication concomitant with the study drug on page two.
  5. The risks concomitant with early release were argued at length.
See also
concurrent with, contemporaneous with, coextensive with, incidental to, with

concurrent with#

Pronunciation
/kənˈkɜrənt wɪθ/ · kun-KUR-unt with · weak /kənˈkɜrənt wəθ/
Русский
одновременный с, идущий параллельно по времени
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — sentencing orders, project schedules, and academic calendars; the adverb concurrently is the commoner form in speech
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other event, term, or period — never a gerund (✗ concurrent with filing, where careful writing puts a noun: concurrent with the filing) and never a clause.
Pattern
[term / event] concurrent with [term / event] · run concurrent with [noun] · concurrently with — the adverb, which is what most writers actually use · concurrent jurisdiction / a concurring opinion — attributive relatives
Variants
simultaneous with is the neutral equivalent; contemporaneous with is the historian's and the lawyer's word for documents made at the same time; coincident with stresses that the overlap may be accidental; at the same time as is the plain phrase.
Contrast
Concurrent with means running at the same time; contemporaneous with means made or existing at the same time. Sentences run concurrent with one another; a memo is contemporaneous with the meeting it records. Against coterminous with, which requires the same beginning and end, concurrent with* only requires overlap. In American sentencing the pair to know is concurrent versus consecutive: concurrent sentences are served together, consecutive ones back to back. ✗ concurrent to is an error.

Running at the same time as something else.

  1. The two sentences run concurrent with each other, so he serves eight years.
  2. Demolition is concurrent with the utility work, which is why the street is closed.
  3. Her fellowship was concurrent with the last year of the grant.
  4. State and federal courts have jurisdiction concurrent with one another here.
  5. The audit was concurrent with the transition to the new system.
See also
contemporaneous with, coterminous with, concomitant with, coextensive with, with

conditional on, conditional upon#

Pronunciation
/kənˈdɪʃənəl ɑn, kənˈdɪʃənəl əˈpɑn/ · kun-DISH-un-ul on, kun-DISH-un-ul uh-PON · weak /kənˈdɪʃənəl ən/
Русский
обусловленный чем-л. — действует, только если
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; ordinary in offers, admissions letters, statistics, and contracts, with upon the drafter's variant
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (conditional on passing the physical) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ conditional on pass the physical, ✗ conditional on to pass. A that-clause is possible only through a dummy noun: conditional on the understanding that you resign.
Pattern
be conditional on [noun / verb-ing] · conditional upon [noun] — same phrase, formal spelling · make [something] conditional on [noun] · a conditional offer — attributive · conditional on [noun] in statistics: the probability conditional on X
Variants
contingent on and contingent upon are the exact equivalents and are commoner in American contracts; dependent on covers the same ground in plain English; subject to is the loosest of the family.
Contrast
Conditional on and contingent on, contingent upon are interchangeable in ordinary use; where they part is flavor — conditional points at a stated condition somebody wrote down, contingent at an uncertain future event. In statistics conditional on is a term of art with no substitute: the distribution of Y conditional on X is the distribution once X is known. Against dependent on, which can describe an ongoing reliance, conditional on describes a gate that has to be passed. ✗ conditional to is an error.

Holding only if something else happens or turns out to be true.

  1. The offer is conditional on a clean background check.
  2. Admission is conditional upon finishing the two prerequisites this summer.
  3. Funding for the second year is conditional on hitting the enrollment target.
  4. Everything in the estimate is conditional on the permit coming through.
  5. The forecast is conditional on the reservoir staying above sixty percent.
See also
contingent on, contingent upon, dependent on, subject to, reliant on, on

conducive to#

Pronunciation
/kənˈdusɪv tu/ · kun-DOO-siv too · weak /kənˈdusəv tə/; the British kun-DYOO-siv is not used in America
Русский
способствующий чему-л. — создающий подходящие условия
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; common in writing about conditions and environments (conducive to learning, conducive to sleep)
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund — conducive to learning, conducive to getting anything done — and never a to-infinitive: ✗ conducive to learn, ✗ conducive to get anything done. The noun test settles it instantly: conducive to sleep ✓, so the to is a preposition. Because so many of its complements are -ing nouns already, the error hides easily.
Pattern
be conducive to [noun / verb-ing] · hardly / not exactly / far from conducive to · conditions conducive to [noun] · conduce to [noun] — the rare formal verb
Variants
favorable to is the neutral equivalent; good for is the plain one; helpful to and supportive of cover parts of the range; propitious for is the literary version.
Contrast
Conducive to names conditions that make something more likely, not something that causes it — a quiet room is conducive to studying, it does not produce studying. The subject is nearly always a setting, a policy, or a state of affairs rather than a person: ✗ she is conducive to the project* is not English. Against amenable to, which is about willingness, conducive to is about circumstances. ✗ conducive for is a frequent error in American student writing, and ✗ conducive with is worse.

Tending to bring something about, by making the conditions right for it.

  1. Fluorescent lighting and a broken chair are not conducive to concentration.
  2. The new schedule is more conducive to getting the trucks out on time.
  3. Warm, wet weather is conducive to mold in these crawlspaces.
  4. Nothing about that meeting was conducive to an honest answer.
  5. A short walk after dinner is conducive to sleeping through the night.
See also
amenable to, detrimental to, harmful to, useful for, to

confident of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑnfɪdənt ʌv/ · KON-fih-dunt uv · weak /ˈkɑnfɪdənt əv/
Русский
уверенный в исходе; about в вопросе, in в чьих-л. способностях
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after of and about (confident of winning, confident about starting over) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective (confident that the funding will come through), and confident in takes the thing or person trusted, not the outcome.
Pattern
be confident of [outcome / verb-ing] · be confident about [matter] · be confident in [person / method / oneself] · be confident (that) [clause]
Variants
sure of and certain of cover the same ground more plainly; optimistic about is the hedged version.
Contrast
Confident of the result is about the outcome; confident in the team is about the thing you trust. You can be confident in a surgeon without being confident of a cure. About is the loose everyday option and takes a general matter (confident about the move). ✗ confident on is an error, and ✗ confident to do it should be confident enough to do it or confident about doing it.

Feeling sure — of an outcome, about a matter, or in someone's ability.

  1. The coach is confident of a win on Saturday.
  2. She's confident about going back to school at forty.
  3. I'm confident in the crew; I'm less confident in the forecast.
  4. He's confident of finishing the draft before the holidays.
  5. Investors were confident that rates had peaked.
See also
certain of, sure of, proud of, of

confused about#

Pronunciation
/kənˈfjuzd əˈbaʊt/ · kun-FYOOZD uh-BOWT · weak /kənˈfjuzd əˈbaʊt/
Русский
не понимающий чего-л., запутавшийся в
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (confused about being billed twice), or a wh-clause (confused about what to do next) after about; noun phrase or gerund after by — never a to-infinitive after either.
Pattern
be confused about [matter / wh-clause] · be confused by [the thing causing confusion] · get confused about [noun] · confuse [X] with [Y]
Variants
unclear about and fuzzy on are the neighbors; puzzled by and baffled by are the intensified versions and prefer by.
Contrast
About names the topic of your confusion; by names its cause — confused about the schedule* (I don't understand it) versus confused by the schedule (it confuses me). Keep both apart from confuse X with Y, the verb pattern meaning to mistake one for the other: I always confuse him with his brother, where ✗ confuse him to his brother is wrong. ✗ confused of and ✗ confused on are errors, though fuzzy on the details is idiomatic.

Unable to understand something, or mixed up about it.

  1. Half the class was confused about which chapters were on the exam.
  2. I'm confused about what the second charge is for.
  3. Voters were confused by the new ballot layout.
  4. She got confused about the time zones and called at four a.m.
  5. Don't confuse the deadline with the postmark date.
See also
aware of, certain of, familiar with, about

congruent to, congruent with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑŋɡruənt tu, ˈkɑŋɡruənt wɪθ/ · KONG-groo-unt too, KONG-groo-unt with · weak /ˈkɑŋɡruənt tə/ and /ˈkɑŋɡruənt wəθ/; the stress-shifted kun-GROO-unt is also heard
Русский
конгруэнтный (мат.); согласующийся с чем-л. (перен.)
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical in the to frame (geometry, number theory), formal-neutral in the with frame (psychology, management, and ordinary argument)
Complement
a noun phrase — a figure, a number, a set of values, a description. A gerund is possible but rare in the with frame (congruent with saying one thing and doing another), and a to-infinitive is impossible in either.
Pattern
[figure] is congruent to [figure] — geometry · a ≡ b congruent to [modulus] — number theory, read "congruent to b modulo n" · be congruent with [values / account] · not entirely congruent with [noun]
Variants
congruent with is the everyday choice outside mathematics and is what most American writers use for beliefs, values, and accounts. Consistent with is the plain equivalent; consonant with the formal one; in keeping with the idiomatic one.
Contrast
The preposition tracks the field. Geometry says triangle ABC is congruent to triangle DEF, and a math teacher will mark congruent with wrong there; outside mathematics, congruent with is standard and congruent to sounds like a misapplied classroom habit. Against equivalent to, which claims equal value, congruence claims that two things match point for point. Against consonant with, which is nearly a synonym in the figurative use, congruent with is the commoner word in American management and therapy talk.

Matching another thing exactly — in shape and size, in the mathematical use; in substance, in the figurative one.

  1. The two triangles are congruent to each other by side-angle-side.
  2. Seventeen is congruent to two modulo five.
  3. Her testimony is not congruent with what she wrote in the log.
  4. We hire people whose habits are congruent with how the shop actually runs.
  5. The budget has to be congruent with the plan the board approved in March.
See also
equivalent to, consonant with, isomorphic to, compatible with, with

conscious of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑnʃəs ʌv/ · KON-shuss uv · weak /ˈkɑnʃəs əv/
Русский
осознающий, замечающий что-л. и держащий это в уме
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (conscious of being stared at) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective (conscious that the room had gone quiet), and ✗ conscious of that is an error.
Pattern
be conscious of [noun / verb-ing] · be conscious (that) [clause] · become conscious of [noun] · acutely conscious of [noun] · [noun]-conscious as a suffix: health-conscious, cost-conscious
Variants
aware of is the everyday twin and follows the same frames. Mindful of adds a note of deliberate attention.
Contrast
Conscious of leans toward felt, immediate awareness; aware of leans toward knowledge. You are conscious of a draft on your neck and aware of a change in the law; either word will pass in most sentences, but the drift is real. The suffixed compounds are a separate and very productive pattern — safety-conscious, brand-conscious — where no preposition appears at all. ✗ conscious about is an error in this sense, though self-conscious about is correct and means embarrassed.

Noticing something, or keeping it in mind.

  1. He was conscious of every squeak the floor made.
  2. She's conscious of how much the delay is costing them.
  3. I became conscious of someone watching from the porch.
  4. The design is conscious of the building's original proportions.
  5. She's self-conscious about her accent on the phone.
See also
aware of, sensitive to, careful with, of

consonant with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑnsənənt wɪθ/ · KON-suh-nunt with · weak /ˈkɑnsənənt wəθ/
Русский
согласующийся с принципом, ценностями — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal and somewhat literary — judicial opinions, policy writing, and criticism; in speech an American says in line with or consistent with
Complement
a noun phrase, or a gerund in careful writing (consonant with treating everyone alike) — never a to-infinitive (✗ consonant with treat everyone alike) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
be consonant with [principle / practice] · wholly / entirely / not consonant with · consonance with — the noun frame, also with with
Variants
consistent with is the neutral American equivalent and is what most editors will substitute; in keeping with is the idiomatic one; congruent with and in harmony with cover the same ground; compatible with is the everyday word.
Contrast
The word carries its musical metaphor: things consonant with each other sound well together, so it fits principles, traditions, and values better than facts. For a factual match, compatible with or consistent with is the better word — the injuries are consistent with a fall, not ✗ consonant with a fall, which a doctor would never write. Do not confuse the adjective with the noun consonant (the speech sound), which takes no preposition. ✗ consonant to is an error.

In agreement with a principle, a practice, or a body of values.

  1. The ruling is consonant with forty years of the court's own precedent.
  2. Their treatment of the tenants was hardly consonant with the mission statement.
  3. Nothing in the proposal is consonant with what the charter says about notice.
  4. The restoration is consonant with the building's original 1911 plans.
  5. He wanted a career consonant with the way he'd been raised.
See also
compatible with, congruent to, congruent with, germane to, repugnant to, with

contemporaneous with#

Pronunciation
/kənˌtɛmpəˈreɪniəs wɪθ/ · kun-tem-puh-RAY-nee-us with · weak /kənˌtɛmpəˈreɪniəs wəθ/
Русский
одновременный с, относящийся к тому же времени — книжн./юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal and technical — evidence law (contemporaneous notes), history, and geology; the everyday word is at the same time as
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other event, document, or period — never a gerund (careful writing nominalizes: contemporaneous with the signing, not ✗ contemporaneous with signing) and never a clause.
Pattern
[document / event] contemporaneous with [event] · roughly / nearly / exactly contemporaneous with · a contemporaneous record — attributive · contemporaneously with — the adverb
Variants
concurrent with is the word for things running at the same time; simultaneous with is the neutral technical choice; coeval with is the literary and geological one; of the same period as is the plain paraphrase.
Contrast
Contemporaneous is not contemporary. Contemporary* means either "of the present day" (contemporary art) or "of the same era as," and takes with only in the second sense; contemporaneous with always means "made or occurring at the same time as" and is the word American lawyers use for notes written while events happened — the whole evidentiary point being that they were not written later. Against concurrent with, which is about duration overlapping, contemporaneous with is about the moment of origin. ✗ contemporaneous to is an error.

Made or happening at the same time as something else, and valuable precisely for that.

  1. Her notes are contemporaneous with the call and were never edited.
  2. The eruption was contemporaneous with the collapse of the settlement.
  3. The email is contemporaneous with the meeting, so it carries weight.
  4. Those deposits are roughly contemporaneous with the lower layer at the dig.
  5. A memo written a year later is not contemporaneous with anything.
See also
concurrent with, concomitant with, coterminous with, antecedent to, with

content with#

Pronunciation
/kənˈtɛnt wɪθ/ · kun-TENT with · weak /kənˈtɛnt wəθ/ — the adjective stresses the second syllable, unlike the noun content /ˈkɑntɛnt/
Русский
довольный тем, что есть, не требующий большего
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to slightly literary
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after with (content with staying home) — never a to-infinitive after with. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive directly (content to wait), which is the commoner frame of the two.
Pattern
be content with [noun / verb-ing] · be content to [verb] · content oneself with [noun / verb-ing] · perfectly / quite content with [noun]
Variants
satisfied with is the plainer synonym; happy with is the everyday one. Contented with is the same word in its fuller adjectival form and sounds more settled.
Contrast
Content with + -ing and content to + verb both exist and mean nearly the same thing — content with waiting and content to wait — which makes this one of the few adjectives here where the infinitive is not an error. The difference is slight: with frames the situation as something accepted, to frames it as something willingly done. Content oneself with is the reflexive frame meaning to settle for less than you wanted. ✗ content of is an error.

Satisfied with what there is, and not pushing for more.

  1. She's content with a small garden and no lawn at all.
  2. He was content to let his brother do the talking.
  3. They had to content themselves with second place.
  4. I'm perfectly content with the arrangement as written.
  5. Nobody here is content with running the same numbers twice.
See also
satisfied with, happy about, pleased with, with

contingent on, contingent upon#

Pronunciation
/kənˈtɪndʒənt ɑn, kənˈtɪndʒənt əˈpɑn/ · kun-TIN-junt on, kun-TIN-junt uh-PON · weak /kənˈtɪndʒənt ən/
Русский
зависящий от — состоится, только если случится нечто неопределённое
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; the standard word in American real-estate contracts (contingent on financing), employment offers, and grant agreements, with upon the drafter's variant
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund — contingent on selling the house ✓ — and never a to-infinitive: ✗ contingent on sell the house, ✗ contingent to sell the house. A clause needs a dummy noun: contingent on the condition that the appraisal clears.
Pattern
be contingent on [noun / verb-ing] · contingent upon [noun] · make [something] contingent on [noun] · a contingency / contingent fee / contingent liability — attributive relatives, no preposition
Variants
conditional on and conditional upon are the exact equivalents; dependent on is the plain-English version; subject to is looser and covers a wider range; hinges on is the informal verb.
Contrast
Contingent on points at an uncertain future event; conditional on points at a stated condition. In practice American contracts use them interchangeably, and contingent on* dominates in real estate: an offer contingent on inspection dies if the inspection fails. Against dependent on, which describes standing reliance (dependent on the grant for salary), contingent on describes a single gate. Note the noun contingency, which takes no preposition in its ordinary use, and the fee arrangement on contingency, where no of or to is possible.

Happening only if some uncertain thing happens first.

  1. The offer is contingent on financing and on a satisfactory inspection.
  2. Her bonus is contingent upon the division clearing its target.
  3. Everything after phase two is contingent on the grant being renewed.
  4. The permit is contingent on the traffic study, which nobody has scheduled.
  5. Whether we drive is contingent on what the pass looks like Saturday.
See also
conditional on, conditional upon, dependent on, subject to, reliant on, on

conversant with#

Pronunciation
/kənˈvɜrsənt wɪθ/ · kun-VUR-sunt with · weak /kənˈvɜrsənt wəθ/; the older KON-vur-sunt is chiefly British
Русский
хорошо разбирающийся в — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — résumés, expert declarations, and job descriptions; a spoken American says knows his way around
Complement
a noun phrase naming a subject, a body of rules, or a tool — never a to-infinitive (✗ conversant with to use the software). A gerund is grammatical but stiff (conversant with running a mill); careful writers use a noun instead.
Pattern
be conversant with [subject / system] · be conversant in [language / field] · thoroughly / fully / not especially conversant with · make oneself conversant with [noun]
Variants
conversant in is standard for languages and disciplines — conversant in Spanish, conversant in tax law — while conversant with suits documents, systems, and practices. Familiar with is the neutral everyday equivalent; versed in and well versed in are the formal ones; fluent in belongs to languages only.
Contrast
With for a thing you have worked with, in for a field you have studied. Both are correct English, and the choice is idiomatic rather than logical: ✓ conversant with the filing system*, ✓ conversant in French. Against acquainted with, which reports an introduction, conversant with claims working knowledge — enough to hold up your end of a conversation, which is where the word comes from. Note that conversant has nothing to do with the verb converse in modern use. ✗ conversant about and ✗ conversant to are errors.

Knowing a subject or a system well enough to work in it without help.

  1. Applicants must be conversant with federal procurement rules.
  2. She's conversant with every payroll system this company has ever run.
  3. The witness was not conversant with the actual conditions on the floor.
  4. He's conversant in Portuguese and can handle the Lisbon calls.
  5. Get conversant with the new codes before the inspection.
See also
familiar with, acquainted with, expert at, skilled at, with

coterminous with#

Pronunciation
/koʊˈtɜrmɪnəs wɪθ/ · koh-TUR-muh-nus with · weak /koʊˈtɜrmənəs wəθ/
Русский
совпадающий по границам или по срокам с — книжн./юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — leases and loan documents (a term coterminous with the note), municipal law, and political geography; never conversational
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other term, boundary, or period — never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
[term / district] be coterminous with [term / district] · make the lease coterminous with [noun] · exactly / not quite coterminous with
Variants
conterminous with is the same word in the spelling geographers prefer for shared borders — and the conterminous United States is the fixed term for the lower forty-eight. Coextensive with is the near twin for scope rather than time; concurrent with for events running together.
Contrast
Coterminous with means beginning and ending together, or sharing a boundary; coextensive with means covering the same ground. A sublease coterminous with the master lease expires on the same day; a duty coextensive with a risk is the same size as the risk. Against concurrent with, which needs only overlap, coterminous with* demands the same endpoints. ✗ coterminous to is an error.

Ending at the same point as something else — in time, or along a shared border.

  1. We made the sublease coterminous with the master lease so both end in June.
  2. The school district is coterminous with the township, which simplifies the tax roll.
  3. His term as trustee is coterminous with the mayor's.
  4. The park's north edge is coterminous with the county line.
  5. The warranty is coterminous with the financing, not with the roof's life.
See also
coextensive with, concurrent with, contemporaneous with, equivalent to, with

crazy about#

Pronunciation
/ˈkreɪzi əˈbaʊt/ · KRAY-zee uh-BOWT · weak /ˈkreɪzi əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈkreɪzi ˈbaʊt/
Русский
без ума от — разг.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
informal; conversational American English
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (crazy about camping) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ crazy about to camp is not English.
Pattern
be crazy about [noun / verb-ing] · not crazy about [noun / verb-ing] · go crazy over [noun] · drive [someone] crazy (no preposition on the object)
Variants
wild about and nuts about are the same register; mad about is the British equivalent and is not the American default. Crazy for survives mostly in song titles and in crazy for you.
Contrast
The negative is the commonest use and it is understatement, not neutrality: I'm not crazy about the color means you dislike it. Keep crazy about (fond of) apart from crazy with (overwhelmed by an emotion — crazy with worry) and from drive someone crazy, which takes a direct object and no preposition at all. In American English mad about means angry about, so mad about her and crazy about her are not synonyms here even though they are in London.

Enthusiastic about something or someone, to a degree the speaker admits is unreasonable.

  1. He's been crazy about trains since he was four.
  2. I'm not crazy about driving that stretch after dark.
  3. She's crazy about the new place, cracked driveway and all.
  4. The kids are crazy about their swim coach.
  5. Everyone's crazy about that bakery on Third.
See also
mad at, keen on, fond of, obsessed with, about

critical of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkrɪtɪkəl ʌv/ · KRIT-ih-kull uv · weak /ˈkrɪtɪkəl əv/
Русский
критикующий что-л., недовольный чем-л.; critical to/for — жизненно важный для
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (critical of spending that much) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be critical of [noun / verb-ing] · highly / sharply / openly critical of [noun] · critical to [something] — a different sense entirely · critical for [purpose]
Variants
disapproving of and disparaging about are the near-synonyms; hard on is the informal one.
Contrast
Critical of means finding fault; critical to and critical for mean essential. This is one word doing two unrelated jobs, and the preposition is the only signal: critical of the plan* opposes it, critical to the plan is indispensable to it. A third sense, in critical condition, takes no preposition at all. ✗ critical about is an error for the fault-finding sense; use of.

Finding fault with something — or, with to and for, indispensable to it.

  1. The auditor was critical of how the invoices were logged.
  2. She's critical of everything except her own department.
  3. Several members were openly critical of holding the vote in August.
  4. Timing is critical to the whole operation.
  5. Reviewers were critical of the ending but praised everything before it.
See also
suspicious of, essential to, impressed with, of

crowded with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkraʊdɪd wɪθ/ · KROW-did with · weak /ˈkraʊdɪd wəθ/
Русский
переполненный чем-л., набитый
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, usually plural or mass (crowded with tourists, crowded with furniture) — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
be crowded with [plural / mass noun] · [place] crowded with [noun] · crowd into [place] — the verb, with a different preposition
Variants
packed with, jammed with, thick with, and filled with all take with in the same frame; full of takes of and is the commonest of the family.
Contrast
Crowded with takes with; full of takes of. They mean nearly the same thing and their prepositions do not swap: ✗ crowded of people*, ✗ full with people. Note that the verb goes elsewhere again — people crowded into the lobby, crowd around the stage — and that crowded alone, with no preposition, is the ordinary way to say a place has too many people in it.

Containing more people or things than there is comfortable room for.

  1. The platform was crowded with commuters by six-fifteen.
  2. Her desk is crowded with photographs and unopened mail.
  3. The calendar is crowded with meetings that could have been emails.
  4. Every bar downtown was crowded with people watching the game.
  5. The little museum is crowded with objects nobody has cataloged.
See also
full of, rich in, short of, with

cruel to#

Pronunciation
/ˈkruəl tu/ · KROO-ull too · weak /ˈkruəl tə/
Русский
жестокий к кому-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming the person or animal treated badly — never a gerund after to and never a clause. The adjective also takes the it-frame with an infinitive: It was cruel to say that, where the infinitive belongs to it, not to cruel.
Pattern
be cruel to [person / animal] · it is cruel to [verb] · it was cruel of [someone] to [verb] · cruel to be kind
Variants
mean to, harsh with, hard on, and unkind to cover the range from playground to courtroom; all the "behavior toward a person" adjectives in this family take to.
Contrast
Every adjective of how you treat someone takes to: cruel to, kind to, nice to, polite to, rude to, mean to, good to. The pattern is reliable enough to guess with. What breaks it is gentle with, patient with, and honest with, which take with because they describe the manner of the handling rather than the direction of the treatment. The double frame It was cruel of him to say that uses of for the doer and to for the deed — ✗ It was cruel from him is an error.

Deliberately causing pain or suffering to someone.

  1. He was cruel to the dog when he thought nobody was watching.
  2. Middle school is cruel to anyone who stands out.
  3. It was cruel of her to bring it up at the table.
  4. The schedule is cruel to anyone with a long commute.
  5. Time has been cruel to that building.
See also
kind to, rude to, nice to, gentle with, to

curious about#

Pronunciation
/ˈkjʊriəs əˈbaʊt/ · KYOOR-ee-uss uh-BOWT · weak /ˈkjʊriəs əˈbaʊt/
Русский
любопытствующий, желающий узнать о
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (curious about trying it), or a wh-clause (curious about how they did it) after about — never a to-infinitive after about. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (curious to see what happens), which is a separate and very common frame.
Pattern
be curious about [noun / verb-ing / wh-clause] · be curious to [verb] · curious as to [wh-clause] · make [someone] curious about [noun]
Variants
interested in is the neutral equivalent; nosy about is the disapproving one. Curious as to whether is the formal register.
Contrast
Curious about names the topic; curious to names what you want to do about it. Curious about the results is wondering; curious to see the results is wanting to look. This adjective is one of the small set — with anxious, eager, interested, and keen — where the bare infinitive is standard alongside the preposition. ✗ curious of and ✗ curious for are errors.

Wanting to know about something.

  1. She's curious about how the machine actually works.
  2. I was curious about the cost before I mentioned it to anyone.
  3. Neighbors were curious about the trucks parked all week.
  4. He was curious to hear what she'd say next.
  5. Nobody seemed curious about where the money had gone.
See also
interested in, eager for, enthusiastic about, about

dedicated to#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɛdɪkeɪtɪd tu/ · DED-ih-kay-tid too · weak /ˈdɛdɪkeɪtɪd tə/
Русский
посвятивший себя чему-л.; о вещи — предназначенный только для этого
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral; warm about a person, institutional about an organization
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (dedicated to keeping the archive open) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ dedicated to keep the archive open is the error, and the noun test settles it: dedicated to the archive ✓, so the to is a preposition. A that-clause is impossible.
Pattern
be dedicated to [cause / person / verb-ing] · dedicate [a book / a bench / one's life] to [someone] · [portion] dedicated to [noun] · a dedicated [noun] — attributive, meaning reserved for one use: a dedicated line
Variants
devoted to and committed to are the near-twins and take the same gerund. Dedicated to sits between them — warmer than committed, more disciplined than devoted. Pledged to and vowed to mean the same thing and take an infinitive instead.
Contrast
Dedicated to + -ing versus pledged to / vowed to + verb. Same meaning, opposite structures: ✓ dedicated to reducing waste, ✓ pledged to reduce waste, ✗ dedicated to reduce waste — an error that turns up constantly in mission statements. Two other senses share the string and are not this pattern: the commemorative one (a bench dedicated to her mother) and the attributive one meaning single-purpose (a dedicated server), which takes no preposition at all. ✗ dedicated for and ✗ dedicated with are errors.

Giving one's whole effort to something — or, of a thing, set aside for a single purpose.

  1. The foundation is dedicated to keeping small-town newspapers alive.
  2. She's dedicated to her patients in a way the schedule never rewards.
  3. Two rooms upstairs are dedicated to files nobody has opened since 2009.
  4. He dedicated the book to the teacher who failed him twice.
  5. The office is dedicated to answering every complaint within a week.
See also
devoted to, committed to, opposed to, accustomed to, to

delighted with#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈlaɪtɪd wɪθ/ · dih-LY-tid with · weak /dɪˈlaɪtɪd wəθ/
Русский
очень довольный, в восторге от
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral, warm; commoner in writing than in casual American speech
Complement
noun phrase after with, by, and at; a gerund is possible after at (delighted at finding it still open) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (delighted to help), which is by far its commonest frame, and a that-clause (delighted that you could come).
Pattern
be delighted with [thing received or achieved] · be delighted by [the cause] · be delighted at [news / turn of events] · be delighted to [verb] · be delighted (that) [clause]
Variants
pleased with is the same shape at lower temperature; thrilled with / by is the same shape at higher.
Contrast
With takes the thing you got; by takes the thing that acted on you; at takes the news or the turn of events. Delighted with the results* is your verdict on them; delighted by her reply points at what caused the delight; delighted at the news reports a reaction to information. The infinitive frame is the polite formula of American correspondence — I'd be delighted to — and it never takes a preposition. ✗ delighted for is an error except in delighted for you, meaning happy on your behalf.

Very pleased — a step warmer than pleased, a step cooler than thrilled.

  1. The client was delighted with the first round of drawings.
  2. She was delighted by how many people showed up.
  3. He was delighted at the news that the school had reopened.
  4. We'd be delighted to have you both for dinner.
  5. Everyone was delighted with the way the porch turned out.
See also
pleased with, happy about, satisfied with, impressed with, with

dependent on#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈpɛndənt ɑn/ · dih-PEN-dunt on · weak /dɪˈpɛndənt ən/
Русский
зависящий от — нуждающийся в чём-л. или определяемый чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (dependent on getting the permit) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. A wh-clause is possible in the "determined by" sense: dependent on how the vote goes.
Pattern
be dependent on [noun / verb-ing] · heavily / entirely / largely dependent on [noun] · dependent on [someone] for [something] · make [X] dependent on [Y]
Variants
dependent upon is the formal equivalent, standard in contracts and academic writing. Reliant on is the close synonym; contingent on is the conditional one.
Contrast
✗ dependent of and ✗ dependent from are errors — the preposition is fixed. Two spelling notes matter in American English: the adjective is dependent with -ent in every case, and the noun for a person you support is also dependent in American usage (claim two dependents), where British English spells the noun dependant. Note the two senses that share the pattern: dependent on her mother (needing support) versus dependent on the weather (determined by), which is why The picnic is dependent on the weather has nothing to do with trust. The opposite is independent of, with of, not from.

Needing someone or something in order to survive or continue — or determined by it.

  1. The town is almost entirely dependent on the mill.
  2. Whether we open Monday is dependent on the inspection.
  3. She has two children dependent on her.
  4. Funding is dependent upon a match from the county.
  5. The whole schedule is dependent on getting the parts by Friday.
See also
reliant on, independent of, based on, subject to, on

derived from#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈraɪvd frʌm/ · dih-RYVED from · weak /dəˈraɪvd frəm/
Русский
происходящий от, выведенный из — из источника, текста или расчёта
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; standard in science, etymology, finance, and cooking labels
Complement
a noun phrase naming the source, or a gerund naming the activity a thing comes out of (income derived from renting the top floor) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ derived from rent the top floor. A clause is impossible.
Pattern
be derived from [source] · [noun] derived from [noun] — the reduced relative, which is where the phrase does most of its work · derive [something] from [source] — the active verb · derived from [language] — in etymology
Variants
comes from is the everyday equivalent; obtained from and extracted from are the physical ones; based on covers a looser relationship; stems from and springs from are the metaphorical verbs.
Contrast
Derived from names an origin the thing was actually made out of or worked out from, which is more than based on claims: a screenplay is based on a novel, but morphine is derived from opium. In finance the word is exact — a derivative derived from* an underlying asset takes its value from it. Note that the source, not the product, follows from: ✓ aspirin is derived from willow bark, ✗ willow bark is derived from aspirin. ✗ derived of is an error, and derived by names the person or method, not the source: derived by Gauss, derived by regression.

Having its origin or its substance in something earlier — a source material, a text, or a calculation.

  1. The dye is derived from a root that only grows in two counties.
  2. Nearly half the town's revenue is derived from the port.
  3. The word is derived from an Old Norse verb meaning to scrape.
  4. These figures are derived from the same survey the state uses.
  5. Most of her income is derived from renting out the two back units.
See also
based on, grounded in, founded on, founded upon, independent of, from

deserving of#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈzɜrvɪŋ ʌv/ · dih-ZUR-ving uv · weak /dəˈzɜrvɪŋ əv/, and before a consonant often /dəˈzɜrvɪŋ ə/
Русский
заслуживающий чего-л. по делам или по достоинствам
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; in speech Americans use the plain verb (she deserves a raise) and keep deserving of for writing and for emphasis
Complement
a noun phrase, usually abstract — deserving of praise, deserving of a second look — or a gerund (deserving of being taken seriously). Never a to-infinitive after of: ✗ deserving of to win. The bare adjective takes no preposition at all in the deserving poor.
Pattern
be deserving of [noun / verb-ing] · hardly / richly / wholly deserving of · deserve [noun] — the verb, with a direct object and no preposition · deserve to [verb] — the verb with an infinitive
Variants
worthy of is the close twin and is commoner; meriting is the participial version; the plain verb deserve replaces the whole phrase in ordinary speech.
Contrast
The adjective takes of; the verb takes nothing. ✓ She deserves an answer. ✓ She is deserving of an answer. ✗ She deserves of an answer. That is the whole trap, and it is the same trap as worthy of. Against worthy of, which can weigh a thing's quality (a film worthy of the original), deserving of is about what someone has earned by conduct. Note the fixed phrase the deserving poor, an old piece of charity vocabulary that now reads as loaded.

Having earned something by conduct or merit, and so entitled to it.

  1. The proposal is deserving of a serious hearing, whatever its flaws.
  2. Nobody on that crew was less deserving of the blame.
  3. Her thirty years there are deserving of more than a sheet cake.
  4. The complaint is deserving of being investigated, not filed away.
  5. He behaved as if he were deserving of an exception to every rule.
See also
worthy of, entitled to, eligible for, guilty of, of

desirous of#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈzaɪrəs ʌv/ · dih-ZYE-rus uv · weak /dəˈzaɪrəs əv/
Русский
желающий чего-л. — устар./офиц., торжественный регистр
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal and dated — contract recitals (the parties being desirous of settling all claims), diplomatic notes, and nineteenth-century prose; in any other setting it sounds pompous, and modern American drafting guides tell you to write want
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (desirous of obtaining a license) — the gerund is by far the commoner of the two in the surviving legal formula. An older to-infinitive (desirous to please) is genuine but archaic and should not be imitated. No that-clause.
Pattern
be desirous of [noun / verb-ing] · the parties, being desirous of [verb-ing], agree as follows — the recital formula · desirous of [noun] in official correspondence
Variants
desiring to + infinitive and wishing to + infinitive are the living formal equivalents; anxious to and eager for cover the everyday range; want is the word to use in any modern document.
Contrast
This is a survival, not a live pattern. Where desirous of still appears, it is nearly always inside a whereas clause in a contract, and the modern replacement is a plain verb: The parties want to settle all claims. Against eager for and anxious about, which describe feelings, desirous of in legal use describes an intention formally recorded. Do not confuse desirous (wanting something) with desirable (wanted by others) — ✗ a desirous location is a common slip for desirable.

Wanting something — in a formal, and now largely ceremonial, register.

  1. The parties, being desirous of settling all claims, agree as follows.
  2. The company is desirous of acquiring the adjoining parcel.
  3. Any member desirous of speaking must be recognized by the chair.
  4. He was desirous of an appointment but too proud to ask for one.
  5. Both governments are desirous of avoiding further escalation.
See also
eager for, intent on, content with, worthy of, of

detrimental to#

Pronunciation
/ˌdɛtrəˈmɛntəl tu/ · det-ruh-MEN-tul too · weak /ˌdɛtrəˈmɛntəl tə/, with the medial t flapped: "det-ruh-MEN-nul-tuh"
Русский
вредный для, наносящий ущерб — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; standard in public-health writing, HR documents, and legal filings, where the plain word would be bad for
Complement
a noun phrase naming what suffers, or a gerund with the same role — never a to-infinitive: ✗ detrimental to sleep well. Note that the complement is the victim, not the cause: detrimental to your health, never ✗ detrimental to smoking.
Pattern
be detrimental to [person / interest / health] · seriously / materially detrimental to · to the detriment of [noun] — the noun frame · detrimental reliance — the legal term, no preposition
Variants
harmful to and damaging to are the neutral equivalents; bad for is the plain one; prejudicial to is the legal one and adds unfairness; deleterious to is the clinical one.
Contrast
Against harmful to, which is the ordinary word for injury, detrimental to is the institutional register and is often used of interests, reputations, and finances rather than bodies. Against prejudicial to, which in law means unfairly damaging to a party's case, detrimental to is neutral about fairness. Watch the direction of the preposition: the thing after to is the one being hurt. ✗ detrimental for and ✗ detrimental of are errors.

Causing harm or loss to somebody or something.

  1. Long shifts without a break are detrimental to accuracy on the line.
  2. Any delay now would be detrimental to the whole schedule.
  3. The report says the runoff is detrimental to the fishery downstream.
  4. Posting about the case was detrimental to her own claim.
  5. Nothing here is detrimental to the tenants; the change is administrative.
See also
harmful to, prejudicial to, conducive to, adverse to, to

devoted to#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈvoʊtɪd tu/ · dih-VOH-tid too · weak /dɪˈvoʊtɪd tə/
Русский
преданный кому-л., целиком отдающий себя чему-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral to warm
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (devoted to restoring the building) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ devoted to restore the building is the error; the noun test settles it (devoted to the restoration ✓).
Pattern
be devoted to [person / cause / verb-ing] · devote [time / a chapter] to [noun / verb-ing] · [portion] devoted to [noun]
Variants
dedicated to and committed to are near-identical and take the same gerund. Given over to is the version used of space and time.
Contrast
Devoted to is warmer than dedicated to and warmer still than committed to — devotion suggests love, dedication suggests discipline, commitment suggests obligation. All three take the gerund, and this is the single most useful thing to know about the trio. A second, purely spatial sense assigns a portion of something: two pages devoted to footnotes, where no feeling is involved. ✗ devoted for and ✗ devoted with are errors.

Giving loyalty, love, or resources entirely to someone or something.

  1. She was devoted to her students for thirty-one years.
  2. Half the budget is devoted to maintaining the trails.
  3. He's devoted to getting the archive digitized before he retires.
  4. The last chapter is devoted to the 1919 strike.
  5. They're a devoted pair, devoted to each other and to nobody else.
See also
committed to, dedicated to, intent on, married to, to

different from#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɪfrənt frʌm/ · DIF-runt from · weak /ˈdɪfrənt frəm/; the careful three-syllable /ˈdɪfərənt/ is also standard
Русский
отличающийся от, не такой, как — from стандартная американская форма
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; different from is the standard American form in every register
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, gerund (different from working alone), or a free relative (different from what I expected) after from — never a to-infinitive. Different than additionally takes a finite clause (different than I expected), which is exactly the environment that makes it useful.
Pattern
[X] is different from [Y] · no different from [noun] · very / quite / entirely different from [noun] · different than [clause] · differ from [noun] — the verb, from only
Variants
different than is common, respectable American English, strongest before a clause; different to is British and is not used in the United States. Differing from and distinct from are the formal equivalents.
Contrast
The honest American position has three parts. From is standard and never wrong: use it before a noun or pronoun. Than is the better choice before a clause, where from forces the clumsy from what: The job turned out different than I expected reads better than different from what I expected, and no American editor will stop you. Before a bare noun (different than his) some editors still object, so from is safer there. To is British — correct in London, foreign in Chicago. Note that the verb differ takes only from: ✗ differs than, ✗ differs to. See also different from, where the word is treated as a comparison preposition in its own right.

Not the same as something else — the central comparison adjective of English, and the one usage question in this file that is still live.

  1. Her second album is completely different from the first.
  2. City driving is different from anything he'd done upstate.
  3. The final bill was different than we'd been told it would be.
  4. This is no different from what happened in 2011.
  5. Winters here are different from winters anywhere else I've lived.
See also
similar to, identical to, equal to, different from, than, from

disappointed with#

Pronunciation
/ˌdɪsəˈpɔɪntɪd wɪθ/ · diss-uh-POYN-tid with · weak /ˌdɪsəˈpɔɪntɪd wəθ/
Русский
разочарованный в ком-л. или чём-л., обманувшийся в ожиданиях
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase after with, in, and by; a gerund after about (disappointed about missing it) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (disappointed to learn) and a that-clause (disappointed that no one called).
Pattern
be disappointed with [thing] · be disappointed in [person] · be disappointed by [the cause] · be disappointed about [situation / verb-ing] · be disappointed to [verb] · be disappointed (that) [clause]
Variants
let down by is the informal equivalent; dissatisfied with is the formal one and takes only with.
Contrast
Disappointed in is personal and moral; disappointed with is an assessment. I'm disappointed in you is a rebuke about character; I'm disappointed with the room is a review. Use in for people whose conduct fell short, with for things and outcomes, by when you want to point at the cause as an agent, and about for a situation. American speech leans on in for people far more than British English does. ✗ disappointed of and ✗ disappointed from are errors.

Let down because something or someone did not measure up.

  1. Fans were disappointed with the ending.
  2. Her father said he was disappointed in her, which was worse than shouting.
  3. We were disappointed by how little the report actually said.
  4. He's disappointed about missing the trip a second year running.
  5. I was disappointed to find the office already closed.

dispositive of#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈspɑzɪtɪv ʌv/ · dih-SPOZ-uh-tiv uv · weak /dəˈspɑzətəv əv/
Русский
разрешающий вопрос окончательно — юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal, narrowly — briefs, opinions, and motions; an American meets it in a court document and essentially nowhere else
Complement
a noun phrase naming the question, issue, or case that the thing settles — never a gerund, never a clause. What follows of is the matter decided, not the deciding fact.
Pattern
be dispositive of [issue / case] · not dispositive of [noun] — the commonest use, since the word usually appears in a denial · a dispositive motion — attributive, meaning one that can end the case
Variants
decisive of is the near equivalent; determinative of is the drier one; conclusive as to takes a different preposition; in plain English, settles the question.
Contrast
Dispositive of means it ends the inquiry, not merely that it matters — which is why the word so often appears negated: the affidavit is relevant but not dispositive of the issue*. Against material to, which means capable of affecting the outcome, and probative of, which means tending to prove a fact, dispositive of is the strongest of the three: material evidence bears on the question, probative evidence supports a finding, dispositive evidence closes it. Note the separate legal sense of dispositive meaning "relating to the disposition of property" (the dispositive provisions of the will), where no of follows. ✗ dispositive to is an error.

Settling a question outright, so that nothing further needs deciding.

  1. The waiver, if valid, is dispositive of the entire claim.
  2. That one email is not dispositive of intent, whatever the plaintiff says.
  3. The court found the timing dispositive of the jurisdictional question.
  4. Neither affidavit is dispositive of who was driving.
  5. Counsel conceded the point was dispositive of the appeal.
See also
material to, probative of, indicative of, pertinent to, of

eager for#

Pronunciation
/ˈiɡər fɔr/ · EE-gur for · weak /ˈiɡər fər/
Русский
жаждущий чего-л., нетерпеливо ожидающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase after for (eager for news) — a gerund after for is possible but stilted, and the natural way to put a verb after this adjective is the bare to-infinitive (eager to start), which is the commonest frame by a wide margin. A that-clause is possible in formal writing (eager that the record show).
Pattern
be eager for [noun] · be eager to [verb] · be eager for [someone] to [verb] · eager beaver (fixed compound)
Variants
keen on + -ing and keen to + verb are the British-flavored equivalents; anxious to and impatient for / to are the everyday American ones.
Contrast
Eager for takes a thing; eager to takes an action. Eager for the results* versus eager to see the results — and the second is what to write when a verb is involved, because eager for seeing the results is not idiomatic. Note the sharp difference from anxious about, which is dread: eager for Monday and anxious about Monday are opposite emotions with the same referent. ✗ eager of and ✗ eager at are errors.

Impatiently wanting something to arrive or to happen.

  1. The whole town was eager for a decision either way.
  2. She's eager to get back on the water.
  3. Investors are eager for any sign that hiring has picked up.
  4. He was eager for his daughter to see the place before it sold.
  5. New hires are always eager for feedback in the first month.
