anxious about, keen on, enthusiastic about, ready for, for

eligible for#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛlɪdʒəbəl fɔr/ · EL-uh-juh-bul for · weak /ˈɛlədʒəbəl fər/
Русский
имеющий право на, подходящий по условиям для
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; the standard word of American benefits, insurance, financial aid, and sports rosters
Complement
after for, a noun phrase naming the benefit, program, or status (eligible for parole, eligible for the tax credit) — and a gerund where the benefit is an activity (eligible for reinstatement, more naturally a noun). After bare to, a to-infinitive: eligible to vote, eligible to apply. The two frames do not mix: ✗ eligible for apply, ✗ eligible to a refund.
Pattern
be eligible for [benefit / program] · be eligible to [verb] · eligible for [noun] after [period] · deem / declare [someone] eligible for [noun] · eligibility for [noun] — the noun frame keeps for
Variants
entitled to is the stronger word — eligibility lets you apply, entitlement means it is owed to you. Qualified for stresses ability rather than status; in line for is informal and predictive.
Contrast
Eligible for + noun, eligible to + verb. This is the single thing to get right, and the mixed forms are the standard errors on American applications: ✓ eligible for benefits*, ✓ eligible to receive benefits, ✗ eligible for receive benefits. Against entitled to, the difference is legal and real: an eligible applicant may still be denied, an entitled one may not. ✗ eligible of and ✗ eligible in are errors.

Meeting the conditions that let you apply for something or take part in it.

  1. You're eligible for the credit if your income was under the cap last year.
  2. He becomes eligible for parole in November.
  3. Part-time staff are eligible for dental coverage after ninety days.
  4. She's eligible to vote in the primary but not in the special election.
  5. The building is eligible for listing on the state register.
See also
entitled to, suitable for, ready for, exempt from, for

encumbered by, encumbered with#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈkʌmbərd baɪ, ɪnˈkʌmbərd wɪθ/ · in-KUM-burd by, in-KUM-burd with · weak /ənˈkʌmbərd bə/ and /ənˈkʌmbərd wəθ/
Русский
обременённый чем-л. — юр. залогом на имуществе; вообще — нагруженный ношей
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
formal; a legal term of art about property, and a literary word about burdens generally
Complement
a noun phrase naming the burden — a lien, a mortgage, a debt, a load, a set of duties. A gerund is not used here (✗ encumbered with paying), and no clause follows.
Pattern
be encumbered by [burden] · be encumbered with [burden] · encumbered by debt / by regulation / with baggage · unencumbered by [noun] — the negative, which is commoner in journalism · an encumbrance on [property] — the noun frame, with on
Variants
burdened with and saddled with are the plainer versions; weighed down by is the physical one; subject to is the neutral legal term for property carrying a lien.
Contrast
The two prepositions are close, and the split is one of feel rather than rule: encumbered by points at the agent doing the burdening (encumbered by debt, encumbered by the rules), while encumbered with points at the load itself (encumbered with three suitcases, encumbered with obligations). In property law both appear, and encumbered by a mortgage is the commoner phrasing. Note the frequent literary use of the negative, unencumbered by, which usually carries a sneer: unencumbered by any knowledge of the subject. The related noun takes on: an encumbrance on the title.

Weighed down by something you have to carry — a debt or lien on property, or a load of any kind.

  1. The parcel is encumbered by a mortgage and two utility easements.
  2. She arrived encumbered with a cooler, a folding chair, and a dog.
  3. The agency is encumbered by rules written for a different century.
  4. A title encumbered by an old judgment can't be insured until it clears.
  5. He offers opinions unencumbered by any acquaintance with the file.
See also
fraught with, replete with, subject to, appurtenant to, with

endemic to#

Pronunciation
/ɛnˈdɛmɪk tu/ · en-DEM-ik too · weak /ɛnˈdɛmɪk tə/
Русский
эндемичный для — встречающийся только здесь и постоянно
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical — ecology, epidemiology, and public health; used loosely in journalism for a problem that will not go away
Complement
a noun phrase naming a place or a population — endemic to New Zealand, endemic to the region, endemic to that industry. Never a gerund (✗ endemic to logging trees) and never a clause. If what follows is not a place or a group, the word is being misused.
Pattern
[species / disease] be endemic to [place] · endemic in [population] — the epidemiologist's variant · endemic [noun] — attributive (endemic corruption, endemic species)
Variants
native to is the neutral word for where a species originated; indigenous to is its formal twin for peoples and species; confined to and restricted to say the same thing about distribution without the biology.
Contrast
Endemic to means found only there; epidemic and pandemic are about rate, not place. A disease endemic to a region is permanently present at a baseline level; an epidemic is a surge above it. Against native to, which says a species originated somewhere, endemic to* says it is found nowhere else — every endemic species is native, but not every native one is endemic. The loose journalistic use (corruption endemic to the department) is now standard but keeps the "built into this place" idea. ✗ endemic of is an error; endemic in is fine before a population.

Found only in a particular place or population, and permanently present there.

  1. The tortoise is endemic to a single island in the chain.
  2. Cholera remains endemic to several delta regions.
  3. Delays of this kind seem endemic to the permitting office.
  4. Half the plants in the preserve are endemic to this valley.
  5. The parasite is endemic to herds in the northern counties.
See also
native to, peculiar to, specific to, common to, to

endowed with#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈdaʊd wɪθ/ · in-DOWD with · weak /ənˈdaʊd wəθ/
Русский
наделённый чем-л. — качеством, полномочием или средствами
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
formal to literary; an American knows it from the Declaration of Independence — endowed by their Creator with certain unalienable Rights — and from institutional writing about gifts
Complement
a noun phrase naming the quality, power, or funds bestowed — never a gerund (✗ endowed with running fast) and never a clause. The giver, when named, goes in a by-phrase before the with-phrase.
Pattern
be endowed with [quality / power] · endowed by [giver] with [gift] · richly / generously / amply endowed with · endow [an institution] with [funds] · an endowed chair — attributive
Variants
blessed with is the warm everyday version; gifted with the slightly precious one; possessed of the formal one for qualities already held; furnished with and equipped with the practical ones.
Contrast
Against possessed of, which reports what somebody has, endowed with implies the quality was given — by nature, by a creator, or by a donor — which is why it fits rights, talents, and funds and not, say, debts. Note the two live senses: the abstract one (endowed with patience) and the financial one (an endowed professorship), where the verb takes money as its second object. A jocular use about physique exists and is worth knowing so as not to trip over it. ✗ endowed by + quality reverses the roles: endowed by nature names the giver, not the gift.

Given some quality, power, or fund of money by someone or something else.

  1. The charter leaves the commission endowed with real subpoena power.
  2. She was endowed with a memory for names that made the job easy.
  3. The chair is endowed with a gift from a class that graduated in 1948.
  4. The soil here is endowed with nothing but rocks and good drainage.
  5. Human beings, the text says, are endowed with reason and conscience.
See also
possessed of, vested with, replete with, full of, with

enforceable against#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈfɔrsəbəl əˈɡɛnst/ · in-FOR-suh-bul uh-GENST · weak /ənˈfɔrsəbəl əˈɡɛnst/, the final t usually dropped before a consonant: "uh-GENS"
Русский
принудительно исполнимый в отношении кого-л. — юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal; the natural home is the enforceability opinion in a loan closing (a valid obligation enforceable against the borrower in accordance with its terms)
Complement
a noun phrase naming the party against whom the obligation can be enforced — never a gerund and never a clause. Related slots take other prepositions: enforceable by [the party who may enforce], enforceable in [a court], enforceable under [a body of law].
Pattern
[obligation] be enforceable against [party] · valid, binding, and enforceable against [party] — the closing-opinion formula · enforceable by [party] against [party] · not enforceable against [third party]
Variants
binding on is the everyday equivalent from the other direction; actionable against is narrower and concerns suits; good against is the older property phrase (good against the world).
Three prepositions divide the roles cleanly
enforceable against the one who must perform, enforceable by the one who may sue, enforceable in the forum. A clause may be enforceable by the employer against the employee in state court — all three at once. Against binding on, binding upon, which states the same fact in the ordinary drafting idiom, enforceable against is the term used when the question is whether a court would actually make somebody perform. ✗ enforceable to and ✗ enforceable on are errors.

Capable of being made binding on a particular person by a court.

  1. The guaranty is enforceable against the parent company, not just the subsidiary.
  2. An oral promise like that is rarely enforceable against anyone.
  3. Counsel opined that the note is valid and enforceable against the borrower.
  4. The covenant runs with the land and is enforceable against later owners.
  5. A settlement signed by one heir is not enforceable against the others.
See also
binding on, binding upon, bound by, liable for, liable to, immune from, immune to, against

engaged to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈɡeɪdʒd tu/ · in-GAYJD too · weak /ɪnˈɡeɪdʒd tə/
Русский
помолвленный с; engaged in — занятый чем-л., engaged with — вовлечённый во что-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person after to — never a gerund (✗ engaged to marrying). After in, a noun phrase or gerund (engaged in negotiating the lease) — never a to-infinitive after either. The single exception is the frozen engaged to be married, where to is an infinitive marker inside a set phrase and no person follows it; that phrase is not a model for anything else the adjective does.
Pattern
be engaged to [person] · get engaged to [person] · be engaged in [activity / verb-ing] · be engaged with [an issue or a community] · engaged to be married
Variants
betrothed to is the archaic form; promised to survives in period fiction. For the activity sense, involved in and occupied with are the everyday equivalents.
Contrast
Engaged to is about marriage; engaged in is about activity; engaged with is about sustained attention. She's engaged to a lawyer* (they will marry), She's engaged in litigation (that is what she is doing), She's engaged with the community (she participates seriously). Swapping them produces nonsense or comedy. ✗ engaged with a lawyer does not mean betrothed. Note also engaged meaning occupied — the line is engaged — which is British; American English says busy.

Promised in marriage, or occupied in an activity, depending on the preposition.

  1. She got engaged to her college roommate's brother.
  2. The two firms are engaged in a dispute over the patent.
  3. He's been engaged to the same woman for six years.
  4. The department is engaged in rewriting the entire handbook.
  5. Students who are engaged with the material don't need to be told twice.
See also
married to, involved in, committed to, to, in

enthusiastic about#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˌθuziˈæstɪk əˈbaʊt/ · in-thoo-zee-ASS-tik uh-BOWT · weak /ɪnˌθuziˈæstɪk əˈbaʊt/
Русский
восторженно относящийся к, горячо поддерживающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the everyday words are excited about and into
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (enthusiastic about trying again) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ enthusiastic to start is not idiomatic; write eager to start.
Pattern
be enthusiastic about [noun / verb-ing] · less than enthusiastic about [noun] · enthusiastic supporter of [noun] — the noun form takes of
Variants
excited about is the everyday twin; keen on the British one; into the casual American one (she's really into pottery).
Contrast
Enthusiastic about takes a gerund; eager to takes an infinitive — this pair is worth memorizing together, because they mean nearly the same thing and cannot borrow each other's structure: ✓ enthusiastic about starting*, ✓ eager to start, ✗ enthusiastic to start. The litotes less than enthusiastic about is the standard American way of saying someone hated it. ✗ enthusiastic for and ✗ enthusiastic of are errors, though the noun enthusiasm takes for: enthusiasm for the project.

Showing eager approval of something.

  1. The staff was enthusiastic about moving to a four-day week.
  2. He's enthusiastic about anything with a two-stroke engine.
  3. The board was less than enthusiastic about the price tag.
  4. She's enthusiastic about teaching the intro section again.
  5. Reviewers were enthusiastic about the first season and cooler about the second.
See also
excited about, eager for, keen on, interested in, about

entitled to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈtaɪtəld tu/ · in-TY-tuld too · weak /ənˈtaɪtəld tə/, the t flapped: "in-TY-dul-duh"
Русский
имеющий право на — по закону, договору или по заслугам
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the standard word in American benefits law, contracts, and everyday argument about rights
Complement
two frames, and both are correct. With a noun phrase, the to is a preposition: entitled to a refund, entitled to back pay. With a bare verb, the to is an infinitive marker: entitled to know, entitled to vote. What is not available is the gerund: ✗ entitled to knowing, ✗ entitled to receiving a refund. This is the one entry in the file where the noun test comes out positive (entitled to a refund ✓) and the infinitive is still legal, so the test has to be used in the other direction — the -ing form is the form to avoid.
Pattern
be entitled to [noun] · be entitled to [verb] · entitle [someone] to [noun] · an entitlement to [noun] — the noun frame · entitled [title] — the unrelated sense, "given the title," as in a book entitled ...
Variants
eligible for is the weaker word (you may apply); have a right to is the plain paraphrase and behaves the same way; due takes no preposition (the refund due her).
Contrast
Entitled to means it is owed you; eligible for means you may apply for it. An eligible applicant can be turned down; an entitled claimant can sue. Note the second, unrelated entitled* meaning "titled" — a memo entitled "Weekend Coverage" — which takes no preposition and no complement of this kind; some American editors prefer plain titled there to keep the two apart. And note the modern pejorative entitled used alone (an entitled kid), which takes no complement at all. ✗ entitled for and ✗ entitled of are errors.

Having a right to something, whether by law, by contract, or by plain desert.

  1. Every employee is entitled to an itemized pay stub.
  2. You're entitled to know what the charge was before you sign.
  3. She's entitled to three weeks off and hasn't taken a day.
  4. Riders are entitled to a refund if the delay runs over an hour.
  5. He isn't entitled to a hearing until the appeal is docketed.
See also
eligible for, deserving of, worthy of, liable for, liable to, to

envious of#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛnviəs ʌv/ · EN-vee-uss uv · weak /ˈɛnviəs əv/
Русский
завидующий кому-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; jealous of is the everyday word
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (envious of having the whole summer free) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be envious of [person / thing] · envious of [someone's] [possession] · make [someone] envious
Variants
jealous of covers the same ground in speech; covetous of is the biblical register.
Contrast
Envious of wants what someone else has; jealous of also guards what it already has. Envious of her promotion* is straightforward; jealous of her promotion means the same thing in ordinary speech, but jealous of his time means protective of it, a sense envious cannot carry. Careful writers keep envy for wanting and jealousy for guarding, though the distinction is widely ignored. ✗ envious about and ✗ envious at are errors.

Wanting what someone else has.

  1. I'm envious of anyone who can sleep on a plane.
  2. She was envious of the extra week they got in August.
  3. He's envious of his brother's easy way with strangers.
  4. Colleagues were quietly envious of the corner office.
  5. Nobody is envious of the job once they've seen the hours.
See also
jealous of, proud of, suspicious of, of

equal to#

Pronunciation
/ˈikwəl tu/ · EE-kwull too · weak /ˈikwəl tə/
Русский
равный чему-л.; идиом. — способный справиться с задачей
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (equal to running the whole thing alone) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ equal to run it is an error, though the idiom equal to the task is a fixed noun frame that hides the same structure.
Pattern
[X] is equal to [Y] · equal to the task / equal to the occasion · equal in [respect] to [noun] · equal parts [X] and [Y] · all things being equal
Variants
equivalent to is the technical version; the same as is the everyday one; on a par with is the idiom of comparison.
Contrast
Equal to takes to, never than. ✗ equal than* is a persistent error made by speakers whose first language uses a comparative there; the same warning applies to superior to, inferior to, senior to, and preferable to, which all reject than. The idiomatic sense — equal to the task, meaning up to it — is not about quantity at all, and equal to + -ing belongs with it. See equal to for the word as a comparison preposition.

The same in amount, value, or capability — or, idiomatically, up to what a job demands.

  1. One tablespoon is equal to three teaspoons.
  2. The settlement was equal to four months' pay.
  3. She proved equal to the task within a week.
  4. Nothing on the menu is equal to what they served here in 2003.
  5. Their offer is equal to ours in everything but timing.
See also
identical to, similar to, superior to, inferior to, equal to

equivalent to#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈkwɪvələnt tu/ · ih-KWIV-uh-lunt too · weak /əˈkwɪvələnt tə/
Русский
равнозначный, эквивалентный чему-л. — равный по действию, но не тождественный
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to technical; standard in science, finance, and translation, and common in ordinary argument
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (equivalent to admitting the whole thing) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ equivalent to admit the whole thing. A wh-clause is fine: equivalent to what the state pays.
Pattern
be equivalent to [noun / verb-ing] · roughly / functionally / morally equivalent to · the equivalent of [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to of · equivalent in [respect] to [noun]
Variants
tantamount to is the near twin, restricted to the "amounts to the same thing" sense and nearly always about something bad; identical to claims sameness rather than equal value; the same as is the plain phrase; comparable to claims only that a comparison is fair.
Contrast
Equivalent to claims equal value or effect; identical to claims the things are the same. A metric wrench may be equivalent to an inch wrench for the job without being identical to it. Note the switch of preposition between adjective and noun: equivalent to a month's rent*, but the equivalent of a month's rent. Against tantamount to, which is judgmental, equivalent to is neutral and quantitative. ✗ equivalent with is an error, and ✗ equivalent as is worse.

Equal in value, effect, or function to something else, without necessarily being the same thing.

  1. The stipend is equivalent to about nineteen dollars an hour.
  2. A year of the certificate program is equivalent to two semesters of credit.
  3. Saying nothing here is equivalent to agreeing with them.
  4. The generic is chemically equivalent to the brand and costs a fifth as much.
  5. That degree is equivalent to an American master's for licensing purposes.
See also
identical to, tantamount to, equal to, synonymous with, to

essential to#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈsɛnʃəl tu/ · ih-SEN-shull too · weak /ɪˈsɛnʃəl tə/
Русский
необходимый для — то, без чего всё рушится
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after to and for (essential to keeping the doors open) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition. The it-frame takes an infinitive with no preposition: It is essential to arrive early, and formal writing uses a subjunctive that-clause: essential that he be present.
Pattern
[X] is essential to [Y] · [X] is essential for [purpose / verb-ing] · it is essential to [verb] · it is essential that [subjunctive clause] · absolutely essential
Variants
vital to, crucial to, critical to, indispensable to, and integral to all pattern the same way and all take to by default and for before a purpose.
Contrast
To names what the thing is essential to; for names what it is essential for. Essential to the plan* (the plan needs it) versus essential for germination (it makes germination happen) — the drift is real but the two overlap widely and neither is wrong in most sentences. Watch the subjunctive: formal American writing keeps It is essential that she be notified, not is notified. ✗ essential of is an error.

Absolutely necessary — the thing without which the rest fails.

  1. Trust is essential to any working partnership.
  2. Water at the right temperature is essential for getting the dough to rise.
  3. Timing was essential to the whole rescue.
  4. It is essential to read the second page before signing.
  5. Redundancy is essential to a system that can't be allowed to fail.
See also
critical of, relevant to, useful for, suitable for, to

excited about#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈsaɪtɪd əˈbaʊt/ · ik-SY-tid uh-BOWT · weak /ɪkˈsaɪtɪd əˈbaʊt/, casually /ɪkˈsaɪtɪd ˈbaʊt/
Русский
радостно предвкушающий что-л., воодушевлённый
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral; extremely common in American speech and business writing
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about and by (excited about starting) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (excited to announce), and that frame is standard American English, not an error.
Pattern
be excited about [noun / verb-ing] · be excited by [the cause] · be excited for [an event, or a person on their behalf] · be excited to [verb] · get excited about [noun]
Variants
thrilled about / to, psyched about (informal), and pumped about (informal) all follow the same frames.
Contrast
This adjective is unusual in taking both the gerund and the infinitive naturally, with a small difference: excited about starting is about the whole prospect, excited to start is about the act itself and is the standard frame in announcements (we're excited to share). Excited by points at the cause as agent (excited by the possibility). Excited for has two uses that are worth separating: excited for the game (looking forward to an event, once informal and now general American) and excited for you (happy on your behalf). ✗ excited of is an error.

Eagerly looking forward to something, or stirred up by it.

  1. The kids are excited about the trip to the coast.
  2. We're excited to announce the new fall schedule.
  3. She's excited about moving closer to her sister.
  4. He was genuinely excited for her when the offer came through.
  5. Everybody got excited about the renovation until they saw the estimate.
See also
enthusiastic about, eager for, happy about, about

exempt from#

Pronunciation
/ɪɡˈzɛmpt frʌm/ · ig-ZEMPT from · weak /əɡˈzɛmp frəm/ — the pt cluster simplifies to /p/ before a consonant, so exempt from is normally "ig-ZEM-frum"
Русский
освобождённый от — от правила, налога, обязанности
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the standard word of American tax, wage-and-hour law, and school and military rules
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund — exempt from paying sales tax ✓ — and never a to-infinitive: ✗ exempt from pay sales tax. The noun test is decisive: exempt from the tax ✓. No clause follows.
Pattern
be exempt from [rule / tax / requirement] · exempt [someone] from [obligation] — the verb · exempt from [noun] under [statute] · tax-exempt / an exempt employee — attributive, no preposition
Variants
excused from is the everyday version for duties and classes; immune from is the legal one for liability and process; free of and free from cover the general sense; not subject to is the drafter's paraphrase.
Contrast
Exempt from releases you from a rule that would otherwise apply to you — which is the exact opposite of subject to, and the two often appear in the same sentence. Against immune from, immune to, the difference is what is escaped: an exemption is granted by a rule, an immunity is a shield against liability or process. In American employment law the word is a noun as well: an exempt employee* is one exempt from overtime, and nonexempt is the pay-by-the-hour category. ✗ exempt of and ✗ exempt to are errors.

Released by a rule from something that would otherwise apply to you.

  1. Nonprofits are exempt from the local business tax.
  2. She's exempt from the language requirement because of the test score.
  3. Nobody in this building is exempt from the fire drill.
  4. Farm vehicles are exempt from the weight limit on that stretch.
  5. Salaried supervisors are exempt from overtime under the state rule.
See also
immune from, immune to, free of, subject to, eligible for, from

expert at#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛkspɜrt æt/ · EK-spurt at · weak /ˈɛkspɜrt ət/
Русский
искусный в чём-л. — прилагательное берёт at/in, а существительное an expert on
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (expert at getting people to talk) and after in (expert in tax law) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ expert to do it is not English.
Pattern
be expert at [skill / verb-ing] · be expert in [field] · an expert on [subject] — the noun takes on · an expert in [field] · expert witness (no preposition)
Variants
skilled at / in, proficient in, adept at, and a whiz at cover the range from formal to slangy.
Contrast
The adjective and the noun take different prepositions, and this is the trap. ✓ She is expert at reading X-rays (adjective, at + skill) and ✓ She is an expert on medieval coins (noun, on + subject). In works for both when a field rather than a skill or a topic is meant: expert in immunology. ✗ expert of is an error in every frame. Note that American English uses the adjective expert far less than the noun; she's an expert at is commoner than she's expert at.

Highly skilled — at a practice, in a field, or, as a noun, on a subject.

  1. He's expert at talking people down from a bad decision.
  2. She's expert in the kind of soil chemistry nobody else here understands.
  3. The witness is an expert on structural failures in older bridges.
  4. They're expert at finding parking downtown, which is its own talent.
  5. We need someone expert in export controls before we bid.

familiar with#

Pronunciation
/fəˈmɪljər wɪθ/ · fuh-MIL-yur with · weak /fəˈmɪljər wəθ/
Русский
хорошо знакомый с чем-л.; familiar to — знакомый кому-л., с другой стороны
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (familiar with running a crew) — never a to-infinitive. A wh-clause is possible in the loose sense (familiar with how it works), and a that-clause is not: ✗ familiar that.
Pattern
be familiar with [thing known] · be familiar to [person who knows it] · become / get familiar with [noun] · be too familiar with [person] — the disapproving sense
Variants
acquainted with is the formal equivalent; up on is the casual one (up on the new rules).
Contrast
The subject flips between the two prepositions, and this is the classic error. I'm familiar with the software — the knower is the subject. The software is familiar to me — the thing known is the subject. ✗ The software is familiar with me and ✗ I'm familiar to the software are both wrong. A third sense of the bare adjective means presumptuous intimacy (don't be familiar), which is why too familiar with the customers can be a complaint rather than a compliment.

Knowing something well enough to work with it — or, from the other side, being recognizable to someone.

  1. Are you familiar with the county's permitting process?
  2. Her face was familiar to everyone in the courtroom.
  3. It takes a week to get familiar with the layout.
  4. He's familiar with running a kitchen twice this size.
  5. The tune was familiar to me but I couldn't place it.
See also
accustomed to, aware of, used to, known for, with, to

famous for#

Pronunciation
/ˈfeɪməs fɔr/ · FAY-muss for · weak /ˈfeɪməs fər/
Русский
знаменитый чем-л.; famous as — знаменитый в качестве кого-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (famous for turning down the part) after for — never a to-infinitive. After as, a noun phrase naming a role (famous as a trial lawyer).
Pattern
be famous for [noun / verb-ing] · be famous as [role] · famous for being [adjective] · world-famous for [noun] · famous throughout [place]
Variants
known for is the neutral everyday version; renowned for and celebrated for are the formal ones; notorious for is the negative one.
Contrast
For names what you did; as names what you are. Famous for her Fourth Symphony* versus famous as a conductor. Against notorious for, which is always negative, famous for is neutral to positive — so famous for cutting corners is a joke that works because it borrows the wrong word on purpose. ✗ famous of and ✗ famous by are errors.

Widely known — for something done, or as something one is.

  1. The town is famous for a pie nobody there actually eats.
  2. She's famous for never missing a deadline.
  3. He was famous as a defense attorney long before the trial.
  4. That stretch of coast is famous for fog in July.
  5. The diner is famous for staying open through three hurricanes.
See also
known for, notorious for, popular with, for

fed up with#

Pronunciation
/ˌfɛd ˈʌp wɪθ/ · fed-UP with · weak /ˌfɛd ˈʌp wəθ/
Русский
сытый по горло чем-л. — разг.
Type
adjective phrase + preposition
Register
informal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (fed up with waiting) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be fed up with [noun / verb-ing] · get fed up with [noun] · fed up (alone, with no complement) · thoroughly / completely fed up with [noun]
Variants
sick of, tired of, sick and tired of, and done with are the American equivalents. Fed up of is British colloquial and is an error in American English.
Contrast
Fed up takes with; sick and tired take of. ✗ fed up of the noise* and ✗ sick with the noise both go wrong by borrowing the other pattern. Against bored with, which reports flatness, fed up with reports irritation that has been building — it is the end of a person's patience, not the end of their interest. The three-word intensifier sick and tired of is fixed and cannot be rebuilt as ✗ sick and fed up of.

Out of patience with something that has gone on too long.

  1. She's fed up with being the only one who answers the phone.
  2. Residents are fed up with the potholes on Elm.
  3. I got fed up with the whole process and hired someone.
  4. He's fed up with excuses about the delivery date.
  5. By March everyone is fed up with winter.
See also
sick of, tired of, bored with, annoyed with, with

fond of#

Pronunciation
/fɑnd ʌv/ · FOND uv · weak /fɑnd əv/
Русский
любящий что-л., питающий давнюю склонность к
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral, slightly old-fashioned and warm
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (fond of telling that story) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ fond to tell is not English.
Pattern
be fond of [noun / verb-ing] · grow / become fond of [noun] · fond of saying [quotation] · not overly fond of [noun]
Variants
keen on is British; partial to is the same shade with a hint of indulgence; like is the plain verb.
Contrast
Fond of + -ing often carries a note of habit rather than pleasure: He's fond of reminding us that he warned them is a criticism, not a compliment. The bare adjective before a noun means affectionate (a fond farewell, fond memories) and is a different use with no preposition. ✗ fond about and ✗ fond for are errors, though the noun takes for: a fondness for old maps.

Having affection or a liking for something — often an established, habitual one.

  1. She's fond of her students in a way that shows up in the details.
  2. He's fond of saying the roof will last another winter.
  3. I've grown fond of the noise from the rail yard.
  4. They're not overly fond of surprises at that office.
  5. My grandmother was fond of anything with cardamom in it.
See also
crazy about, keen on, proud of, of

founded on, founded upon#

Pronunciation
/ˈfaʊndɪd ɑn, ˈfaʊndɪd əˈpɑn/ · FOWN-did on, FOWN-did uh-PON · weak /ˈfaʊndəd ən/
Русский
основанный на — на принципе, доказательствах или допущении
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
formal; the natural home is writing about principles, claims, and institutions, and upon is the rhetorical variant
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (founded on keeping everyone in the dark) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ founded on keep everyone in the dark. Also founded in before an abstract quality (founded in fact, founded in law), where in is idiomatic.
Pattern
be founded on [principle / evidence] · founded upon [noun] — the formal variant · well founded / ill founded — as one word in unfounded · found [an institution] on [principle] — the active verb
Variants
based on is the ordinary everyday equivalent; grounded in is the near twin and prefers in; predicated on is the drier formal one; resting on is the metaphor made visible.
Contrast
Against based on, which is neutral and common, founded on carries the architectural metaphor and suits institutions, doctrines, and claims — a republic founded on a written constitution, a suspicion founded on nothing. Against grounded in, note the preposition split: things are founded on a foundation but grounded in a medium. The negative has fused into a single word: an unfounded rumor, never ✗ a not founded rumor. ✗ founded in is fine before fact, law, and truth, but ✗ founded of and ✗ founded to are errors.

Built on something as its base — a principle, a body of evidence, or an assumption.

  1. The whole complaint is founded on a misreading of one paragraph.
  2. Their objection is founded upon forty years of practice in the district.
  3. A partnership founded on a handshake is hard to unwind.
  4. The estimate was founded on prices from before the tariffs.
  5. Her confidence is founded on having done the job twice before.
See also
based on, grounded in, derived from, dependent on, on

fraught with#

Pronunciation
/frɔt wɪθ/ · FRAWT with · weak /frɔt wəθ/, the t often lost before the w: "FRAW-with"
Русский
чреватый чем-л. — риском, трудностями, напряжением; книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to journalistic; the phrase fraught with peril and fraught with risk are near-clichés of American editorial writing
Complement
a noun phrase naming something bad and abundant — danger, difficulty, risk, error, tension. Never a gerund (✗ fraught with delaying) and never a clause. The complement is essentially always negative: ✗ fraught with opportunity reads as a mistake, though the word once meant simply "laden."
Pattern
be fraught with [danger / difficulty] · a process fraught with [noun] · fraught — used alone as a modern adjective meaning tense (a fraught conversation), with no complement
Variants
riddled with is the near twin for flaws and errors; rife with is the journalist's word for something bad and widespread; replete with is the neutral-to-positive counterpart; laden with keeps the original cargo metaphor.
Contrast
Against replete with, which is neutral or admiring and means "abundantly supplied," fraught with is always negative — the two words are the same idea with opposite polarity. Note the recent and now fully standard bare use of fraught: the whole meeting was fraught takes no complement at all and means tense rather than dangerous, and it is an adjective, not this preposition pattern. ✗ fraught of and ✗ fraught by are errors.

Loaded with something bad — risk, difficulty, tension, or error.

  1. The handoff between the two systems is fraught with opportunities for error.
  2. Any conversation about the will is fraught with forty years of grievance.
  3. The route is fraught with switchbacks and no place to turn around.
  4. Early estimates were fraught with assumptions nobody wrote down.
  5. Their first season back was fraught with injuries.
See also
replete with, encumbered by, encumbered with, full of, crowded with, with

free of#

Pronunciation
/fri ʌv/ · FREE uv · weak /fri əv/
Русский
свободный от, лишённый чего-л. нежелательного
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase after of and from (free of debt, free from interference) — a gerund is possible but rare (free from having to explain herself). The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (free to leave), which is a different frame meaning permitted, not lacking.
Pattern
be free of [unwanted thing] · be free from [an imposed condition] · be free to [verb] · [noun]-free as a suffix: tax-free, gluten-free, scot-free
Variants
free of and free from overlap almost completely; from is slightly more formal and is preferred where the thing removed was imposed by someone (free from persecution). Rid of and devoid of are the neighbors, both with of.
Contrast
Free of / from means lacking; free to means allowed. Free of charge* costs nothing; free to go may leave. The suffix -free has become the productive American way to say the same thing in a single word (fee-free, fragrance-free) and takes no preposition at all. ✗ free with means something different again — generous, and often too generous: free with other people's money.

Without something unwanted, or released from it.

  1. The house came back from inspection free of any structural issues.
  2. She's been free of the debt for two years.
  3. The area is now free from the flooding that plagued it for a decade.
  4. You're free to leave whenever you like.
  5. Their new formula is free of dyes and preservatives.
See also
safe from, absent from, independent of, short of, of

friendly with#

Pronunciation
/ˈfrɛndli wɪθ/ · FREND-lee with · weak /ˈfrɛndli wəθ/
Русский
в дружеских отношениях с; friendly to — приветливый, доброжелательный к
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person or group after with and to; a noun after toward — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive after any of them.
Pattern
be friendly with [person] · be friendly to / toward [person] · get friendly with [person] · [noun]-friendly as a suffix: kid-friendly, pet-friendly, user-friendly
Variants
toward is the American spelling; towards is the British one, and both are read as ordinary in the United States. On good terms with is the formal equivalent of friendly with.
Contrast
Friendly with means you have a relationship; friendly to means you behave pleasantly. She's friendly with the mayor* implies they know each other; She's friendly to everyone who comes in describes her manner and implies nothing about acquaintance. That difference matters — the first can insinuate favoritism, which is exactly why reporters choose it. The productive suffix -friendly means accommodating to and takes no preposition: a dog-friendly patio. ✗ friendly of is an error, though That was friendly of you is a different, correct frame.

On good terms with someone, or pleasant in manner toward them.

  1. He's been friendly with the family since before the kids were born.
  2. The staff is friendly to anyone who walks in, regulars or not.
  3. She got friendly with the contractor and now gets the good slots.
  4. Their policy is friendly toward small vendors.
  5. It's a friendly town, friendly to newcomers in a way that surprised us.
See also
kind to, nice to, polite to, popular with, with

frightened of#

Pronunciation
/ˈfraɪtnd ʌv/ · FRY-tund uv · weak /ˈfraɪtnd əv/
Русский
напуганный чем-л., боящийся
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral; slightly more formal than scared of
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (frightened of falling) after of; noun phrase after by — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (frightened to move), a separate frame.
Pattern
be frightened of [noun / verb-ing] · be frightened by [the event that caused it] · be frightened to [verb] · be frightened for [someone] · frighten [someone] (no preposition on the object)
Variants
afraid of and scared of are the everyday twins; terrified of and petrified of are the intensified ones.
Contrast
Frightened of is a standing fear; frightened by is a single event. She's frightened of dogs* describes a phobia; She was frightened by the dog describes one afternoon. This of / by split is the same one that runs through annoyed, bored, confused, and shocked, and it is the most transferable pattern in this file: of or with for the standing state, by for the agent that produced it. And as with afraid, frightened to move means too scared to act, not scared of the movement.

Feeling fear — of something in general, or by something in particular.

  1. He's been frightened of elevators since the outage.
  2. The horse was frightened by the truck backfiring.
  3. She was too frightened to say anything at the time.
  4. Small children are often frightened of men with beards.
  5. They were frightened for their neighbors, whose lights had been off for days.
See also

full of#

Pronunciation
/fʊl ʌv/ · FULL uv · weak /fʊl əv/, and in fast speech /ˈfʊlə/ — the fulla of fulla nonsense
Русский
полный чего-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, usually without an article for mass and plural nouns (full of sand, full of tourists) — a gerund is not used, and never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
be full of [noun] · full of [abstract quality]: full of ideas, full of himself · fill [something] with [something] — the verb takes with · filled with [noun]
Variants
filled with, packed with, and loaded with take with; replete with is the formal one; chock-full of is the emphatic American one.
Contrast
Full takes of*; fill and filled take with. ✗ full with water* and ✗ filled of water are the two errors, and they are mirror images. The idiomatic full of himself means conceited; full of it is a polite American way to say someone is lying. Note that full also works with no complement (I'm full), where it means having eaten enough.

Containing as much as it can hold, or containing a great deal of something.

  1. The trunk was full of road salt and jumper cables.
  2. Her first draft is full of good sentences in the wrong order.
  3. The lot was full of cars with out-of-state plates.
  4. He's full of plans and short on follow-through.
  5. The room was full of people who had never met the deceased.

furious with#

Pronunciation
/ˈfjʊriəs wɪθ/ · FYOOR-ee-uss with · weak /ˈfjʊriəs wəθ/
Русский
в ярости на кого-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; the strongest ordinary word for anger
Complement
a person after with; a noun phrase or gerund after about and at (furious about being kept waiting) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: furious that no one had called.
Pattern
be furious with [person] · be furious about / at / over [situation] · be furious (that) [clause] · furious with [person] for [verb-ing]
Variants
livid, irate, and incensed are the same intensity; incensed prefers at or by, irate prefers about or at.
Contrast
Same split as angry: with for the person, about or over for the situation, at for either. Furious with her lawyer about the delay fills both slots at once. Note that furious also means very fast (a furious pace), a sense with no preposition at all and no anger in it. ✗ furious on is an error.

Extremely angry.

  1. She was furious with the contractor for starting without a permit.
  2. Parents were furious about the way the closure was announced.
  3. He's furious at himself for not reading the fine print.
  4. The whole shop was furious with management by Thursday.
  5. They were furious that nobody had thought to call the family first.

generous with#

Pronunciation
/ˈdʒɛnərəs wɪθ/ · JEN-uh-russ with · weak /ˈdʒɛnərəs wəθ/
Русский
щедрый на что-л.; generous to — щедрый к кому-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun naming what is given after with (generous with her time); a person after to and toward — never a gerund after either, and never a to-infinitive, though the it-frame takes one: It was generous of you to offer.
Pattern
be generous with [resource] · be generous to / toward [person] · it is generous of [someone] to [verb] · generous in [respect]
Variants
liberal with is the older, less loaded version; free with is the version that implies excess (free with his opinions).
Contrast
With takes what you give; to takes who you give it to. Generous with his time* and generous to his employees both work, and generous to his time does not. The of-frame is a third pattern common to all judgment adjectives — kind of you, rude of him, generous of them — where of names the doer and an infinitive names the deed. ✗ generous for is an error.

Giving freely — of a resource, or to a person.

  1. She's generous with her time in a way that costs her weekends.
  2. The company has been generous to families of the crew.
  3. He's generous with praise and stingy with raises.
  4. It was generous of you to cover the difference.
  5. Be generous with the butter or the whole thing sticks.
See also
kind to, gentle with, patient with, with

gentle with#

Pronunciation
/ˈdʒɛntəl wɪθ/ · JEN-tull with · weak /ˈdʒɛntəl wəθ/
Русский
бережно, мягко обращающийся с кем-л. или чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun naming what is handled after with — a person, an animal, or an object. Never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, and never a clause. The it-frame takes an infinitive: It was gentle of him to say it that way is possible but rare.
Pattern
be gentle with [person / animal / object] · go gentle on [someone] — informal · gentle on [something], of products: gentle on skin
Variants
easy on is the informal equivalent (go easy on him); tender with is the warmer one; careful with overlaps almost entirely.
Contrast
Gentle takes with*, not to, which breaks the pattern that governs most of the behavior adjectives — kind to*, cruel to, rude to, but gentle with and patient with. The reason is that gentle and patient describe the manner of handling rather than the direction of treatment, and with is the preposition of manner. Product English uses a fourth frame, gentle on: gentle on sensitive skin. ✗ gentle to is not idiomatic in American English.

Handling someone or something carefully, without force.

  1. Be gentle with the latch; it's original to the house.
  2. She's remarkably gentle with frightened animals.
  3. The nurse was gentle with him during the whole exam.
  4. Go easy — be gentle with yourself about the first draft.
  5. The formula is gentle on skin and still gets the grease off.
See also
careful with, patient with, kind to, with

germane to#

Pronunciation
/dʒərˈmeɪn tu/ · jur-MAYN too · weak /dʒərˈmeɪn tə/
Русский
имеющий прямое отношение к обсуждаемому предмету — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; an American meets it in parliamentary procedure (the amendment is not germane to the bill), in court, and in academic argument
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (germane to deciding the motion) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ germane to decide the motion. A wh-clause is fine: germane to what the committee is considering.
Pattern
be germane to [question / matter] · not germane to [noun] — the commonest frame, since the word is usually used to rule something out · strictly / directly germane to
Variants
relevant to is the neutral everyday equivalent; pertinent to is its formal twin; material to is the legal one and is stronger; apposite to is the literary one; on point is the American lawyer's idiom.
Contrast
Germane to is the word for topical fit, and its home is the rules of debate — in the House and in most American legislatures an amendment must be germane to the bill, meaning it must address the same subject. Against relevant to, which is the general word, germane to* claims a closer relation: relevant evidence bears on the case, a germane amendment belongs to the very question at hand. Against material to, which asks whether something could change the outcome, germane to asks only whether it belongs to the subject. ✗ germane with and ✗ germane for are errors.

Belonging to the very subject under discussion, not merely touching on it.

  1. The chair ruled that the amendment was not germane to the bill.
  2. Whatever happened in 2011 is not germane to this year's audit.
  3. Only questions germane to the witness's direct testimony are allowed.
  4. Keep the comments germane to the item on the agenda.
  5. That objection is germane to the schedule but not to the design.
See also
relevant to, pertinent to, material to, specific to, to

glad about#

Pronunciation
/ɡlæd əˈbaʊt/ · GLAD uh-BOWT · weak /ɡlæd əˈbaʊt/
Русский
радующийся тому, что что-л. так
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral, slightly old-fashioned in the about frame
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about (glad about not having to drive) — never a to-infinitive after about. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (glad to help) and a that-clause (glad that you called), and those two frames are far commoner than the prepositional one.
Pattern
be glad about [noun / verb-ing] · be glad of [something one is grateful for] · be glad to [verb] · be glad (that) [clause] · be glad for [someone]
Variants
happy about is the everyday equivalent and covers all the same frames; pleased about is the slightly more formal one.
Contrast
Glad to + verb is the workhorse and means willing (glad to help); glad about + noun reports a feeling about a state of affairs. Glad of* is an older frame that survives in glad of the company and glad of the chance and reads as literary in American English. Glad for you means pleased on your behalf, exactly parallel to happy for you and excited for you. ✗ glad with is an error.

Pleased that something is the case.

  1. I'm glad about the way the whole thing worked out.
  2. She's glad about not having to make the drive twice.
  3. He was glad of the company on such a long stretch.
  4. We'd be glad to look at the drawings tomorrow.
  5. I'm just glad that somebody said it out loud.
See also

good at#

Pronunciation
/ɡʊd æt/ · GOOD at · weak /ɡʊd ət/
Русский
умелый в чём-л. — at умение, for полезный, with умеющий обращаться с, to добрый к
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (good at listening) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ good at listen and ✗ good to listening are the two ways this goes wrong. After for, a person or purpose; after with, a category of thing handled.
Pattern
be good at [skill / verb-ing] · be good for [person / purpose] · be good with [people / money / one's hands] · be good to [person] · be good about [verb-ing] · no good at [noun]
Variants
skilled at, strong at, and a natural at are the formal, the sporting, and the admiring versions.
Contrast
This is the four-way split that every learner of English eventually has to memorize. Good at = skilled: good at chemistry. Good for = beneficial: good for your knees; and, in a second sense, good for one more season = lasting. Good with = able to handle a category: good with kids, good with numbers, good with her hands. Good to = kind: She was good to me. A fifth, good about + -ing, means reliably doing something: good about calling ahead. ✗ good in math is an error in American English, though good in a crisis is fixed and correct.

Skilled, beneficial, capable with something, or kind — the preposition decides which.

  1. She's good at explaining the same thing four different ways.
  2. Walking is good for your back and your temper.
  3. He's good with horses and hopeless with paperwork.
  4. That neighbor was good to us when my father was sick.
  5. They're good about letting you know before the water goes off.
See also
bad at, skilled at, expert at, useful for, kind to, at

grateful to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɡreɪtfəl tu/ · GRAYT-full too · weak /ˈɡreɪtfəl tə/
Русский
благодарный кому-л. (to) за что-л. (for)
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a person or institution after to; a noun phrase or gerund after for (grateful for being given a second chance) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (grateful to have it) and a that-clause (grateful that no one was hurt).
Pattern
be grateful to [person] · be grateful for [thing] · be grateful to [person] for [thing / verb-ing] · be grateful to [verb] · be grateful (that) [clause] · I'd be grateful if you would [verb]
Variants
thankful for covers the for half only; indebted to and obliged to cover the to half and are formal.
Contrast
To takes the person, for takes the thing, and both slots can be filled at once: grateful to the neighbors for the ride*. The trap is the double to: I'm grateful to have it uses an infinitive, not the person-slot, so ✗ I'm grateful to the chance is an error where for was needed. Thankful cannot take a person at all: ✗ thankful to the neighbors is odd, though thankful to God is a fixed exception.

Feeling thanks — to whoever gave it, for whatever was given.

  1. We're grateful to the crew that stayed through the night.
  2. She's grateful for every extra week the treatment bought.
  3. I'm grateful to my sister for not saying I told you so.
  4. He'd be grateful to have the report by Friday.
  5. They were grateful that the storm turned before it reached the harbor.

grounded in#

Pronunciation
/ˈɡraʊndɪd ɪn/ · GROWN-did in · weak /ˈɡraʊndəd ən/
Русский
опирающийся на — на доказательства, традицию или практический опыт
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; common in academic writing, policy prose, and criticism
Complement
a noun phrase — a body of evidence, a tradition, a discipline — or a gerund (grounded in doing the work yourself). Never a to-infinitive: ✗ grounded in do the work. No clause follows.
Pattern
be grounded in [evidence / tradition] · firmly / solidly / deeply grounded in · ground [a claim] in [evidence] — the verb · well grounded in [subject] — meaning well trained in it
Variants
rooted in is the organic metaphor and the commonest alternative; based on is the plain word; founded on takes on rather than in; anchored in is the nautical version.
Contrast
The preposition is in, not on, and the metaphor explains why: you are grounded in a medium that surrounds and holds you, but founded on a base you sit atop. ✗ grounded on turns up and reads as a slip. Note the two other groundeds an American hears daily: the punished teenager (grounded for a week), and the aircraft that cannot fly (the fleet was grounded) — neither takes in. And note well-grounded in, which is about a person's training rather than an argument's support.

Resting on something solid — evidence, tradition, or practical experience.

  1. The recommendation is grounded in three years of intake data.
  2. Her teaching is grounded in the years she spent on the floor of a plant.
  3. An argument grounded in nothing but anecdote won't survive review.
  4. The program is grounded in the same theory the state manual uses.
  5. He wanted a plan grounded in what the crews could actually do.
See also
based on, founded on, founded upon, derived from, implicit in, in

guilty of#

Pronunciation
/ˈɡɪlti ʌv/ · GIL-tee uv · weak /ˈɡɪlti əv/
Русский
виновный в чём-л.; guilty about — испытывающий чувство вины из-за мелочи
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; legal in the of frame
Complement
a noun naming an offense, or a gerund naming the act (guilty of falsifying the records) after of — never a to-infinitive. After about, a noun phrase or gerund naming what you feel bad over (guilty about leaving early).
Pattern
be / be found guilty of [offense / verb-ing] · plead guilty to [charge] — the one frame with to · feel guilty about [noun / verb-ing] · guilty as charged · guilty conscience (no preposition)
Variants
culpable of is the formal legal version; responsible for covers the non-criminal sense of having caused something.
Contrast
Guilty of is about what you did; guilty about is about how you feel. He's guilty of tax fraud* is a verdict; He feels guilty about the tax return is a mood. The two do not swap: ✗ guilty about tax fraud would report only remorse, and ✗ feel guilty of leaving early is wrong. A third preposition belongs to one fixed frame: you plead guilty to a charge — ✓ pleaded guilty to two counts, ✗ pleaded guilty of two counts — while the verdict itself keeps of: found guilty of two counts. The opposite of the legal sense is innocent of, also with of. ✗ guilty for is an error, though guilty for having done it appears in speech.

Having committed an offense — or, with about, feeling bad over something minor.

  1. He was found guilty of two counts of mail fraud.
  2. She pleaded guilty to the lesser charge, which is the one frame that uses to.
  3. I'm guilty of forgetting names about four seconds after hearing them.
  4. He feels guilty about not visiting more often.
  5. The report found the agency guilty of nothing worse than sloppiness.
See also
innocent of, ashamed of, responsible for, sorry for, of

happy about#

Pronunciation
/ˈhæpi əˈbaʊt/ · HAP-ee uh-BOWT · weak /ˈhæpi əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈhæpi ˈbaʊt/
Русский
довольный чем-л.; happy with результатом, happy for за кого-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about and with (happy about being asked) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (happy to help) and a that-clause (happy that it's settled).
Pattern
be happy about [situation / verb-ing] · be happy with [thing or result being judged] · be happy for [person] · be happy to [verb] · be happy (that) [clause] · not happy about [noun]
Variants
pleased about / with is the more formal twin; glad about the slightly old-fashioned one; thrilled about the intensified one.
Contrast
Three prepositions, three targets. About = the situation: happy about the move. With = your verdict on a thing you are assessing: happy with the contractor's work — this is the one used in reviews and evaluations. For = on someone else's behalf: happy for you, which says nothing about your own situation. The infinitive frame happy to is a politeness formula in American business writing and means willing. The litotes not happy about is standard understatement for angry. ✗ happy of is an error.

Pleased — about a state of affairs, with a result, or for someone else.

  1. Nobody was happy about the schedule change, but nobody fought it.
  2. Are you happy with how the floors turned out?
  3. She's genuinely happy for him, which is harder than it sounds.
  4. I'd be happy to send over what we have.
  5. Management wasn't happy about finding out from the newspaper.
See also
pleased with, glad about, satisfied with, excited about, about

harmful to#

Pronunciation
/ˈhɑrmfəl tu/ · HARM-full too · weak /ˈhɑrmfəl tə/
Русский
вредный для, причиняющий вред
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the everyday equivalent is bad for
Complement
noun phrase naming the thing damaged — a person, a body part, an environment, an interest. A gerund is possible where a practice is meant (harmful to letting the market clear is awkward; prefer a noun). Never a to-infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
[X] is harmful to [Y] · potentially / directly harmful to [noun] · harmful effects on [noun] — the noun effect takes on, not to
Variants
damaging to, injurious to, and detrimental to are the formal equivalents, all with to. Bad for is the everyday one, with for.
Contrast
Harmful to takes to; bad for takes for. They mean the same thing and their prepositions do not trade: ✗ harmful for your lungs* is a common slip. Watch the related nouns, which take a third preposition: harm to the reef but an effect on the reef and an impact on the reef. ✗ harmful with is an error.

Causing damage or injury to something.

  1. Sustained noise at that level is harmful to hearing.
  2. The runoff is harmful to everything downstream of the plant.
  3. Rumors like that are harmful to morale long after they're disproved.
  4. The additive was found to be harmful to pollinators.
  5. Delay is harmful to the case in ways the client doesn't see yet.
See also
bad at, safe from, vulnerable to, sensitive to, to

hooked on#

Pronunciation
/hʊkt ɑn/ · HOOKT on · weak /hʊkt ən/
Русский
подсевший на что-л., не могущий оторваться — разг., чаще шутливо
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
informal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (hooked on watching the whole series at once) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be / get hooked on [noun / verb-ing] · get [someone] hooked on [noun] · hooked (alone): one episode and I was hooked
Variants
addicted to is the neutral-to-clinical equivalent with a different preposition; into is the mild informal one (she's into birding).
Contrast
Hooked takes on*; addicted takes to. ✗ hooked to* and ✗ addicted on are the two mirror errors, and they are common. Register also separates them: hooked on is almost always light, so using it for a real dependency reads as either euphemism or bravado. Note the unrelated literal hooked to, meaning physically attached (hooked to a monitor), which is correct and is a different word combination entirely.

Unable to leave something alone — usually said lightly.

  1. She got hooked on crossword puzzles during the shutdown.
  2. One taste and the kids were hooked on the stuff.
  3. He's been hooked on those late-night repair videos for a year.
  4. Don't get hooked on checking the numbers every hour.
  5. The patient was hooked to a monitor — a different hooked entirely.
See also
addicted to, obsessed with, crazy about, on

hopeless at#

Pronunciation
/ˈhoʊpləs æt/ · HOHP-luss at · weak /ˈhoʊpləs ət/
Русский
безнадёжно неспособный к чему-л. — разг., чаще о себе
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
informal; usually self-deprecating
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (hopeless at remembering birthdays) — never a to-infinitive. After with, a category handled badly. There is no clause frame.
Pattern
be hopeless at [skill / verb-ing] · be hopeless with [category] · a hopeless case · hopeless alone, of a situation: the traffic is hopeless
Variants
terrible at, useless at, no good at, and bad at are the same idea at different volumes; hopeless is the most theatrical and the least literal.
Contrast
All the "not good at" adjectives take at for skills and with for categories — hopeless at chess, hopeless with computers — exactly parallel to good at / good with. ✗ hopeless in math is an error in American English. Do not confuse the skill sense with the situational one: the situation is hopeless means beyond saving and takes no preposition at all.

Very bad at something — said about oneself far more often than about anyone else.

  1. I'm hopeless at keeping plants alive.
  2. He's hopeless at saying no to a second helping.
  3. She's hopeless with anything that requires a manual.
  4. We're both hopeless at directions and we married each other anyway.
  5. They're hopeless at scheduling and excellent at everything after that.
See also
bad at, terrible at, poor at, good at, at

identical to#

Pronunciation
/aɪˈdɛntɪkəl tu/ · eye-DEN-tih-kull too · weak /aɪˈdɛntɪkəl tə/
Русский
идентичный, в точности такой же, как
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, and never a clause. A free relative works: identical to what we ordered.
Pattern
[X] is identical to [Y] · [X] is identical with [Y] · virtually / nearly / all but identical to [noun] · identical twins (no preposition)
Variants
identical with is the older form, still standard and preferred in logic and mathematics (A is identical with B), while identical to is the ordinary modern choice in American English and the safer default.
Contrast
Identical takes to* or with, never than and never as. ✗ identical than the first* and ✗ identical as the first are both wrong; the as error comes from the same as, which is the one member of this family that does take as. Note the strength ladder: similar to (alike), identical to (indistinguishable), the same as (one and the same, or indistinguishable). Purists once objected to nearly identical on the grounds that identity admits no degrees; it is universal and unremarkable now.

Exactly the same as something else in every detail.

  1. The second invoice was identical to the first, down to the typo.
  2. Her handwriting is nearly identical to her mother's.
  3. The replacement part is identical to the one that failed.
  4. Their proposal is identical with ours in every material respect.
  5. The two buildings are identical to each other from the street.
See also
similar to, equal to, different from, the same as, to

illustrative of#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈlʌstrətɪv ʌv/ · ih-LUSS-truh-tiv uv · weak /əˈlʌstrətəv əv/; the older IL-uh-stray-tiv is chiefly British
Русский
наглядно показывающий что-л., служащий тому примером
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — academic and analytical writing, exhibits in briefs, museum labels; conversation says a good example of
Complement
a noun phrase, often abstract — a tendency, a problem, a style — or a gerund (illustrative of how the office handles complaints is the commoner wh-form). Never a to-infinitive: ✗ illustrative of to handle complaints.
Pattern
be illustrative of [tendency / problem] · merely / particularly illustrative of · by way of illustration — the noun frame · illustrative purposes only — attributive, in the standard American disclaimer
Variants
indicative of points at evidence for a conclusion; representative of claims the example is typical; emblematic of and symptomatic of are the loaded versions; an example of is the plain noun phrase.
Contrast
Illustrative of says a case shows what something is like; indicative of says it is evidence that something is so. A photograph is illustrative of the damage; a rising fever is indicative of infection. Against representative of, which claims the example is typical of a whole, illustrative of* claims only that it makes the point clear. Note the fixed American legal and marketing phrase for illustrative purposes only, which disclaims that the sample binds anyone. ✗ illustrative for and ✗ illustrative to are errors.

Serving as an example that makes something clear.

  1. The 2019 filing is illustrative of the problem, though it isn't the worst one.
  2. His answer was illustrative of how little the training had covered.
  3. These two cases are illustrative of a pattern the auditors flagged.
  4. The exhibit is illustrative of the technique, not a finished piece.
  5. The numbers are illustrative of a trend that started well before the merger.
See also
indicative of, representative of, symptomatic of, suggestive of, of

immune from, immune to#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈmjun frʌm, ɪˈmjun tu/ · ih-MYOON from, ih-MYOON too · weak /əˈmjun frəm/ and /əˈmjun tə/
Русский
невосприимчивый к (to, биол. или по привычке); юр. обладающий иммунитетом от (from)
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral in the medical sense, legal in the from sense (immune from suit, immune from prosecution)
Complement
after either preposition, a noun phrase or a gerund (immune to being embarrassed, immune from having to testify) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ immune to be embarrassed. No clause follows either form.
Pattern
be immune to [disease / influence] · be immune from [suit / prosecution / liability] · grant [someone] immunity from [noun] — the noun frame keeps from · immune system — attributive, no complement
Variants
resistant to is the weaker medical word (it slows the thing down rather than stopping it); exempt from is the administrative twin of immune from; impervious to is the figurative twin of immune to; insulated from is the neutral paraphrase.
Contrast
To for what cannot affect you; from for what cannot reach you. You are immune to* measles and immune to flattery because they no longer work on you; you are immune from prosecution and immune from liability because the law puts them out of reach. The figurative uses divide the same way: immune to criticism means it bounces off, immune from criticism means nobody may deliver it. Both are standard American English, and the noun almost always takes from: immunity from prosecution. ✗ immune of and ✗ immune against are errors, though vaccinated against is correct with the other adjective.

Protected against something — by biology or by habit in the to frame, and by law in the from frame.

  1. Most of the crew is immune to the strain that went through in the spring.
  2. The witness testified only after being made immune from prosecution.
  3. He seems entirely immune to embarrassment.
  4. State agencies are immune from suit in these circumstances.
  5. Nobody in that market was immune to the rate increase.
See also
impervious to, exempt from, vulnerable to, susceptible to, susceptible of, from

impervious to#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈpɜrviəs tu/ · im-PUR-vee-us too · weak /əmˈpɜrviəs tə/
Русский
непроницаемый для (воды); перен. — глухой к доводам, критике, стыду
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; literal in building and materials writing (impervious to water), figurative and slightly literary about people
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (impervious to being told twice) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ impervious to be told. No clause follows.
Pattern
be impervious to [substance / influence] · utterly / seemingly / cheerfully impervious to · an impervious surface — attributive, and a term of art in stormwater ordinances
Variants
immune to is the near twin in the figurative use; resistant to is the weaker one; proof against is the older phrase and lives on in compounds (waterproof, bulletproof, fireproof); insensible to is the literary version.
Contrast
Against immune to, which suggests a defense that was acquired, impervious to suggests a material that never let anything through in the first place — which is why it is the crueler word about people: impervious to argument says nothing gets in. Note the American municipal use, where impervious surface means pavement and roof area that sheds rainwater, and the stormwater fee is charged by the square foot of it. ✗ impervious of and ✗ impervious from are errors.

Letting nothing through — water, in the literal sense; argument, criticism, or shame in the figurative one.

  1. The liner is impervious to everything the plant runs through it.
  2. He is impervious to hints and always has been.
  3. The finish is impervious to road salt for about four winters.
  4. The committee proved impervious to every argument the neighbors made.
  5. She's impervious to the cold in a way the rest of us envy.
See also
immune from, immune to, oblivious to, indifferent to, vulnerable to, to

implicit in#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈplɪsɪt ɪn/ · im-PLISS-it in · weak /əmˈplɪsət ən/
Русский
подразумеваемый в чём-л. — присутствующий, но прямо не сказанный
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; the natural home is analysis — of texts, contracts, arguments, and policies
Complement
a noun phrase naming the thing that contains the unstated element — a text, a claim, a bargain, a design. A gerund works where the container is an action (implicit in asking for a raise), and a to-infinitive is impossible: ✗ implicit in to ask.
Pattern
be implicit in [text / claim / arrangement] · implicit in [noun] is [the thing implied] — the inverted frame, which is how the phrase usually appears · implicit bias / implicit consent — attributive, no preposition
Variants
inherent in is the near neighbor and means built into the nature of the thing rather than left unsaid; tacit covers the same ground as an attributive adjective (a tacit agreement); understood and unspoken are the plain words.
Contrast
Implicit in means present but unstated; inherent in means part of the thing's nature. A promise of good faith is implicit in the contract because nobody wrote it down; risk is inherent in the business because you cannot have the one without the other. The direct opposite is explicit*, which takes no preposition in this sense (the contract is explicit), and implicit also has an unrelated older sense of "unquestioning," as in implicit trust, where again no in follows. ✗ implicit to is an error, and ✗ implicit of is not English.

Contained in something without being said outright.

  1. Implicit in the offer is an assumption that the roof gets replaced.
  2. A duty of good faith is implicit in every contract in this state.
  3. The criticism was implicit in the way she read the numbers aloud.
  4. There's a threat implicit in the last paragraph, whatever they claim.
  5. Consent is implicit in using the parking lot, according to the sign.
See also
inherent in, intrinsic to, integral to, indicative of, in

impressed with#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈprɛst wɪθ/ · im-PREST with · weak /ɪmˈprɛst wəθ/
Русский
впечатлённый чем-л., приятно удивлённый
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after with and by (impressed by how quickly they turned it around) — never a to-infinitive. A wh-clause is common (impressed with how she handled it), and a that-clause attaches to the bare adjective (impressed that anyone noticed).
Pattern
be impressed with [person / thing] · be impressed by [the thing that impressed you] · be impressed (that) [clause] · impress [something] on [someone] — a different verb frame · not impressed
Variants
impressed with and impressed by are interchangeable in almost every sentence; by is a shade more formal and points harder at the cause. Taken with is the warmer version; struck by the more sudden one.
Contrast
This is the one with / by pair in the file where the difference is nearly nil — unlike annoyed with / annoyed by or bored with / bored by, where the split is real, impressed with the design and impressed by the design mean the same thing. Keep both apart from impress on, the verb frame meaning to make someone understand: impress on them that the deadline is real. ✗ impressed of and ✗ impressed about are errors, and the British not impressed with is identical to the American use.

Admiring something because it was better than expected.

  1. The committee was impressed with the second candidate.
  2. I was impressed by how fast the crew cleared the lot.
  3. She wasn't impressed with the excuse and said so.
  4. Everyone was impressed that he'd fixed it with a pocketknife.
  5. We came away impressed with the whole operation.
See also

incapable of#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈkeɪpəbəl ʌv/ · in-KAY-puh-bull uv · weak /ɪnˈkeɪpəbəl əv/
Русский
неспособный на что-л., не имеющий сил или характера сделать
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (incapable of sitting still) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ incapable to do it is the error, exactly parallel to ✗ capable to do it; the negatives of able to are unable to and not able to, both with the infinitive.
Pattern
be incapable of [noun / verb-ing] · seemingly / apparently incapable of [verb-ing] · incapable of [abstract noun]: incapable of malice
Variants
unable to + infinitive is the everyday negative with the opposite structure; not up to + -ing is the informal one.
Contrast
Incapable of + -ing versus unable to + verb. The two negatives of ability take opposite complements and cannot borrow: ✓ incapable of finishing*, ✓ unable to finish, ✗ incapable to finish, ✗ unable of finishing. There is also a difference of scope: unable to finish usually means blocked in this instance, while incapable of finishing impugns the person's capacity in general — which is why the second one insults and the first does not.

Lacking the power, skill, or character to do something.

  1. He seems incapable of leaving a message under two minutes.
  2. The old boiler is incapable of heating the third floor.
  3. She's incapable of passing a bookstore without going in.
  4. The court found him incapable of managing his own affairs.
  5. They were incapable of agreeing on even the meeting time.
