incident to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪnsɪdənt tu/ · IN-suh-dunt too · weak /ˈɪnsədənt tə/
Русский
вытекающий из чего-л. как правовое следствие — юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal; the American reader meets it in one place above all — a search incident to arrest — and otherwise in deeds, employment agreements, and tax rules
Complement
a noun phrase naming the principal thing — an arrest, an office, an ownership interest, a transaction. A gerund appears in drafting (expenses incident to closing the sale), and a to-infinitive is impossible: ✗ incident to close the sale.
Pattern
[thing] incident to [principal thing] · a search incident to [arrest] — the constitutional doctrine · rights and duties incident to [office / ownership] · incident to employment — the workers' compensation phrase
Variants
incidental to is the everyday twin and is the form to use for minor accompanying things; appurtenant to is the property-law word for rights attached to land; attendant on is the literary version.
Contrast
Incident to says a thing belongs to another as one of its normal legal consequences; incidental to says it merely happens alongside and is minor. Powers incident to the office of trustee come with the office as of right; the printing costs incidental to the filing are trivia. American lawyers keep the pair apart deliberately, and outside law the shorter form has almost entirely won. Do not read incident* here as the noun meaning "an event" — different word, and it takes no preposition. ✗ incident of means something else again (an incident of ownership, in estate tax, is a right that comes with it).

Arising as a normal legal consequence of something else, and belonging to it as of right.

  1. Officers may search the passenger compartment incident to a lawful arrest.
  2. The powers incident to the office pass to whoever holds it next.
  3. Costs incident to recording the deed are the buyer's.
  4. Injuries incident to employment are covered without regard to fault.
  5. Any right incident to the policy has to be listed on the schedule.
See also
incidental to, appurtenant to, ancillary to, concomitant with, to

incidental to#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪnsɪˈdɛntəl tu/ · in-suh-DEN-tul too · weak /ˌɪnsəˈdɛntəl tə/, with a flapped t: "in-suh-DEN-nul-tuh"
Русский
побочный при чём-л. — сопутствующий главному и малозначительный рядом с ним
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; ordinary in expense reports (incidental expenses), contracts, and analytical prose
Complement
a noun phrase naming the main thing, or a gerund (incidental to running the shop) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ incidental to run the shop.
Pattern
be incidental to [main activity] · [cost / effect] incidental to [noun] · merely / purely incidental to · incidentals — the noun, meaning small extra costs, with no preposition
Variants
ancillary to is the formal twin for supporting services; incident to is the legal form for consequences that come as of right; secondary to ranks the two things; peripheral to stresses the edge.
Contrast
Incidental to means minor and merely accompanying, and it is the word to reach for outside law. Against incident to, which claims a legal connection, incidental to* claims no connection beyond happening alongside. Against ancillary to, which supports the main thing on purpose, incidental to implies nobody planned it. In per-diem rules the frozen American phrase is meals and incidental expenses — the noun incidentals is what a hotel bill calls the minibar. ✗ incidental of and ✗ incidental with are errors.

Happening alongside the main thing and of small importance next to it.

  1. The travel costs are incidental to the grant and capped at eight percent.
  2. Whatever damage the tree did was incidental to the storm, not the crew.
  3. Filing is incidental to her actual job, which is answering the phone.
  4. The gift shop is incidental to the museum's mission, if lucrative.
  5. Any benefit to the neighbors was incidental to a project built for the port.
See also
incident to, ancillary to, concomitant with, peculiar to, to

incumbent on, incumbent upon#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈkʌmbənt ɑn, ɪnˈkʌmbənt əˈpɑn/ · in-KUM-bunt on, in-KUM-bunt uh-PON · weak /ənˈkʌmbənt ən/
Русский
лежащий на ком-л. как обязанность — книжн., в обороте it is incumbent on X to
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — editorials, opinions, and official correspondence; the whole phrase is a set piece and upon is at least as common as on
Complement
a noun phrase or pronoun naming the person under the duty — never the duty itself. The duty goes in a to-infinitive at the end of the sentence, which makes this a two-part frame: it is incumbent on the board to review the policy. ✗ incumbent on reviewing the policy is an error, and so is ✗ the board is incumbent on reviewing.
Pattern
it is incumbent on [person] to [verb] — nearly the only frame in live use · it is incumbent upon us to [verb] · the duty incumbent on [person]
Variants
the burden is on [someone] to [verb] is the plain equivalent; obligated to and required to say the same thing about a person directly; behooves is the archaic verb that fills the same slot (it behooves us to check).
Contrast
The frame is impersonal and the infinitive is obligatory, which is what makes this entry unusual in a file about gerunds: the on takes the person, and the duty must be an infinitive. Note that the unrelated noun incumbent — the officeholder currently in the job — takes no preposition and is far commoner in American political writing; the incumbent is running again has nothing to do with this pattern. ✗ incumbent to and ✗ incumbent for are errors.

Resting on someone as a duty — in a fixed frame with it and an infinitive.

  1. It is incumbent on the seller to disclose what she knows about the roof.
  2. It is incumbent upon every member to read the minutes before the vote.
  3. The duty incumbent on a trustee does not end when the money runs out.
  4. It's incumbent on us to say plainly what the study did not find.
  5. It was incumbent upon the agency to publish the rule before enforcing it.
See also
obligated to, obliged to, binding on, binding upon, responsible for, answerable to, on

independent of#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪndɪˈpɛndənt ʌv/ · in-dih-PEN-dunt uv · weak /ˌɪndɪˈpɛndənt əv/
Русский
независимый от — не подконтрольный, не содержащийся и не определяемый чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun; a gerund is possible where an activity is meant (independent of needing outside funding) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be independent of [noun] · be independent from [a former parent body] · independently of [noun] — the adverb · financially independent
Variants
independent from is used, and it is idiomatic where separation from a former controller is meant (independent from Spain since 1821). For the ordinary "not determined by" sense, of is the standard American choice: independent of the outcome.
Contrast
Independent of is the default; independent from is for secession. A variable is independent of* another; a colony becomes independent from an empire. The negative side of the same word takes on, not of: ✓ dependent on, ✗ dependent of — so the pair dependent on / independent of is one of the small number of places in English where a prefix changes the preposition. The adverb keeps the of: arrived at independently of one another.

Not controlled by, supported by, or determined by something else.

  1. The audit is independent of both the board and the union.
  2. Two labs reached the same figure independently of each other.
  3. She's been financially independent of her parents since twenty.
  4. The result is independent of how the sample was ordered.
  5. The territory became independent from the mainland administration in 1976.
See also
dependent on, free of, reliant on, of

indicative of#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈdɪkətɪv ʌv/ · in-DIK-uh-tiv uv · weak /ənˈdɪkətəv əv/
Русский
свидетельствующий о, указывающий на
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; standard in clinical notes, audit findings, and analytical journalism, where the plain phrase would be a sign of
Complement
a noun phrase, or a gerund where the sign points to an activity (indicative of having been moved) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ indicative of to be moved. A wh-clause is common and correct: indicative of what the crew found.
Pattern
be indicative of [condition / conclusion] · not necessarily indicative of [noun] — the hedged frame, and a standing phrase in American investment disclaimers (past performance is not indicative of future results) · the indicative mood — the grammar term, unrelated
Variants
a sign of and evidence of are the plain noun phrases; suggestive of is weaker; symptomatic of is the medical one; diagnostic of is the strongest — it settles the question.
A scale of strength runs through this family
suggestive of hints, indicative of points, symptomatic of names a condition by its signs, and diagnostic of proves. Against illustrative of, which offers an example, indicative of offers evidence. Do not confuse the adjective with the grammatical indicative (the mood of ordinary statements), which never takes of. ✗ indicative to and ✗ indicative for are errors.

Serving as a sign that something is the case.

  1. The wear pattern is indicative of a wheel that was never aligned.
  2. Two late filings are not indicative of fraud by themselves.
  3. Her tone was indicative of how the rest of the meeting would go.
  4. Past performance is not indicative of future results.
  5. Soil staining indicative of an old leak turned up under the slab.
See also
suggestive of, symptomatic of, illustrative of, predictive of, of

indifferent to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈdɪfərənt tu/ · in-DIF-runt too · weak /ɪnˈdɪfərənt tə/
Русский
равнодушный к, безразличный
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun; a gerund where an activity is meant (indifferent to being noticed) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be indifferent to [noun / verb-ing] · seemingly / utterly indifferent to [noun] · a matter of indifference to [someone] — the noun keeps to
Variants
unmoved by, unconcerned about, and apathetic about / toward are the neighbors; couldn't care less about is the informal one.
Contrast
Indifferent to takes to, not about or by. ✗ indifferent about the outcome* is a common slip; the word that takes about is unconcerned. Note the second, older sense of indifferent meaning mediocre (an indifferent meal), where no preposition appears at all — this sense is now chiefly literary but still turns up in reviews.

Having no interest in something, and no feeling either way about it.

  1. He is genuinely indifferent to what anyone thinks of the house.
  2. The cat was indifferent to the entire commotion.
  3. Voters were indifferent to both candidates and stayed home.
  4. She's indifferent to prizes, which is why she keeps winning them.
  5. The market has been indifferent to the announcement all week.
See also
interested in, bored with, sensitive to, to

inferior to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈfɪriər tu/ · in-FEER-ee-ur too · weak /ɪnˈfɪriər tə/
Русский
уступающий чему-л. — ниже по качеству, рангу или значимости
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
[X] is inferior to [Y] · inferior in [respect] to [noun] · vastly / markedly inferior to [noun] · an inferior [noun] — attributive, no preposition
Variants
worse than is the everyday equivalent and takes than, which is exactly the confusion this entry exists to prevent. Not a patch on is the informal British one.
Contrast
Inferior takes to*, never than. ✗ inferior than* is one of the most persistent errors in academic English, because the adjective is comparative in meaning even though it is not comparative in form. The same rule governs superior to, senior to, junior to, prior to, preferable to, and anterior to — all Latin comparatives, all with to, all rejecting than. Note also that inferior cannot be intensified with more: ✗ more inferior to is redundant.

Lower in quality, rank, or importance than something else.

  1. The replacement fabric is noticeably inferior to the original.
  2. He never felt inferior to anyone in the room, which was half the problem.
  3. The second edition is inferior to the first in every way but the index.
  4. These parts are inferior in finish to what we ordered.
  5. Nothing about the design is inferior to the competition; it's just late.
See also
superior to, equal to, different from, than, to

inherent in#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈhɪrənt ɪn/ · in-HEER-unt in · weak /ənˈhɪrənt ən/; the variant in-HAIR-unt is equally American
Русский
присущий чему-л. по природе, неотделимый от
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; standard in law, risk writing, and philosophy
Complement
a noun phrase naming the thing whose nature contains the quality — inherent in the design, inherent in any partnership. A gerund is available where the container is an activity (inherent in running a plant this old), and a to-infinitive is impossible: ✗ inherent in to run a plant.
Pattern
be inherent in [thing / activity] · risks inherent in [noun] — the commonest collocation by a wide margin · inherent [noun] — attributive (an inherent conflict, inherent powers) · inhere in [noun] — the rare verb
Variants
intrinsic to is the near twin and takes to; built into is the plain modern phrase; of the essence of is the philosopher's formula; native to covers the biological case.
Contrast
Inherent takes in; intrinsic to takes to, and this is the pair most often crossed. ✗ inherent to the design* is now common in American technical writing and is still marked as an error by careful editors; write inherent in the design or intrinsic to the design. Against implicit in, which is about what was left unsaid, inherent in is about what could not be removed: the risk inherent in the work would exist even if everyone spelled it out. ✗ inherent of is not English.

Belonging to the nature of a thing, so that it cannot be separated from it.

  1. There's a conflict inherent in letting the vendor audit its own invoices.
  2. The risks inherent in the procedure were explained twice.
  3. Delay is inherent in any process that needs three signatures.
  4. Courts have powers inherent in the judicial function itself.
  5. The instability is inherent in the design, not a manufacturing defect.
See also
intrinsic to, implicit in, integral to, endemic to, in

innocent of#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪnəsənt ʌv/ · IN-uh-sunt uv · weak /ˈɪnəsənt əv/
Русский
невиновный в чём-л.; шутл. — начисто лишённый какого-л. качества
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal in the legal sense; literary in the extended one
Complement
a noun naming an offense, or a gerund naming the act (innocent of having any part in it) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be innocent of [offense / verb-ing] · be found innocent of [charge] · innocent of [any knowledge / all irony] — the extended sense · presumed innocent (no preposition)
Variants
not guilty of is the everyday and, strictly, the legally accurate phrase; cleared of is what happens after a verdict; blameless in covers the non-criminal sense.
Contrast
Innocent of takes of, matching guilty of. The legal note is worth knowing: American juries return not guilty*, not innocent, so found innocent of is journalism rather than law. The extended sense means entirely lacking something and is faintly ironic: a paragraph innocent of punctuation has none. ✗ innocent from and ✗ innocent about are errors.

Not guilty of an offense — or, playfully, entirely without some quality.

  1. He maintained he was innocent of every charge on the indictment.
  2. She was innocent of any involvement in the second transfer.
  3. The jury found him not guilty; the paper said innocent of all counts.
  4. His memo was innocent of any acquaintance with the actual budget.
  5. They were innocent of the whole scheme until the subpoena arrived.
See also
guilty of, free of, responsible for, of

integral to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪntɪɡrəl tu/ · IN-tuh-grul too; the stress-shifted in-TEG-rul is also standard American · weak /ˈɪntəɡrəl tə/
Русский
составляющий неотъемлемую часть чего-л., а не добавку к нему
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; ordinary in business and technical writing about how parts fit into wholes
Complement
a noun phrase naming the whole, or a gerund naming the activity (integral to keeping the line running) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ integral to keep the line running.
Pattern
be integral to [whole / process] · absolutely / utterly integral to · an integral part of [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to of · integral [noun] — attributive, as in integral memory
Variants
essential to and central to are the everyday equivalents; crucial to and vital to raise the temperature; part and parcel of is the idiom; intrinsic to is about nature rather than function.
Contrast
Adjective to, noun of. ✓ integral to the process, ✓ an integral part of the process, ✗ an integral part to the process. Against essential to, which says the whole fails without it, integral to says the thing is a built-in part of the whole — the difference is between necessity and membership, though in practice the two are used interchangeably. Against intrinsic to, note that integral is about structure (it is one of the parts) and intrinsic about nature (it is of the essence). ✗ integral for and ✗ integral in are errors.

Forming a necessary part of a larger whole rather than an addition to it.

  1. The loading dock is integral to how the store gets restocked.
  2. Her role became integral to the project about a month in.
  3. Testing is integral to the release process, not a step you can skip.
  4. The porch is integral to the structure and can't simply be removed.
  5. Volunteers are integral to keeping the clinic open on Saturdays.
See also
essential to, intrinsic to, inherent in, ancillary to, to

intent on#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈtɛnt ɑn/ · in-TENT on · weak /ɪnˈtɛnt ən/
Русский
твёрдо намеренный сделать что-л., поглощённый этим — после on герундий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (intent on finishing before dark) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ intent to finish is wrong as an adjective phrase, though the noun intent does take an infinitive: with intent to defraud, which is exactly where the confusion comes from.
Pattern
be intent on [noun / verb-ing] · be intent upon [noun / verb-ing] · seemingly intent on [verb-ing] · with intent to [verb] — the noun frame
Variants
intent upon is the formal equivalent. Bent on is the near-identical informal one and always implies a bad outcome; determined to takes an infinitive instead.
Contrast
Intent on + -ing versus determined to + verb. Same meaning, opposite structure: ✓ intent on winning*, ✓ determined to win, ✗ intent to win as an adjective. Note the legal noun phrase with intent to kill, where intent is a noun and takes an infinitive correctly — the adjective and the noun are spelled the same and take different complements, which is why this pair produces so many mistakes. Bent on is the version reserved for destructive purposes: bent on revenge.

Fixed on doing something, with attention that excludes everything else.

  1. She was intent on getting the whole roof done in one weekend.
  2. He seemed intent on proving a point nobody had disputed.
  3. The department is intent on keeping the case out of the papers.
  4. They were intent upon the map and didn't hear the door.
  5. He was charged with assault with intent to rob — the noun frame.
See also
committed to, serious about, reluctant to, obsessed with, on

interested in#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪntrəstɪd ɪn/ · IN-truss-tid in · weak /ˈɪntrəstɪd ən/; the careful four-syllable /ˈɪntərəstɪd/ also occurs
Русский
интересующийся чем-л., заинтересованный в
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (interested in buying) — never a to-infinitive after in. ✗ interested in to buy and ✗ interested to buy for this meaning are both errors. The adjective does take a bare to-infinitive in a different sense — interested to hear, interested to see — meaning curious to learn, and that frame works only with verbs of perceiving and learning.
Pattern
be interested in [noun / verb-ing] · be interested in [someone] -ing · be interested to [hear / know / see / learn] · take an interest in [noun] · an interest in [noun] — the noun keeps in
Variants
into is the casual American version (he's into woodworking); keen on the British one; curious about the neighboring one.
Contrast
The single highest-frequency error with this adjective is ✗ I am interested to buy your car. Write interested in buying. The infinitive frame is real but narrow: it takes verbs of finding out — I'd be interested to hear what she says — and it means "curious", not "wanting to acquire". Compare interested in (wanting, engaged with) against curious about (wondering) and concerned with (dealing with). ✗ interested about is an error, though interested in and interested by differ the usual way, by pointing at the cause.

Wanting to know about something, or wanting to have or do it.

  1. She's interested in nineteenth-century glass, the more chipped the better.
  2. We'd be interested in buying the lot next door if it ever comes up.
  3. He's not interested in arguing about it again.
  4. I'd be interested to hear how the meeting went.
  5. Anyone interested in volunteering should email the office.
See also

intrinsic to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈtrɪnzɪk tu/ · in-TRIN-zik too; also in-TRIN-sik · weak /ənˈtrɪnzək tə/
Русский
внутренне присущий чему-л., идущий от самой его сути
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal — philosophy, biology, economics, and careful technical writing
Complement
a noun phrase naming the thing whose nature includes the property, or a gerund (intrinsic to being a small landlord) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ intrinsic to be a landlord.
Pattern
be intrinsic to [thing / role] · intrinsic [noun] — attributive (intrinsic value, intrinsic motivation, intrinsic muscles) · intrinsically [adjective] — the adverb
Variants
inherent in is the near twin with a different preposition; essential to is the plainer word; built into is the everyday phrase; of its very nature is the formal paraphrase. The antonym extrinsic takes to as well.
Contrast
Intrinsic to versus inherent in is a preposition split with no meaning behind it — the two adjectives cover nearly the same ground and simply take different prepositions, which is exactly why writers cross them. Against integral to, which is about being one of the parts, intrinsic to* is about belonging to the nature of the thing. In finance the attributive use is a term of art: intrinsic value is what the thing is worth apart from what the market says. ✗ intrinsic in is a slip, though it appears; ✗ intrinsic of is not English.

Belonging to the essential nature of something rather than coming from outside it.

  1. Curiosity is intrinsic to the job; the paperwork is not.
  2. Some risk is intrinsic to lending, however good the underwriting.
  3. The delay is intrinsic to the process, not a sign anybody is slacking.
  4. Rewards intrinsic to the work keep people longer than bonuses do.
  5. That quality is intrinsic to the material and survives any finish.
See also
inherent in, integral to, essential to, peculiar to, to

involved in#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈvɑlvd ɪn/ · in-VOLVD in · weak /ɪnˈvɑlvd ən/
Русский
участвующий в, замешанный в
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after in and with (involved in running the campaign) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ involved to run it is an error.
Pattern
be involved in [activity / verb-ing] · be involved with [person / organization] · get involved in [situation] · involve [someone] in [something] — the verb · deeply / heavily involved in [noun]
Variants
engaged in is the formal equivalent for activities; mixed up in is the disapproving informal one; caught up in is the passive one.
Contrast
Involved in an activity; involved with a person or an organization. Involved in the merger* describes what you were doing; involved with the company describes an ongoing connection; and involved with someone is a standard American euphemism for a romantic relationship, which is why the two prepositions are worth keeping apart. Note the verb frame involve someone in something and the noun frame involvement in / with, which follow the same split. ✗ involved on is an error.

Taking part in something, or connected with someone or something.

  1. Three departments were involved in drafting the policy.
  2. She's been involved with the food bank since it opened.
  3. He got involved in a dispute that had nothing to do with him.
  4. They were involved in running the whole festival for eleven years.
  5. Nobody wanted to be involved with that firm after the settlement.

isomorphic to#

Pronunciation
/ˌaɪsəˈmɔrfɪk tu/ · eye-suh-MOR-fik too · weak /ˌaɪsəˈmɔrfək tə/
Русский
изоморфный чему-л. — мат., имеющий в точности ту же структуру
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical — mathematics above all (group theory, graph theory), then linguistics, crystallography, and, as a borrowed metaphor, organizational theory
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other structure — a group, a graph, a system — never a gerund and never a clause. The two things compared must be structures of the same kind.
Pattern
[structure] is isomorphic to [structure] · isomorphic with [noun] — the variant, commoner outside mathematics · an isomorphism between [X] and [Y] — the noun frame, with between · isomorphic [noun] — attributive
Variants
isomorphic with is standard in the looser social-science use; homomorphic to is the weaker mathematical relation; structurally identical to is the paraphrase; congruent to is geometry's version of the same instinct.
Contrast
In mathematics isomorphic to is exact and technical: two structures are isomorphic when a one-to-one mapping preserves all the relevant operations, which is a far stronger claim than similarity. The borrowed use in organizational theory (the new agency is isomorphic to the one it replaced) means only that the shapes match, and specialists find it loose. Against analogous to, which says reasoning carries over, isomorphic to says the structures match exactly. Note the noun's different preposition: an isomorphism between two groups, not ✗ of them. ✗ isomorphic of is an error.

Having exactly the same structure as something else, so that one can be mapped onto the other point for point.

  1. That group is isomorphic to the integers under addition.
  2. Any two cyclic groups of the same order are isomorphic to each other.
  3. The two graphs look different but are isomorphic to one another.
  4. The new org chart is isomorphic to the one they abandoned in 2016.
  5. He showed the puzzle is isomorphic to a problem already solved in 1974.
See also
congruent to, congruent with, analogous to, equivalent to, identical to, to

jealous of#

Pronunciation
/ˈdʒɛləs ʌv/ · JEL-uss uv · weak /ˈdʒɛləs əv/
Русский
завидующий кому-л.; ревниво оберегающий своё
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (jealous of having to share the office) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be jealous of [person / thing] · be jealous of [someone's] [possession or advantage] · jealously guard [something] · jealous of [something one protects]: jealous of her privacy
Variants
envious of is the formal twin for the wanting sense only; possessive of and protective of cover the guarding sense.
Contrast
Jealous does two jobs with one preposition. Jealous of her sister's job* wants what someone else has — that is envious of territory. Jealous of her own time guards what it already has, and envious cannot mean that. A third use covers romantic insecurity: jealous of the new coworker can mean either wanting his position or fearing he is a rival, and only context separates them. ✗ jealous about and ✗ jealous with are errors; the noun keeps of: jealousy of his success.

Wanting what someone else has — or fiercely protective of something you have.

  1. He's jealous of anyone who gets to work from home.
  2. She's jealous of her Sundays and won't schedule anything.
  3. The younger one is jealous of every minute the baby takes.
  4. Small firms are jealous of their independence.
  5. I'm jealous of the way he can sleep through anything.
See also

keen on#

Pronunciation
/kin ɑn/ · KEEN on · weak /kin ən/
Русский
увлечённый чем-л. — в амер. англ. чаще в отрицании: not keen on
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral in British English; in American English it reads as mildly British or bookish, and survives mainly in the negative
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after on (keen on hiking) — never a to-infinitive after on. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (keen to start), which is the frame American writers reach for when they use the word at all.
Pattern
be keen on [noun / verb-ing] · be keen to [verb] · not too keen on [noun] · keen alone, meaning sharp or intense: a keen eye, keen interest
Variants
into, crazy about, and enthusiastic about are the American everyday equivalents; eager to replaces keen to.
Contrast
Keen on + -ing versus keen to + verb is the same split as eager for* / eager to: on takes the thing, to takes the action. The American usage note matters here — a sentence like She's keen on the idea is understood everywhere but sounds imported in the United States, while the negative I'm not too keen on that is thoroughly naturalized American understatement. ✗ keen about and ✗ keen of are errors; the intensifier sense (a keen sense of smell) takes no preposition.

Enthusiastic about something — most often heard, in America, in the negative.

  1. I'm not too keen on driving all that way for one meeting.
  2. He's keen on anything involving a boat.
  3. The board wasn't keen on the timing.
  4. She's keen to get started before the permits expire.
  5. They were keen on the location and cool on the price.
See also
enthusiastic about, crazy about, eager for, fond of, on

kind to#

Pronunciation
/kaɪnd tu/ · KYND too · weak /kaɪnd tə/
Русский
добрый, внимательный к кому-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person or animal after to — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The judgment frame takes of plus a doer plus an infinitive: It was kind of you to call.
Pattern
be kind to [person / animal] · it is kind of [someone] to [verb] · be kind enough to [verb] · kind about [something] — tolerant regarding · kind to [a surface or a body part]: kind to your knees
Variants
nice to, good to, and decent to are the everyday equivalents; considerate of and thoughtful toward are the formal ones.
Contrast
To names who receives the kindness; of names who supplied it. Kind to me* and kind of you are both correct and mean different things, and the second is a fixed politeness frame in American English: That's kind of you. ✗ kind with is an error, which is worth flagging because gentle with and patient with sit right next door and take with. The extended sense — a shampoo that's kind to color-treated hair — keeps to.

Treating someone with warmth and consideration.

  1. She was kind to every nervous first-year who came through the door.
  2. Be kind to the guy at the counter; it's his second day.
  3. It was kind of you to wait.
  4. The years have been kind to that house.
  5. The new surface is kind to runners' knees.

known for#

Pronunciation
/noʊn fɔr/ · NOHN for · weak /noʊn fər/
Русский
известный чем-л.; known as — как кто-л., known to — кому-л. известный
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after for (known for showing up early) — never a to-infinitive after for. After as, a noun naming a role or a name (known as the Iron Range). After to, a person who knows it. The passive known to + infinitive (known to happen) is a separate frame meaning reported to.
Pattern
be known for [quality / verb-ing] · be known as [role / name] · be known to [person] · be known to [verb] · make [something] known to [someone] · little known outside [place]
Variants
famous for is the strong version; notorious for the negative one; renowned for the formal one; recognized as the credential version.
Contrast
Four prepositions, four jobs, and this is one of the busiest words in the file. For = what you do or have: known for her sourdough. As = what you are called or counted as: known as Big Jim, known as a hard grader. To + person = who knows it: known to the police. To + infinitive = reported to do it: bears are known to open car doors. Substituting one for another goes wrong immediately: ✗ known as her sourdough. ✗ known of is an error, though unknown to and known by (with an agent) are both correct.

Recognized by other people — for something, as something, or to someone.

  1. The neighborhood is known for its stubborn holdout houses.
  2. He's known as the only person who can fix that machine.
  3. The car was already known to the county sheriff.
  4. Storms like that are known to strand traffic for hours.
  5. She's known for returning calls the same day, which is rarer than it should be.
See also
famous for, notorious for, familiar with, as, for

lacking in#

Pronunciation
/ˈlækɪŋ ɪn/ · LAK-ing in · weak /ˈlækɪŋ ən/
Русский
лишённый качества, которого следовало бы ожидать
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
an abstract quality after in (lacking in imagination) — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive. The bare participle also takes a direct object with no preposition (lacking any evidence), and the plain verb lack takes a direct object and never a preposition.
Pattern
be lacking in [quality] · be lacking [noun] · be sadly / conspicuously lacking in [quality] · lack [something] — the verb, with no preposition · a lack of [noun] — the noun, with of
Variants
deficient in is the technical version; short on and low on the informal ones; devoid of and wanting in the formal ones.
Contrast
The three related forms take three different structures, and this is the trap. ✓ The plan lacks detail (verb, no preposition — ✗ lacks of detail). ✓ The plan is lacking in detail (adjective, in). ✓ There is a lack of detail (noun, of). Learners who have met the noun first tend to write ✗ lacks of detail, which is the single commonest error involving this word; see the zero-preposition entry for lack. Lacking in prefers abstract qualities, so lacking in courage is natural and lacking in a hammer is not.

Short of some quality that ought to be there.

  1. The proposal is not lacking in ambition, only in numbers.
  2. His apology was conspicuously lacking in any actual apology.
  3. The building is lacking in the kind of light the studio needs.
  4. She's never been lacking in nerve.
  5. The report is lacking several attachments referenced in the index.
See also
lack, short of, absent from, free of, in

late for#

Pronunciation
/leɪt fɔr/ · LAYT for · weak /leɪt fər/
Русский
опоздавший на что-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun naming an appointment or event after for (late for the meeting) — never a gerund after for and never a to-infinitive. After in, a gerund naming what was done late (late in filing), which is the formal frame. After with, the thing owed (late with the rent).
Pattern
be late for [event] · be late to [event or place] · be late in [verb-ing] · be late with [something owed] · run late · late alone, of time: it's getting late
Variants
late to is increasingly common in American English before an event, and late to the party is a fixed idiom. Behind on covers the "late with" sense: behind on the rent.
Contrast
For takes the event you failed to reach on time; with takes the thing you failed to hand over; in takes the action you were slow to perform. Late for the hearing*, late with the filing, late in filing — three frames, three grammatical shapes. The opposite is on time for or punctual for, both with for; there is no ✗ early for / late of symmetry, since early for is correct and late of means something else entirely (Ann Reilly, late of Cleveland is the obituary formula for "formerly of").

Arriving or acting after the time you were supposed to.

  1. She was late for the interview and got the job anyway.
  2. He's late with the rent for the third month running.
  3. The district was late in notifying families about the closure.
  4. Don't be late to your own party.
  5. I'm always late for things that start on the half hour.
See also
punctual for, ready for, prepared for, for

liable for, liable to#

Pronunciation
/ˈlaɪəbəl fɔr, ˈlaɪəbəl tu/ · LY-uh-bul for, LY-uh-bul too · weak /ˈlaɪəbəl fər/ and /ˈlaɪəbəl tə/; two syllables, LY-bul, is a common casual reduction
Русский
юр. отвечающий за (for) перед кем-л. (to); разг. — склонный, того и гляди сделает
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal in the for frame, neutral to informal in the "likely" frame — and the informal one is thoroughly American
Complement
after for, a noun phrase naming the harm or debt you must answer for (liable for the damages) — never a gerund in the legal sense. After to, either a noun naming the person owed or a person harmed (liable to the buyer), or a bare infinitive in the entirely separate "likely to" sense (liable to break). The infinitive frame is the informal one: ✗ liable to breaking.
Pattern
be liable for [damages / debt] · be liable to [person] for [amount] · be liable to [verb] — the "likely" sense · hold [someone] liable for [noun] · jointly and severally liable for — the standard American formula
Variants
responsible for is the everyday equivalent of the legal sense; on the hook for is the slang one; apt to and likely to replace the informal sense; answerable for is the formal one.
Contrast
Three frames, three meanings. Liable for the repairs names what you owe. Liable to the landlord names whom you owe it to. Liable to freeze tonight means likely, has nothing to do with law, and is only ever followed by a bare verb — a use that strikes British ears as folksy and is unremarkable in America. There is also the older liable to + noun meaning "exposed to" (liable to prosecution, liable to a fine), which survives in statutes. ✗ liable of is an error, and ✗ liable to pay in the legal sense should be liable for payment unless you mean it is likely to happen.

Legally answerable for something, answerable to somebody — or, in the everyday sense, likely to do something.

  1. The contractor is liable for any damage to the sidewalk.
  2. Partners are jointly and severally liable for the firm's debts.
  3. She's liable to the tenants for the deposit, whatever the manager did.
  4. That pipe is liable to freeze if the crawlspace door stays open.
  5. He's liable to say something he can't take back.
See also
responsible for, accountable to, accountable for, answerable to, subject to, for

look forward to#

Pronunciation
/lʊk ˈfɔrwərd tu/ · look FOR-wurd too · weak /lʊk ˈfɔrwərd tə/
Русский
с нетерпением ждать — после to герундий, не инфинитив
Type
verb + preposition — included here because it is the classic case of the fixed to
Register
neutral; the standard sign-off of American business correspondence
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (look forward to hearing from you) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ I look forward to hear from you is the single most-corrected error in professional English, and the noun test proves the point: look forward to the weekend ✓, so to is a preposition.
Pattern
look forward to [noun / verb-ing] · look forward to [someone] -ing (look forward to your joining us) · be looking forward to [noun / verb-ing] · much / greatly looking forward to [noun]
Variants
look ahead to is the neutral news-writing version; anticipate takes a direct object with no preposition at all (anticipate the results).
Contrast
The whole difficulty is that forward to looks like an infinitive and is not. Compare the three: ✓ look forward to seeing you, ✓ hope to see you, ✗ look forward to see you. The same fixed to governs object to, be opposed to, be accustomed to, be used to, be committed to, be dedicated to, confess to, admit to, and resort to — a list worth learning as a set, because every one of them takes -ing: ✓ object to being asked, ✓ confessed to taking it, ✓ resorted to calling her at home. In the progressive, I'm looking forward to is warmer and more personal than the simple I look forward to, which is the formal sign-off. This is the one verb phrase given a full entry here rather than next door; the rest of the look family — look at, look for, look after, look into — is treated with the verbs.

Anticipate something with pleasure.

  1. I look forward to hearing from you at your convenience.
  2. She's looking forward to three weeks with no email.
  3. We look forward to working with your team on the next phase.
  4. He looks forward to Thursdays the way other people look forward to Fridays.
  5. Nobody is looking forward to explaining this to the board.
See also
accustomed to, used to, opposed to, committed to, look at / for / after / into, to

loyal to#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɔɪəl tu/ · LOY-ul too · weak /ˈlɔɪəl tə/
Русский
верный, преданный кому-л. или чему-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun phrase or pronoun naming the person, group, or cause you stand by — never a gerund (✗ loyal to showing up) and never a clause. The abstract complement (loyal to the idea, loyal to a fault — the fixed phrase) is a noun in every case.
Pattern
be loyal to [person / institution / cause] · remain / stay loyal to · fiercely / doggedly loyal to · loyal to a fault — the frozen phrase · loyalty to [noun] — the noun keeps to
Variants
faithful to is the near twin and covers marriage and duty; devoted to stresses feeling; true to covers principles and one's word; committed to is the workplace version.
Contrast
Against devoted to, which is about attachment and effort, loyal to is about not defecting — it presupposes that leaving was an option. Against faithful to, the difference is mostly domain: faithfulness belongs to promises and marriages, loyalty to people, teams, and brands. Note that loyal rarely takes a complement at all in advertising (loyal customers), where the noun follows the adjective instead. ✗ loyal with and ✗ loyal for are errors.

Steadfast in supporting a person, a group, or a cause when it would be easy not to be.

  1. She stayed loyal to the shop through two changes of ownership.
  2. He's loyal to the people who hired him, not to the company that bought it.
  3. Customers loyal to the old formula complained for a year.
  4. They remained loyal to a candidate everyone else had written off.
  5. Nobody in that district is loyal to a party for its own sake.
See also
devoted to, committed to, dedicated to, married to, to

mad at#

Pronunciation
/mæd æt/ · MAD at · weak /mæd ət/
Русский
злящийся на кого-л. — разг., обычное американское слово
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
informal; the American conversational default for anger
Complement
a person after at; a noun phrase or gerund after about (mad about being left out) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: mad that nobody told her.
Pattern
be mad at [person] · be mad about [thing] · get mad at [person] · be mad at [person] for [verb-ing] · be mad about [someone] — the British "in love with" sense · mad alone, meaning insane
Variants
angry with / at is the neutral version; ticked off at and pissed at are the escalating informal ones.
Contrast
The American and British senses of mad about are opposites, and this is a genuine transatlantic trap. In American English, mad about the schedule means angry about it; in British English, mad about the boy means crazy about him. Americans use crazy about for affection and reserve mad for anger, so a British sentence like She's mad about him will be misread in the United States. Within American usage the split is clean: at takes the person, about takes the situation. ✗ mad with is an error in American English except in the fixed mad with grief, meaning driven insane.

Angry — the everyday American word, informal but universal.

  1. She's still mad at me for telling her mother.
  2. He got mad about the parking situation and wrote three emails.
  3. Don't be mad at the dog; you left the gate open.
  4. They were mad that the meeting had been moved without notice.
  5. I'm not mad at you, I'm mad about how it was handled.
See also
angry with, furious with, annoyed with, crazy about, at

married to#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɛrid tu/ · MAIR-eed too · weak /ˈmɛrid tə/
Русский
женатый на, замужем за; перен. — намертво привязанный к
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person after to; in the figurative sense, an idea or an institution (married to the job) — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
be married to [person] · get married to [person] · marry [person] — the verb, with no preposition · be married (alone) · married with children — a fixed idiom about having them, not about whom you married · married into [a family]
Variants
wed to is the headline and literary version and takes the same to.
Contrast
Married to, not ✗ married with. This is one of the most common errors made by speakers of Romance and Slavic languages, whose equivalents use "with," and it is worth drilling: ✓ She's married to a pilot*, ✗ She's married with a pilot. The one place with is correct is the fixed phrase married with two kids, where with introduces the children, not the spouse. Note also that the verb takes no preposition at all — ✓ He married her, ✗ He married with her, ✗ He married to her — so the adjective and the verb differ; see the zero-preposition entry for marry. Married into means joining a family by marriage.

Joined in marriage to someone — or, figuratively, bound to something.

  1. She's been married to the same man since 1994.
  2. He got married to his high-school lab partner twenty years later.
  3. They're married with three kids and a dog nobody planned on.
  4. The department is married to a process that stopped working years ago.
  5. She married a man from Duluth — the verb, with no preposition.
See also
marry, engaged to, committed to, related to, to

material to#

Pronunciation
/məˈtɪriəl tu/ · muh-TEER-ee-ul too · weak /məˈtɪriəl tə/
Русский
существенный для решения или исхода — юр./книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal and accounting; in those fields it is a precise term (material to the decision, a material misstatement), and it is nearly the only place the adjective takes a complement
Complement
a noun phrase naming the question, decision, or outcome that the fact could affect, or a gerund (material to deciding the motion). Never a to-infinitive: ✗ material to decide the motion. A wh-clause is fine: material to what the buyer agreed to pay.
Pattern
be material to [issue / decision] · a fact material to [noun] · not material to [noun] — the common denial · a material breach / material weakness — attributive terms of art, no preposition
Variants
relevant to is the broader everyday word; germane to is about topical fit; pertinent to is the formal synonym of relevant; significant to is the plain paraphrase.
Contrast
Relevant is the low bar and material the high one. Evidence is relevant to a case if it bears on it at all; a fact is material to a decision only if it could have changed the outcome — which is why American securities law defines materiality by whether a reasonable investor would want to know. Against dispositive of, which settles the question outright, material to* only means the fact could matter. Watch the unrelated senses of material: the noun (building material), and the adjective meaning physical (material evidence in the sense of tangible), neither of which takes to. ✗ material for and ✗ material in are errors here.

Important enough to a decision or an outcome that it could change it.

  1. The lawsuit is material to the buyer's decision and has to be disclosed.
  2. Whether the roof leaked is material to the price, not just to the mood.
  3. Auditors flagged an error material to the quarter's results.
  4. Nothing in the correspondence is material to the jurisdictional question.
  5. Any change material to the coverage must be reported within ten days.
See also
relevant to, germane to, pertinent to, dispositive of, to

mindful of#

Pronunciation
/ˈmaɪndfəl ʌv/ · MYND-ful uv · weak /ˈmaɪn(d)fəl əv/ — the d is usually dropped: "MYNE-ful-uv"
Русский
помнящий о чём-л. и сообразующий с этим свои действия
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; common in official statements and in careful speech, and quite separate from the wellness sense of mindfulness
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (mindful of being the newest person in the room) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ mindful of to be the newest. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective with no preposition: mindful that the deadline is Friday ✓.
Pattern
be mindful of [noun / verb-ing] · be mindful (that) [clause] · ever / always / painfully mindful of · bear [something] in mind — the idiom, with a different shape
Variants
conscious of and aware of are the neutral equivalents; cognizant of the formal one; alert to points forward at a risk; heedful of is the literary version.
Contrast
Mindful of means you are letting the fact govern how you act, which is more than aware of claims: a driver aware of the ice knows about it, a driver mindful of the ice slows down. Against conscious of, which reports what is present in your mind, mindful of* reports what is guiding your conduct — which is why it fits instructions and public statements so well. Note the separate modern noun mindfulness, a meditation practice, which takes of only in its older sense. ✗ mindful about and ✗ mindful to are errors, though the imperative mind the gap is a different construction entirely.

Keeping something in mind and letting it shape what you do.

  1. Be mindful of the neighbors when you run the saw before eight.
  2. The court was mindful of the burden this places on small towns.
  3. Ever mindful of the budget, she priced three options before calling.
  4. He wrote the memo mindful of how it would read in six months.
  5. We're mindful of being guests in somebody else's building.
See also
aware of, conscious of, cognizant of, careful with, of

native to#

Pronunciation
/ˈneɪtɪv tu/ · NAY-tiv too · weak /ˈneɪtəv tə/
Русский
родом из, исконно обитающий или произрастающий в
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to technical — biology, gardening, and geography; also used figuratively of habits and instincts
Complement
a noun phrase naming a place, a region, or a habitat — native to the Pacific Northwest, native to alkaline soils. Never a gerund and never a clause. When the complement is a person rather than a place, the frame changes: native speaker of Spanish, a native of Ohio — both with of.
Pattern
[species] be native to [place] · plants native to [region] · a native of [place] — the noun frame, with of · native [noun] — attributive (native grasses, native speaker)
Variants
indigenous to is the formal equivalent and is the preferred word for peoples; endemic to is stronger and means found nowhere else; originating in is the neutral paraphrase; the opposites are introduced, nonnative, and invasive.
Contrast
Native to says it originated here; endemic to says it is found only here. Douglas fir is native to the Northwest and also grows elsewhere; an island snail may be endemic to one valley. Note the switch between adjective and noun: a species is native to* Ohio, but a person is a native of Ohio — ✗ he is native to Ohio is not how Americans say it about people. In computing, native to has a live technical sense (an app native to the platform), meaning built for it rather than ported. ✗ native of after the adjective and ✗ native in are errors.

Originating in a particular place and belonging there naturally.

  1. The shrub is native to the dry side of the mountains.
  2. These beetles aren't native to North America and have no predators here.
  3. The nursery stocks only plants native to the watershed.
  4. Cynicism about zoning is native to this county.
  5. The format is native to the older software and has to be converted.
See also
endemic to, peculiar to, specific to, common to, to

nervous about#

Pronunciation
/ˈnɜrvəs əˈbaʊt/ · NUR-vuss uh-BOWT · weak /ˈnɜrvəs əˈbaʊt/
Русский
нервничающий из-за предстоящего
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about (nervous about flying) — never a to-infinitive after about. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (nervous to ask), less commonly. After of, a thing feared — a usage that is British-flavored and rarer in the United States.
Pattern
be nervous about [noun / verb-ing] · get nervous about [noun] · be nervous of [thing] — chiefly British · make [someone] nervous (no preposition on the object)
Variants
anxious about is the more formal twin; worried about the more consequential one; jittery about and antsy about the informal American ones.
Contrast
Nervous about is about an upcoming event; afraid of is about a standing fear. Nervous about the flight* is one flight; afraid of flying is every flight. In American English, ✗ nervous of dogs sounds foreign — the American choice is afraid of dogs or nervous around dogs. Note the useful fourth frame nervous around, which names the company that produces the feeling rather than the event.

Uneasy about something that is coming.

  1. He's nervous about the presentation on Monday.
  2. She gets nervous about driving in snow after what happened.
  3. Everyone was nervous about how the vote would go.
  4. I'm nervous about leaving the house empty for three weeks.
  5. He's nervous around horses, which makes them nervous too.
See also

new to#

Pronunciation
/nu tu/ · NOO too · weak /nu tə/ — American English says NOO, not the British NYOO
Русский
новичок в чём-л., ещё не освоившийся; о вещи — впервые встреченный кем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral and everyday
Complement
a noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (new to managing people) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ new to manage people. The noun test settles it: new to the job ✓.
Pattern
be new to [place / job / activity] · new to [someone] — meaning unfamiliar to them: that idea is new to me · brand new to [noun] · fairly / relatively new to
Variants
unfamiliar with looks at the same fact from the other end; a newcomer to is the noun phrase; green at is the informal one for inexperience; just starting out is the plain paraphrase.
Contrast
The subject decides which way the phrase points. With a person as subject, new to means inexperienced at something: she's new to the department. With a thing as subject, it means unfamiliar to somebody: this rule is new to me. The two are the same construction pointed in opposite directions, and only context tells them apart. Against unfamiliar with, which reports what you do not know, new to reports how recently you arrived. ✗ new in is used of places in some varieties (new in town is the fixed American exception) and ✗ new at survives only in new at this.

Recently arrived at something and not yet used to it — or, of a thing, not previously encountered.

  1. He's new to the route and still checking addresses twice.
  2. I'm new to keeping bees and have already been stung twice.
  3. That objection is new to me — nobody raised it in committee.
  4. She's new to the city but not new to the work.
  5. Anyone new to the building gets a badge on the first day.
See also
familiar with, accustomed to, used to, acquainted with, to

nice to#

Pronunciation
/naɪs tu/ · NYSS too · weak /naɪs tə/
Русский
любезный, приветливый с кем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
informal to neutral
Complement
a person or animal after to — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The judgment frame takes of plus a doer and an infinitive (nice of you to come), and the evaluative frame takes an infinitive with no preposition (nice to meet you).
Pattern
be nice to [person] · it is nice of [someone] to [verb] · it is nice to [verb] · be nice about [something] — gracious regarding · play nice with [someone]
Variants
kind to, good to, pleasant to, and decent to are the same frame at different temperatures.
Contrast
The three nice frames say three different things. Nice to meet you evaluates the meeting. Nice of you to come praises the person for coming. Be nice to your brother instructs about treatment. The last is the one that takes the fixed preposition, and it patterns with kind to, polite to, rude to, and cruel to. ✗ nice with is an error except in the informal play nice with, and ✗ nice for you to come changes the meaning to convenience rather than kindness.

Behaving pleasantly toward someone.

  1. Be nice to your sister; she's had a long week.
  2. The staff there is nice to everybody, which is not nothing.
  3. It was nice of him to drive all that way.
  4. She was nice about the whole misunderstanding.
  5. The weather has been nice to us for three straight weekends.
See also

notorious for#

Pronunciation
/noʊˈtɔriəs fɔr/ · noh-TOR-ee-uss for · weak /noʊˈtɔriəs fər/
Русский
печально известный чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to journalistic
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after for (notorious for showing up late) — never a to-infinitive. After as, a noun naming a role (notorious as a bad tipper).
Pattern
be notorious for [fault / verb-ing] · be notorious as [role] · notoriously [adjective] — the adverb frame · notorious among [group]
Variants
infamous for is the same idea with a stronger moral charge; known for is the neutral one; famous for is the positive one.
Contrast
Notorious is always negative. Famous for its pies and notorious for its pies are opposite reviews. The word cannot be used approvingly without irony, which is why ✗ notorious for her generosity reads as a joke. The adverb frame is often the neater choice — a notoriously slow intersection beats an intersection notorious for slowness. ✗ notorious of and ✗ notorious about are errors.

Widely known for something bad.

  1. That intersection is notorious for fender-benders.
  2. He's notorious for rewriting the agenda ten minutes before the meeting.
  3. The permit office is notorious for losing paperwork.
  4. She was notorious as the toughest grader in the department.
  5. August is notorious for thunderstorms that arrive exactly at rush hour.

obligated to, obliged to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɑblɪɡeɪtɪd tu, əˈblaɪdʒd tu/ · OB-luh-gay-tid too, uh-BLYJED too · weak /ˈɑbləɡeɪdəd tə/ and /əˈblaɪdʒd tə/
Русский
обязанный сделать что-л. по долгу или закону; в старом смысле — признательный кому-л.
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition or infinitive marker
Register
obligated to is the standard American form; obliged to is commoner in Britain and survives in America in the courteous much obliged
Complement
two frames, and the to is a different word in each. Before a person or body, to is a preposition: obligated to the bank, obliged to her for the introduction. Before a duty, to is an infinitive marker and a bare verb follows: obligated to notify the tenant. What is impossible is the gerund: ✗ obligated to notifying the tenant. The noun test tells you which frame you are in — obligated to the bank ✓ is prepositional, obligated to notify is not.
Pattern
be obligated to [verb] · be obligated to [person] to [verb] — both slots at once · feel obligated to [verb] · be under an obligation to [verb] · much obliged — the fixed courtesy, no complement
Variants
required to and bound to fill the duty frame; indebted to and beholden to fill the person frame in the "owe a favor" sense; have to is the plain everyday verb.
Contrast
Obligated for duties, obliged for favors is the working American split: I'm obligated to file by the fifteenth* is a duty, I'm obliged to you for the ride is thanks. British English uses obliged for both and finds obligated heavy. Against beholden to, which always implies a constraining debt, obligated to is neutral about how you feel. Note that this entry is one of the few in the file where a bare infinitive is required — the fixed to here is not always a preposition, which is exactly why the gerund error does not occur with it.

Bound by duty or by law to do something — or, in the older sense, indebted to someone for a kindness.

  1. The landlord is obligated to give twenty-four hours' notice.
  2. Nobody is obligated to answer a question at the counter.
  3. She felt obligated to say something before the vote.
  4. We're obligated to the bondholders first and to everyone else after.
  5. I'm much obliged to you for driving out here in this weather.
See also
beholden to, bound by, incumbent on, incumbent upon, liable for, liable to, to

oblivious to#

Pronunciation
/əˈblɪviəs tu/ · uh-BLIV-ee-us too · weak /əˈblɪviəs tə/
Русский
не замечающий того, что прямо перед ним — обычно с укором
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral, with a critical edge — to call someone oblivious is not a compliment
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (oblivious to being watched) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ oblivious to be watched. A that-clause is impossible; use unaware that instead.
Pattern
be oblivious to [noun / verb-ing] · oblivious of [noun] — the older variant · completely / blissfully / cheerfully oblivious to · oblivious — used alone, with the complement understood
Variants
unaware of is the neutral equivalent; blind to is the metaphorical one; heedless of is literary; out of it is the slang paraphrase.
Contrast
Oblivious to and oblivious of are both correct, with to* now dominant in American English and of the older and more literary choice; usage guides once insisted the word meant only "forgetful of" (from Latin oblivisci), but that battle ended a century ago. Against unaware of, which is neutral, oblivious to implies the person should have noticed — the word carries a judgment. Against impervious to, which means it does not get through, oblivious to means it never registered at all. ✗ oblivious about is an error.

Not noticing something that is right there, and usually blamed for it.

  1. He walked into the meeting oblivious to what everyone else had heard.
  2. She's oblivious to how loud that laugh is.
  3. Drivers seem oblivious to the new signal at the crosswalk.
  4. The kids played on, oblivious to the fact that it was pouring.
  5. He was oblivious to being the only one still arguing.
See also

obsessed with#

Pronunciation
/əbˈsɛst wɪθ/ · ub-SEST with · weak /əbˈsɛst wəθ/
Русский
одержимый чем-л., не могущий перестать думать о
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral in the clinical sense; heavily used as hyperbole in American speech
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (obsessed with getting it right) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be obsessed with [noun / verb-ing] · become obsessed with [noun] · obsessed by [an idea that pursues you] · an obsession with [noun] — the noun keeps with
Variants
fixated on takes on; preoccupied with takes with; consumed by takes by. The casual American can't stop thinking about is the plain paraphrase.
Contrast
Obsessed with is the ordinary choice; obsessed by casts the idea as the agent that pursues the person — obsessed by the thought that he'd forgotten something* is more literary and slightly more sinister than obsessed with. ✗ obsessed about is an error, although the verb obsess takes about correctly (stop obsessing about it), which is the source of the mistake. ✗ obsessed on borrows from fixated on.

Unable to stop thinking about something.

  1. She's obsessed with getting the drainage right before the spring rains.
  2. He's been obsessed with that band since eighth grade.
  3. The department is obsessed with metrics that measure nothing.
  4. I'm obsessed with this bread and I'm not going to apologize.
  5. He was obsessed by the idea that the file had been altered.

opposed to#

Pronunciation
/əˈpoʊzd tu/ · uh-POHZD too · weak /əˈpoʊzd tə/
Русский
выступающий против чего-л. — после to герундий
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (opposed to raising the fee) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ opposed to raise the fee is the error, and it is common in policy writing; the noun test settles it (opposed to the increase ✓).
Pattern
be opposed to [noun / verb-ing] · be strongly / firmly / bitterly opposed to [noun] · oppose [something] — the verb, with no preposition · as opposed to [noun] — the comparison idiom
Variants
against is the plain preposition that says the same thing and takes the same complement (against raising the fee). Averse to is the formal near-synonym and also takes a gerund.
Contrast
The verb and the adjective take different structures. ✓ They oppose the plan (no preposition), ✓ They are opposed to the plan — and ✗ They oppose to the plan is a frequent error. Note the separate idiom as opposed to, which is a comparison marker rather than a statement of opposition: hourly as opposed to salaried simply contrasts two categories; see as opposed to. Not opposed to is standard American understatement for mildly in favor.

Against something — a plan, a policy, a course of action.

  1. The council is opposed to rezoning the whole block at once.
  2. She's not opposed to working weekends, within reason.
  3. Most residents are opposed to the highway extension.
  4. He's firmly opposed to anything that lengthens the meeting.
  5. We're talking about a lease, as opposed to a purchase.
See also
committed to, reluctant to, against, as opposed to, to

orthogonal to#

Pronunciation
/ɔrˈθɑɡənəl tu/ · or-THOG-uh-nul too · weak /ɔrˈθɑɡənəl tə/
Русский
перпендикулярный (мат.); перен. — лежащий в другой плоскости, не относящийся к делу
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical in mathematics and statistics; borrowed as jargon into engineering, economics, and American office talk, where orthogonal to means "beside the point"
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other vector, factor, dimension, or question — never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
[vector] be orthogonal to [vector] · orthogonal to [the question at hand] — the jargon use · mutually orthogonal · orthogonality — the noun
Variants
perpendicular to is the geometric equivalent in two and three dimensions; at right angles to is the plain phrase; independent of and unrelated to are what the jargon use actually means; uncorrelated with is the statistician's term.
Contrast
Perpendicular to is about lines you can draw; orthogonal to generalizes the idea to any number of dimensions — and to statistics, where orthogonal factors vary independently. The office use (that's orthogonal to what we're deciding*) is a borrowed metaphor meaning "unrelated, and let's not conflate them"; some readers find it affected, and unrelated to says the same thing. Against independent of, which is the plain word for the same relation, orthogonal to claims the two things can be varied without touching each other. ✗ orthogonal with and ✗ orthogonal from are errors.

At right angles to something — literally, in geometry, and figuratively, of questions that do not bear on each other.

  1. The two vectors are orthogonal to each other, so their dot product is zero.
  2. Choose a basis in which each axis is orthogonal to the others.
  3. The two treatment factors were made orthogonal to one another by design.
  4. Cost is orthogonal to the question of whether the thing works at all.
  5. His objection is orthogonal to the motion on the floor.
See also
perpendicular to, parallel to, independent of, tangent to, to

parallel to#

Pronunciation
/ˈpærəˌlɛl tu/ · PAIR-uh-lel too · weak /ˈpærəˌlɛl tə/ — note the spelling, one r and two l's in the middle
Русский
параллельный чему-л. — в пространстве или в развитии
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to technical; geometry, road directions, and analytical writing about resemblances
Complement
a noun phrase naming the line, path, course, or case that runs alongside — never a gerund and never a clause. The variant parallel with takes the same complement.
Pattern
[line / street] be parallel to [line] · run parallel to [noun] · parallel with [noun] — the equally standard variant · a parallel to / a parallel with [noun] — the noun frame, both prepositions available · draw a parallel between [X] and [Y]
Variants
parallel with is fully standard and slightly commoner when the two things move together in time; alongside is the plain preposition; analogous to covers the figurative sense more precisely.
Contrast
Parallel to and parallel with are both correct, and no American reader will notice which you used; if there is a tendency, to* suits fixed geometric relations (a line parallel to the base) and with suits things developing side by side (her career ran parallel with his). Against analogous to, which claims corresponding relations, parallel to claims that two courses run alongside without meeting. The noun takes between in the draw a parallel frame, which is a third pattern worth keeping separate. ✗ parallel of is an error, except in the geographic noun (the 49th parallel).

Running alongside something at a constant distance, in space or in development.

  1. The service road runs parallel to the highway for about four miles.
  2. Draw a line parallel to the base through the midpoint.
  3. Her research is parallel to what the Michigan group published last spring.
  4. The bike path is parallel to the river the whole way.
  5. The two investigations proceeded parallel to each other for months.
See also
perpendicular to, adjacent to, analogous to, orthogonal to, to

passionate about#

Pronunciation
/ˈpæʃənɪt əˈbaʊt/ · PASH-uh-nit uh-BOWT · weak /ˈpæʃənət əˈbaʊt/, and in fast speech the preposition reduces to "'BOWT"
Русский
страстно увлечённый чем-л. — не на минуту, а надолго
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; a fixture of American cover letters, mission statements, and dating profiles, to the point of being a cliché in all three
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (passionate about getting kids outdoors) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ passionate about to teach, ✗ passionate to teach in this sense. The noun test is immediate: passionate about the work ✓.
Pattern
be passionate about [noun / verb-ing] · deeply / genuinely passionate about · a passion for [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to for · passionate [noun] — attributive (a passionate defense)
Variants
enthusiastic about is the neutral equivalent; crazy about and nuts about are the informal ones; devoted to and committed to take to; keen on is British-leaning.
Contrast
Adjective about, noun for. ✓ passionate about food, ✓ a passion for food, ✗ a passion about food, ✗ passionate for food. That switch is the only structural trap here. The register trap is larger: American hiring managers have read passionate about so many thousand times that the phrase now carries almost no information, and a concrete verb usually beats it. Against enthusiastic about, which is about visible energy, passionate about claims a deeper and more durable commitment.

Caring intensely about something over a long stretch, not just at the moment.

  1. She's passionate about getting the trail finished before the fall.
  2. He's passionate about barbecue in a way that has cost him a garage.
  3. The whole staff is passionate about the work and underpaid for it.
  4. I'm not passionate about spreadsheets, but I'm good at them.
  5. They're passionate about keeping the pool open year-round.
See also

patient with#

Pronunciation
/ˈpeɪʃənt wɪθ/ · PAY-shunt with · weak /ˈpeɪʃənt wəθ/
Русский
терпеливый с кем-л., не раздражающийся
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person, animal, or process after with — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive. The adjective also appears bare (be patient) and with about naming the matter (patient about the delay).
Pattern
be patient with [person / process] · be patient about [matter] · be patient (alone) · lose patience with [person] — the noun keeps with
Variants
tolerant of takes of; forbearing toward is the formal one; easy on is the informal one.
Contrast
Patient takes with*, not to, which puts it with gentle with* and against kind to, nice to, and polite to. The reason is the same: with marks the manner of dealing, to marks the direction of treatment. Note that the noun follows the adjective — patience with the process, out of patience with him — and that the medical noun patient is a different word entirely, taking of only in the possessive sense (a patient of Dr. Reyes). ✗ patient at is an error.

Willing to put up with delay, difficulty, or slowness without getting angry.

  1. She's endlessly patient with students who ask the same question twice.
  2. Be patient with the software; it takes a minute to index.
  3. He was never patient with anyone who hadn't read the material.
  4. The coach is patient with mistakes and hard on carelessness.
  5. Everyone lost patience with the process about six months in.

peculiar to#

Pronunciation
/pɪˈkjuljər tu/ · pih-KYOOL-yur too · weak /pəˈkjuljər tə/
Русский
свойственный только кому-л. или чему-л. и никому больше
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; standard in scholarly and technical writing, where it means "belonging exclusively to" and carries none of the "odd" sense
Complement
a noun phrase naming the group, place, or thing that alone has the feature — never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
[feature] be peculiar to [group / place] · not peculiar to [noun] — a common frame for denying that a problem is local · a usage peculiar to [dialect] · peculiar — the everyday adjective meaning strange, with no complement
Variants
specific to is the neutral modern equivalent; unique to is the strongest; characteristic of stresses typicality rather than exclusivity; endemic to covers species and diseases.
Contrast
Peculiar to means found only there; the bare adjective peculiar means odd, and the presence of the complement is what tells the two apart: a custom peculiar to the valley* is exclusive, a peculiar custom is strange. Against characteristic of, which says the trait is typical of a thing but may appear elsewhere, peculiar to claims exclusivity. Against common to, the two are near opposites: common to several, peculiar to one. ✗ peculiar for and ✗ peculiar of are errors.

Belonging to one group, place, or thing and to no other.

  1. The vowel is peculiar to speakers born east of the river.
  2. That kind of feud is not peculiar to small towns, whatever people say.
  3. The bookkeeping method is peculiar to this one branch of the business.
  4. There's a stubbornness peculiar to people who have farmed dry land.
  5. The defect is peculiar to units built in the first two months.
See also
specific to, characteristic of, endemic to, common to, to

perpendicular to#

Pronunciation
/ˌpɜrpənˈdɪkjələr tu/ · pur-pun-DIK-yuh-lur too · weak /ˌpɜrpənˈdɪkjələr tə/
Русский
перпендикулярный чему-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to technical — geometry, construction, and directions
Complement
a noun phrase naming the line, surface, or direction — never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
[line] be perpendicular to [line / plane] · run / sit / set [something] perpendicular to [noun] · the perpendicular — the noun, meaning a vertical line · at right angles to — the plain equivalent
Variants
at right angles to is the everyday paraphrase; orthogonal to is the generalized mathematical term; normal to is the physicist's word for perpendicular to a surface; square to is the carpenter's.
Contrast
Against orthogonal to, which generalizes to any number of dimensions and to statistics, perpendicular to stays concrete and geometric. Note that in physics and engineering the word for the same relation to a surface is normal to, which is the term a reader will meet in optics and mechanics. In carpentry, square to does the same job. ✗ perpendicular with and ✗ perpendicular from are errors.

Meeting something at a right angle.

  1. The new wing is perpendicular to the original building.
  2. Set the fence perpendicular to the blade before you cut.
  3. The cross street runs perpendicular to the avenue for two blocks.
  4. Drop a line perpendicular to the base from the opposite vertex.
  5. Park perpendicular to the curb here, not at an angle.
