pertinent to#

Pronunciation
/ˈpɜrtənənt tu/ · PUR-tuh-nunt too · weak /ˈpɜrtənənt tə/
Русский
относящийся к делу, имеющий прямое касательство к
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; the natural home is a document request, a policy memo, or a records search — all documents pertinent to the transaction
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (pertinent to deciding whether to appeal) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ pertinent to decide. A wh-clause is fine: pertinent to what the committee has to vote on.
Pattern
be pertinent to [matter] · [documents / facts] pertinent to [noun] · directly / hardly pertinent to · pertain to [noun] — the verb, catalogued with the verb patterns
Variants
relevant to is the neutral everyday word and will replace it anywhere; germane to is the parliamentary one; material to is the legal one; applicable to covers rules rather than facts; appertaining to is the archaic legal form.
Contrast
Pertinent to and relevant to are close to interchangeable, with pertinent the more formal and slightly narrower of the two — it suggests the thing bears directly on the matter rather than merely touching it. Against material to, which in law asks whether the fact could change the outcome, pertinent to asks only whether it belongs to the subject. Do not confuse the adjective with impertinent, whose everyday meaning is "rude," though its older sense — not pertinent — survives in the legal phrase scandalous and impertinent matter. ✗ pertinent for and ✗ pertinent with are errors.

Bearing directly on the matter at hand.

  1. Please produce every email pertinent to the September order.
  2. That fact is interesting but not pertinent to the claim she filed.
  3. The manual's third chapter is the only part pertinent to our setup.
  4. Keep the questions pertinent to the position, not to her family.
  5. Anything pertinent to safety goes in the first section of the report.
See also
relevant to, germane to, material to, probative of, to

pleased with#

Pronunciation
/plizd wɪθ/ · PLEEZD with · weak /plizd wəθ/
Русский
довольный чем-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase after with, about, and by; a gerund after about (pleased about being asked) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (pleased to announce) and a that-clause (pleased that it worked out).
Pattern
be pleased with [thing assessed] · be pleased about [situation] · be pleased by [the cause] · be pleased to [verb] · be pleased (that) [clause] · pleased with oneself
Variants
happy with is the everyday equivalent; satisfied with the more measured one; delighted with the warmer one.
Contrast
With takes what you are judging; about takes the situation; by takes the cause. Pleased with the results* is a verdict; pleased about the outcome is a mood; pleased by her reaction points at what produced it. The infinitive frame is a formality marker — We are pleased to inform you — and never takes a preposition. Pleased with himself is fixed and means smug, not merely satisfied. ✗ pleased of is an error.

Satisfied and glad about something.

  1. The client was pleased with the revised layout.
  2. She's pleased about getting the earlier slot.
  3. Everyone was pleased by how smoothly the move went.
  4. We are pleased to offer you the position.
  5. He looked far too pleased with himself for someone who'd just been lucky.

polite to#

Pronunciation
/pəˈlaɪt tu/ · puh-LYT too · weak /pəˈlaɪt tə/
Русский
вежливый с кем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person after to — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive. The judgment frame takes of plus a doer and an infinitive: It was polite of him to ask first.
Pattern
be polite to [person] · it is polite to [verb] · it is polite of [someone] to [verb] · polite about [something] — gracious regarding
Variants
courteous to, civil to, and respectful toward are the formal ladder; nice to is the everyday one.
Contrast
Polite to joins kind to, nice to, rude to, and cruel to in taking to for the person treated. ✗ polite with* is an error. The difference from nice to is one of temperature: politeness is a matter of form and can be cold — He was perfectly polite to her often implies he was nothing more — while nice to implies warmth. Polite about names the matter one is gracious concerning: polite about the burned dinner.

Showing good manners toward someone.

  1. Be polite to the inspector even if he's wrong.
  2. She was polite to everyone and warm to almost no one.
  3. It's polite to let the host start eating first.
  4. It was polite of him to pretend not to notice.
  5. The clerk stayed polite to the very end of a long afternoon.

poor at#

Pronunciation
/pʊr æt/ · POOR at · weak /pʊr ət/; also /pɔr/, rhyming with pore
Русский
слабый в чём-л. (о навыке); poor in — бедный чем-л. (о ресурсе)
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; bad at is the everyday version
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (poor at estimating time) — never a to-infinitive. After in, a resource lacked (poor in mineral content), which is a different sense and the direct opposite of rich in.
Pattern
be poor at [skill / verb-ing] · be poor in [resource] · a poor [noun] — attributive, no preposition · poor showing / poor judgment
Variants
bad at, weak at, not strong at, and terrible at run from neutral to blunt; deficient in is the formal counterpart of poor in.
Contrast
Poor at a skill; poor in a resource. Poor at math* is unskilled; soil poor in nitrogen is lacking a substance, and the second sense is the exact mirror of rich in. ✗ poor on and ✗ poor with are errors for the skill sense, though poor with money does occur informally on the model of good with money. Note the register split: American writing prefers bad at in speech and poor at in evaluations and reports.

Not good at something — or, in the other sense, short of some substance.

  1. He's poor at estimating how long anything will take.
  2. The topsoil here is poor in organic matter.
  3. She's poor at delegating and knows it.
  4. The team is poor at closing out games it should win.
  5. Most of the district's schools are poor in everything but enthusiasm.

popular with#

Pronunciation
/ˈpɑpjələr wɪθ/ · POP-yuh-lur with · weak /ˈpɑpjələr wəθ/
Русский
популярный среди, пользующийся любовью у
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person or group after with and among — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive. After in, a place: popular in the Midwest.
Pattern
be popular with [group] · be popular among [group] · be popular in [place] · prove popular with [group] · not popular with [group] — the standard understatement
Variants
popular among is the formal equivalent and is preferred when the group is large or statistical; a hit with is the informal one; well liked by is the passive one.
Contrast
With takes the audience; in takes the territory; among takes a group considered as a set. Popular with teachers* and popular among teachers differ only in formality, but popular in Texas means something else — geography, not audience. ✗ popular by is an error (that would be the passive liked by), and ✗ popular to is an error, though popular with and appeal to cover the same ground with different words.

Liked by a lot of people in some group or place.

  1. The evening section is popular with students who work days.
  2. That policy was never popular with the people who had to enforce it.
  3. Sardines are popular in parts of the coast and nowhere else.
  4. The trail is popular among trail runners on weekday mornings.
  5. Telling them at the last minute will not be popular with anyone.

possessed of#

Pronunciation
/pəˈzɛst ʌv/ · puh-ZEST uv · weak /pəˈzɛst əv/, and before a consonant often /pəˈzɛs ə/ — the t drops: "puh-ZESS-uh"
Русский
обладающий чем-л. — качеством, способностью, имуществом; книжн./юр.
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
formal and literary, with a legal survival; an American meets possessed of in wills, in older fiction (a single man in possession of a good fortune is the same idea), and in judicial prose
Complement
a noun phrase naming what is held — a quality, a fortune, an estate, a faculty. Never a gerund and never a clause. The related but opposite frames take other prepositions and are set out under Contrast.
Pattern
be possessed of [quality / property] · seized and possessed of [land] — the old conveyancing pair · possessed of [one's faculties] · in possession of [noun] — the noun frame, with of
Variants
in possession of is the ordinary modern phrase and is what a police report uses; endowed with covers qualities given rather than held; have is the plain verb that replaces all of them.
Contrast
Possessed of means holding; possessed by means owned or controlled by; possessed with means gripped by an idea. A landowner is possessed of a hundred acres, a character in a horror film is possessed by a demon, and an obsessive is possessed with the notion that the neighbors are listening. Only the first is this entry. Against endowed with, which implies a giver, possessed of* says only that the thing is held. The whole pattern is formal: in ordinary American English, write has. ✗ possessed to is not English.

Having something — a quality, a faculty, or an estate — in the formal register.

  1. The testator was possessed of three properties at the time of death.
  2. She is possessed of a memory that makes note-taking look optional.
  3. Anyone possessed of the least common sense would have called first.
  4. He remained possessed of his faculties to the end.
  5. The trust is possessed of assets that have never been appraised.
See also
endowed with, vested with, seized of, seised of, replete with, of

predictive of#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈdɪktɪv ʌv/ · prih-DIK-tiv uv · weak /prəˈdɪktəv əv/
Русский
позволяющий предсказать что-л., прогностический в отношении
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical — statistics, medicine, and evaluation research; the everyday phrase is predicts or is a good sign of
Complement
a noun phrase naming the outcome predicted, or a gerund (predictive of dropping out) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ predictive of to drop out. A wh-clause is fine: predictive of who will renew.
Pattern
be predictive of [outcome] · highly / only weakly predictive of · predict [outcome] — the verb, direct object, no preposition · predictive value / predictive model — attributive
Variants
a predictor of is the noun phrase and takes of as well; associated with is the weaker and more careful claim; diagnostic of concerns present conditions rather than future ones; prognostic of is the clinical word for outcome.
Contrast
Predictive of points forward; indicative of points at what is already the case. A test result indicative of infection says something about now; a score predictive of relapse says something about later. Statisticians are careful with the word because prediction is not causation — predictive of* claims only that knowing one improves your guess about the other. Note the verb takes no preposition at all: ✓ the model predicts turnover, ✗ the model predicts of turnover. ✗ predictive for is an error, though a predictor for turns up in clinical writing.

Useful for forecasting something else, whether or not it causes it.

  1. Attendance in the first two weeks is predictive of whether a student finishes.
  2. The marker is only weakly predictive of recurrence.
  3. Nothing in the interview turned out to be predictive of performance.
  4. Rainfall in March is predictive of the fire season by August.
  5. Late payments are predictive of default about a year out.
See also
indicative of, symptomatic of, suggestive of, proportional to, of

prejudicial to#

Pronunciation
/ˌprɛdʒəˈdɪʃəl tu/ · pred-juh-DISH-ul too · weak /ˌprɛdʒəˈdɪʃəl tə/
Русский
наносящий ущерб правам или позиции, притом несправедливо — юр.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal; an American meets it in a courtroom objection (unfairly prejudicial to the defendant) and in employment and military discipline (conduct prejudicial to good order and discipline)
Complement
a noun phrase naming the person, party, or interest harmed — never a gerund and never a clause. The complement is the victim, never the cause.
Pattern
be prejudicial to [party / interest] · unfairly prejudicial to [noun] — the evidentiary standard · conduct prejudicial to [noun] · without prejudice to [rights] — the fixed phrase, catalogued with the legal prepositions
Variants
detrimental to is the neutral equivalent; damaging to the plain one; adverse to points at interests; harmful to covers people and bodies.
Contrast
Prejudicial to adds unfairness to harm. In American evidence law all damaging evidence hurts one side — that is the point of it — so the question is never whether evidence is prejudicial but whether it is unfairly* prejudicial, meaning it invites the jury to decide on the wrong basis. Against detrimental to, which is neutral about fairness, and adverse to, which describes a position rather than an effect, prejudicial to is the strongest of the three. Note the noun phrase without prejudice to, which means "without giving up," and the everyday sense of prejudice as bias, which takes against. ✗ prejudicial for and ✗ prejudicial of are errors.

Damaging to someone's rights, position, or case, and unfairly so.

  1. The photographs were ruled unfairly prejudicial to the defendant.
  2. Delay of this length is prejudicial to the tenants' ability to respond.
  3. The transfer would be prejudicial to creditors who filed on time.
  4. Conduct prejudicial to good order carries its own charge.
  5. Nothing in the amendment is prejudicial to rights already vested.
See also
detrimental to, adverse to, harmful to, material to, to

preparatory to#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈpærəˌtɔri tu/ · prih-PAIR-uh-tor-ee too · weak /prəˈpærəˌtɔri tə/
Русский
предваряющий что-л., совершаемый в порядке подготовки к — книжн.
Type
adjective functioning as a complex preposition
Register
formal and somewhat stiff — corporate minutes, engineering reports, and older prose; in speech an American says before or to get ready for
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (preparatory to filing the petition) — the gerund is the commoner of the two — and never a to-infinitive: ✗ preparatory to file the petition.
Pattern
[action] preparatory to [noun / verb-ing] · steps preparatory to [noun] · preparatory [noun] — attributive (preparatory work, a preparatory school)
Variants
prior to and in advance of are the neutral formal equivalents; in preparation for is the noun phrase and takes for; before is the plain word and beats all of them in most sentences.
Contrast
The phrase implies purpose, not just sequence. Preparatory to says the first action was done in order to make the second possible, where prior to says only that it came first. Note the switch of preposition in the noun frame: preparatory to the audit but in preparation for the audit. American style guides treat the whole phrase as a candidate for cutting — before the audit usually says it better. For the plain time prepositions, see time. ✗ preparatory for is an error in this frame.

Done in order to get ready for something that follows.

  1. The crew shut off the water preparatory to replacing the main.
  2. Files were boxed and indexed preparatory to the move.
  3. She resigned from the board preparatory to filing her own suit.
  4. Soil samples were taken preparatory to any grading work.
  5. The rehearsal is preparatory to Thursday's walkthrough with the inspector.
See also
previous to, antecedent to, prepared for, ready for, to

/prɪˈpɛrd fɔr/ · prih-PAIRD for · weak /prɪˈpɛrd fər/
Русский
готовый к чему-л.; с инфинитивом — готовый сделать что-л.
Type

neutral
Complement
a noun naming what is coming after for (prepared for the storm) — a gerund after for is possible but heavy. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (prepared to leave), which is the frame for willingness and is very common. Never a to-infinitive after for.
Pattern
be prepared for [event / eventuality] · be prepared to [verb] · prepare for [event] — the verb · prepare [someone] for [event] · be well prepared for [noun]
Variants
ready for / ready to runs exactly parallel and is the everyday version; braced for is the version reserved for something bad.
Contrast
Prepared for is readiness; prepared to is willingness. I'm prepared for a long meeting* means I have the coffee; I'm prepared to stay late means I'm willing. This is the same split as ready for / ready to, and the pair is worth learning as one lesson. ✗ prepared of is an error, and note that the verb prepare takes for the same way (prepare for the worst) but prepare + object takes no preposition (prepare dinner).

Made ready in advance — for what is coming, or to do what is asked.

  1. The crew was prepared for a two-day outage and got a five-day one.
  2. She's prepared to testify if it comes to that.
  3. Nobody was prepared for how quiet the building would be.
  4. Are you prepared for the questions they're going to ask?
  5. We came prepared for rain and got sun the whole week.
See also
ready for, late for, suitable for, for

previous to#

Pronunciation
/ˈpriviəs tu/ · PREE-vee-us too · weak /ˈpriviəs tə/
Русский
предшествующий чему-л., до — книжн. вместо before
Type
adjective functioning as a complex preposition
Register
formal and slightly dated; prior to has largely displaced it in American writing, and before beats both
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (previous to joining the firm) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ previous to join the firm. No clause follows; for a clause use before.
Pattern
[event] previous to [event] · previous to [verb-ing] · the previous [noun] — the ordinary attributive adjective, which is far commoner · previously — the adverb
Variants
prior to is the living formal twin and is what American business writing uses; before is the plain word; ahead of covers sequence in queues and schedules; antecedent to and anterior to are the archaic-formal versions.
Contrast
Everything previous to can do, before can do more simply, which is why usage guides on both sides of the Atlantic single the phrase out: before the hearing is shorter than previous to the hearing and loses nothing. Where it survives is in formal narrative and in older legal prose. Against preparatory to, which implies purpose, previous to claims nothing but sequence. Note that the attributive adjective (the previous tenant) is entirely ordinary modern English and is not this pattern. For the plain time prepositions, see time.

Coming before something in time — a formal way of saying before.

  1. Previous to the merger, the two firms shared a single supplier.
  2. She had no experience with grant work previous to joining the office.
  3. Nothing in the file is dated previous to March.
  4. Previous to that season, the team had never sold out a weeknight game.
  5. Any claim arising previous to closing stays with the seller.
See also
antecedent to, preparatory to, anterior to, posterior to, subject to, to

privy to#

Pronunciation
/ˈprɪvi tu/ · PRIV-ee too · weak /ˈprɪvi tə/
Русский
посвящённый во что-л. — допущенный к тайне, плану, договору; не просто знающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; common in journalism and in speech about secrets (not privy to those conversations), and a technical term in contract law
Complement
a noun phrase naming the information, discussion, or agreement you are inside of — never a to-infinitive (✗ privy to know), and a gerund is awkward enough that careful writers avoid it: prefer privy to the discussions over ✗ privy to their discussing it.
Pattern
be privy to [information / discussion] · not privy to [noun] — the commonest frame by far · privy to the contract — the legal sense · privity of contract / privity with [party] — the noun frame
Variants
in on is the informal equivalent (in on the plan); party to is the legal near-twin and is catalogued with the noun patterns; aware of is weaker; in the loop is the office idiom.
Contrast
Privy to means admitted to something private, not merely aware of it — the word implies you were let in. Against aware of, which reports knowledge from any source, privy to* reports access. The legal sense is narrower still: parties in privity have a direct contractual relationship, which is why a subcontractor is generally not privy to the owner's contract. Note the unrelated noun privy meaning an outhouse, a homograph that gives the word a faint comic edge in speech. ✗ privy of and ✗ privy with are errors, though in privity with is the correct legal phrase.

Let in on something private — a conversation, a plan, or a contract.

  1. I wasn't privy to whatever they decided in that meeting.
  2. Only three people were privy to the offer before it was announced.
  3. Staff privy to the numbers signed confidentiality agreements.
  4. The subcontractor was never privy to the owner's contract.
  5. She was privy to the discussions but had no vote.
See also
aware of, cognizant of, apprised of, acquainted with, to

probative of#

Pronunciation
/ˈproʊbətɪv ʌv/ · PROH-buh-tiv uv · weak /ˈproʊbədəv əv/, with the t flapped
Русский
доказывающий спорный факт — юр., доказательственный
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
legal, narrowly — evidence law; the word is essentially confined to briefs, rulings, and evidence classrooms
Complement
a noun phrase naming the fact to be proved — probative of intent, probative of knowledge, probative of identity. Never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
be probative of [fact at issue] · highly / marginally probative of · probative value — the noun phrase weighed against unfair prejudice under the American rules of evidence · more probative than prejudicial
Variants
tending to prove is the plain paraphrase; evidence of is the everyday noun phrase; indicative of is the general word; dispositive of is the strongest.
The whole American law of evidence turns on a scale this word sits on
evidence must be relevant, its probative value is then weighed against the risk of unfair prejudice, and only rarely is anything dispositive of the question. So probative of claims that the item tends to prove a fact — no more. Against indicative of, which is the general-purpose word for a sign, probative of is a term of art with a legal test behind it. ✗ probative to and ✗ probative for are errors.

Tending to prove a fact that is actually in dispute.

  1. The receipt is probative of when he was in the building, not of why.
  2. Counsel argued the tattoo was not probative of membership in anything.
  3. Prior repairs are probative of notice, which is the whole issue here.
  4. The court found the recording highly probative of intent.
  5. Testimony about her reputation is not probative of what happened that night.
See also
dispositive of, material to, indicative of, suggestive of, of

proportional to#

Pronunciation
/prəˈpɔrʃənəl tu/ · pruh-POR-shuh-nul too · weak /prəˈpɔrʃənəl tə/
Русский
пропорциональный чему-л.; разг. — соразмерный
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical in the mathematical sense, neutral in the everyday one about fairness
Complement
a noun phrase naming the other quantity — never a gerund and never a clause. Where the relation is inverse, the fixed phrase is inversely proportional to, with the same complement.
Pattern
[quantity] be proportional to [quantity] · directly proportional to · inversely proportional to — as one quantity rises the other falls · in proportion to [noun] — the prepositional phrase · proportionate to [noun] — the variant adjective, commoner in law and about fairness
Variants
proportionate to is the near-identical alternative and is the usual word in legal and ethical writing (a punishment proportionate to the offense); in proportion to is the phrase for informal comparisons; scales with is the working-scientist's verb; commensurate with is the formal near-synonym.
Contrast
Proportional to is a claim about a constant ratio: if y is proportional to x, doubling x doubles y, and inversely proportional to* means doubling x halves y. The everyday sense is looser and means only "appropriately sized," which is where proportionate to usually sits. Against equivalent to, which claims equal value, proportionality claims a fixed relationship between two changing quantities. The noun switches preposition: in proportion to, never ✗ in proportional to. ✗ proportional with is an error.

Standing in a constant ratio to something else — or, loosely, sized to fit it.

  1. The force is proportional to the square of the current.
  2. Brightness falls off inversely proportional to the square of the distance.
  3. Each partner's share is proportional to what they put in.
  4. Staffing should be proportional to the number of beds, not to the square footage.
  5. The fine is proportional to the tonnage, so the big haulers pay most.
See also
equivalent to, equal to, predictive of, related to, to

proud of#

Pronunciation
/praʊd ʌv/ · PROWD uv · weak /praʊd əv/
Русский
гордящийся чем-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (proud of having finished it alone) — never a to-infinitive after of. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (proud to serve) and a that-clause (proud that she'd asked).
Pattern
be proud of [person / thing / verb-ing] · be proud of [someone] for [verb-ing] · be proud to [verb] · be proud (that) [clause] · take pride in [noun] — the noun frame uses in
Variants
pleased with is the cooler version; boastful about the negative one; take pride in the formal noun frame.
Contrast
Proud of doing looks back at something accomplished; proud to do looks forward or reports an honor. I'm proud of serving* recalls the service; I'm proud to serve accepts it now. Note that the noun changes preposition: ✓ proud of her work, ✓ pride in her work, ✗ pride of her work — a good example of why these patterns cannot be reasoned out from meaning. ✗ proud for and ✗ proud about are errors.

Feeling satisfaction at something you or someone connected to you has done.

  1. She's proud of every one of those students.
  2. He's proud of having built the whole deck himself.
  3. They were proud of their daughter for saying something.
  4. I'm proud to be part of the project.
  5. The town takes pride in the fact that nothing here has ever burned down.
See also

proximal to, distal to#

Pronunciation
/ˈprɑksəməl tu, ˈdɪstəl tu/ · PROK-suh-mul too, DISS-tul too · weak /ˈprɑksəməl tə/ and /ˈdɪstəl tə/
Русский
проксимальный / дистальный по отношению к — анат., ближе к месту прикрепления или дальше
Type
paired adjectives + preposition
Register
technical — anatomy, surgery, dentistry, and gastroenterology; an American meets them in an operative report or a physical-therapy note and nowhere else
Complement
a noun phrase naming the reference point on the body or along a structure — never a gerund and never a clause. The pair is relative: everything is proximal or distal to something, so the complement is obligatory unless the reference point is understood (the distal end).
Pattern
[structure] be proximal to [structure] · [structure] be distal to [structure] · just proximal to / immediately distal to · the proximal / distal end — attributive, complement understood
Variants
nearer to and farther from are the plain equivalents; upstream of and downstream of do the same job for vessels, bowel, and pipelines; central and peripheral cover the same idea for the body as a whole.
Contrast
Proximal means nearer the trunk or the point of origin; distal means farther from it, so the elbow is proximal to the wrist and the wrist is distal to the elbow. The pair runs along a limb or a tube, unlike anterior to, posterior to, which runs front to back. Outside anatomy, proximal* has a live use in psychology (proximal cause, the zone of proximal development), and in law the near-homophone is proximate cause — a different word with an -ate ending. ✗ proximal from and ✗ distal from are errors: both take to.

Nearer to the point of attachment, or farther from it, along a limb or a tube.

  1. The fracture is just proximal to the wrist.
  2. The stent sits distal to the narrowing.
  3. Sensation is intact everywhere distal to the injury.
  4. The elbow is proximal to the forearm and distal to the shoulder.
  5. Bleeding was found distal to the anastomosis.
See also
anterior to, posterior to, adjacent to, close to, native to, to

punctual for#

Pronunciation
/ˈpʌŋktʃuəl fɔr/ · PUNK-choo-ull for · weak /ˈpʌŋktʃuəl fər/
Русский
приходящий точно к назначенному времени
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; American speech says on time
Complement
a noun naming an appointment or event after for (punctual for appointments) — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. After in, a gerund naming what is done on time (punctual in paying).
Pattern
be punctual for [event] · be punctual in [verb-ing] · be punctual about [noun / verb-ing] · punctual alone: she is always punctual
Variants
on time for is the ordinary American equivalent and is far commoner; prompt about / in covers the same ground in business writing.
Contrast
Punctual for an event; punctual in or about an activity. The word is the positive counterpart of late for* and takes the same for. In American English it is markedly formal — a manager writes punctual and dependable in a review but says she's always on time out loud — so choosing it in casual writing sounds stiff. ✗ punctual to is an error, and ✗ punctual at is an error except with a clock time: punctual at nine is possible but odd.

Arriving or acting exactly at the appointed time.

  1. He is unfailingly punctual for every shift.
  2. The trains here are punctual for about eight months of the year.
  3. She's punctual in answering even the tedious emails.
  4. Guests are asked to be punctual for the six o'clock seating.
  5. Being punctual for the first meeting bought him a lot of goodwill.

ready for#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛdi fɔr/ · RED-ee for · weak /ˈrɛdi fər/
Русский
готовый к чему-л. — подготовленный, согласный или вот-вот приступающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun naming what is coming after for (ready for the season) — a gerund after for is uncommon and reads as heavy. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (ready to go), which is the frame for willingness or imminence. Never a to-infinitive after for.
Pattern
be ready for [event / thing] · be ready to [verb] · get ready for [event] · be ready with [something prepared in hand] · ready when you are
Variants
prepared for / to is the formal twin and splits the same way; set for and all set to are the informal ones.
Contrast
Ready for takes a thing; ready to takes an action. ✓ ready for the interview*, ✓ ready to answer, ✗ ready for answering in most contexts. Note that ready to has a second, non-volitional sense meaning on the point of — ready to collapse does not mean the building is willing. Ready with is a third frame naming something held in reserve: ready with an excuse. ✗ ready of is an error.

Prepared, willing, or on the point of something.

  1. The field won't be ready for play until Thursday.
  2. She's ready to sign as soon as the numbers come back.
  3. Get the kids ready for school; the bus moved up ten minutes.
  4. He was ready with an answer before anyone finished asking.
  5. That transmission looks ready to give out any day now.

related to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈleɪtɪd tu/ · rih-LAY-tid too · weak /rɪˈleɪtɪd tə/
Русский
связанный с — родством, причинно или по теме
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun; a gerund where an activity is meant (costs related to running the shop) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
[X] is related to [Y] · closely / distantly related to [noun] · [noun]-related: weather-related delays · relate to [something] — the verb, meaning to identify with · in relation to [noun]
Variants
connected to / with, linked to, and tied to are the neighbors; akin to is the loose literary one.
Contrast
Related to covers kinship, causal connection, and topical relevance with one preposition. ✗ related with* is an error, and it is a common one because connected with is correct. Keep the adjective apart from the verb relate to meaning to sympathize (I can relate to that), which is informal American English and says something about the speaker rather than the two things. The compound suffix -related has become the compact newsroom form: a heat-related death.

Connected — by family, by cause, or by subject matter.

  1. She's related to half the families on that road.
  2. The two outages turned out not to be related to each other.
  3. Anything related to payroll goes through the third floor.
  4. Costs related to running the second location doubled in a year.
  5. Sales are up, though not for any reason related to the campaign.

relevant to#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛləvənt tu/ · REL-uh-vunt too · weak /ˈrɛləvənt tə/
Русский
имеющий значение для, относящийся к обсуждаемому
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun; a gerund where an activity is meant (relevant to deciding the appeal) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
[X] is relevant to [Y] · directly / hardly / no longer relevant to [noun] · relevance to [noun] — the noun keeps to · relevant for [purpose] — less common
Variants
pertinent to and germane to are the formal equivalents, both with to; bear on is the verb version (that bears on the question).
Contrast
Relevant takes to*, never for in the ordinary sense and never with. ✗ relevant with the discussion* is an error; ✗ relevant of is an error. Relevant to the case means bearing on it; relevant for the case is used occasionally to mean useful for that purpose, but to is the safe default. Compare related to, which asserts a connection of any kind, against relevant to, which asserts that the connection matters for the question at hand.

Bearing on the matter under discussion, in a way that matters.

  1. None of that is relevant to whether the permit was filed on time.
  2. The judge ruled the testimony relevant to the second count.
  3. Half the syllabus is no longer relevant to how the work is actually done.
  4. Anything relevant to the audit should be flagged.
  5. Her earlier experience is directly relevant to this job.

reliant on#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈlaɪənt ɑn/ · rih-LY-unt on · weak /rɪˈlaɪənt ən/
Русский
зависящий от чего-л., полагающийся на
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (reliant on getting the grant renewed) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
be reliant on [noun / verb-ing] · be reliant upon [noun] · heavily / increasingly / overly reliant on [noun] · self-reliant (no complement) · rely on [someone] to [verb] — the verb frame
Variants
reliant upon is the formal equivalent; dependent on is the everyday twin and is far commoner.
Contrast
Reliant on, like dependent on, takes on — never ✗ reliant of or ✗ reliant to. The difference from dependent on* is one of tone: dependent can suggest weakness or need, while reliant is neutral about the relationship and is the word chosen in policy and technical writing (reliant on imported grain). Note the verb rely on takes an infinitive after a person — rely on him to lock up — and that this frame belongs to the verb, not the adjective: ✗ reliant on him to lock up is odd.

Needing something in order to function.

  1. The region is heavily reliant on a single employer.
  2. The whole system is reliant on one aging server.
  3. Rural clinics are reliant on volunteers for weekend coverage.
  4. She's reliant on the bus until the car is fixed.
  5. We've become reliant upon forecasts that were never that accurate.

reluctant to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈlʌktənt tu/ · rih-LUCK-tunt too · weak /rɪˈlʌktənt tə/
Русский
неохотно делающий что-л., не желающий — после to инфинитив, а не герундий
Type
adjective + infinitive marker — the counterexample in this file
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a bare to-infinitive (reluctant to sign) — and here the to is not a preposition, so ✗ reluctant to signing is the error. Apply the noun test: ✗ reluctant to the signature fails, which proves the to is an infinitive marker. The adjective takes about + gerund for the prepositional frame: reluctant about signing.
Pattern
be reluctant to [verb] · be reluctant about [noun / verb-ing] · reluctantly [verb] — the adverb is commoner than the about frame
Variants
unwilling to, hesitant to, loath to, and disinclined to all take the infinitive the same way; averse to takes a gerund and is the odd one out of that set.
Contrast
This entry exists to mark the boundary. Reluctant to, hesitant to, unwilling to, ready to, able to, likely to, and about to all take a bare verb — the to is an infinitive marker. Accustomed to, used to, opposed to, averse to, committed to, dedicated to, devoted to, subject to, and look forward to all take a gerund — the to is a preposition. There is no way to tell from the spelling, and the noun test is the only reliable check: if to the noise is grammatical, write -ing. Note the near-pair averse to doing versus reluctant to do, which mean the same thing and take opposite complements.

Unwilling to do something, and showing it.

  1. The bank was reluctant to extend the line of credit.
  2. She's reluctant to commit before seeing the final numbers.
  3. Witnesses were reluctant to say anything on the record.
  4. He was reluctant about the whole arrangement from the start.
  5. Nobody is reluctant to take the credit once it works.

replete with#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈplit wɪθ/ · rih-PLEET with · weak /rəˈplit wəθ/
Русский
изобилующий чем-л., до краёв полный — книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to literary; a reviewer's and a judge's word (the record is replete with examples), and it sounds ornate in speech
Complement
a noun phrase naming what the thing is full of, usually plural or mass — never a gerund and never a clause. The complement may be good or bad, which is what separates the word from fraught with.
Pattern
be replete with [examples / detail / errors] · the record is replete with [noun] — the standing judicial phrase · replete — the rare bare use, meaning full up
Variants
full of is the plain everyday word; rife with is negative and journalistic; fraught with is negative and about danger; teeming with is about living things; abounding in is the literary variant with a different preposition.
Contrast
Against fraught with, which is always negative, replete with is neutral and often admiring — a book replete with detail is a compliment, a process fraught with detail is not. Against full of, the difference is register alone: replete with is what full of wears to a hearing. Note the older sense of replete meaning "having eaten enough," which survives in repletion in clinical writing. ✗ replete of and ✗ replete in are errors.

Abundantly supplied with something — filled up with it.

  1. The record is replete with warnings the agency chose to ignore.
  2. Her first novel is replete with the kind of detail only a nurse would know.
  3. The manual is replete with diagrams and short on plain instructions.
  4. That period of the archive is replete with letters from the same three families.
  5. The report is replete with qualifications, which is how you know it was lawyered.

representative of#

Pronunciation
/ˌrɛprɪˈzɛntətɪv ʌv/ · rep-rih-ZEN-tuh-tiv uv · weak /ˌrɛprəˈzɛnədəv əv/, with both t's flapped in fast speech
Русский
показательный для, представляющий собой целое в миниатюре
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to technical; standard in survey research, sampling, and criticism
Complement
a noun phrase naming the whole that the sample or example stands for — never a gerund and never a clause. What follows of is the population, not the trait.
Pattern
be representative of [population / body of work] · broadly / hardly representative of · a representative sample of [noun] · a representative of [organization] — the noun, which looks identical and means a delegate
Variants
typical of is the everyday equivalent; illustrative of claims only that it makes the point; emblematic of is the loaded literary word; characteristic of looks at the trait rather than the sample.
Contrast
Representative of claims the example resembles the whole in the ways that matter — which is a statistical claim when the whole is a population, and a critical judgment when it is a body of work. Against typical of, which says something is what you would expect from a group, representative of* says it can stand in for the group. Watch the identical-looking noun: a representative of the union is a person, and confusion between the two is easy in a sentence like she is a representative of the district versus her district is representative of the state. ✗ representative for is an error in this sense, though a representative for the company exists as a job title.

Standing in for a larger whole because it resembles it in the relevant ways.

  1. The sample isn't representative of the district's actual age spread.
  2. These three cases are representative of the hundred we reviewed.
  3. That painting is not representative of his later work at all.
  4. Weekend traffic is not representative of what the intersection sees at rush hour.
  5. The panel was chosen to be representative of the counties, not of the parties.
See also
typical of, illustrative of, characteristic of, indicative of, of

repugnant to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈpʌɡnənt tu/ · rih-PUG-nunt too · weak /rəˈpʌɡnənt tə/ — the g is pronounced, unlike in impugn
Русский
противоречащий высшей норме (юр.); разг. — глубоко отвратительный кому-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
two registers, and both are formal: a legal one meaning "inconsistent with and therefore void" (a statute repugnant to the Constitution), and a moral one meaning "disgusting to"
Complement
a noun phrase naming the higher authority or the offended party — never a gerund in the legal sense; the moral sense allows a gerund as subject rather than complement (being asked to lie was repugnant to her). No clause follows.
Pattern
[statute / clause] be repugnant to [constitution / statute] · [conduct] be repugnant to [person / conscience] · morally repugnant to · repugnant — the bare adjective, meaning disgusting
Variants
inconsistent with and contrary to are the neutral legal equivalents; offensive to and abhorrent to cover the moral sense; incompatible with is the general word.
Contrast
In law the word means "cannot stand with," not "disgusting" — Marbury's famous formula is that a law repugnant to the Constitution is void, and no revulsion is implied. In ordinary use the moral sense has taken over so completely that the legal one now reads as a term of art. Against consonant with and compatible with, repugnant to is the direct opposite in the legal sense. Note that the bare adjective needs no complement at all in the moral sense: a repugnant argument. ✗ repugnant with and ✗ repugnant for are errors.

Irreconcilable with a higher rule — or, in the everyday sense, deeply offensive to somebody.

  1. A local ordinance repugnant to state law cannot be enforced.
  2. The clause is repugnant to the granting language earlier in the deed.
  3. The practice was repugnant to every doctor who testified.
  4. Nothing in the settlement is repugnant to the court's earlier order.
  5. He found the whole arrangement repugnant to his sense of the job.
See also
consonant with, adverse to, compatible with, opposed to, to

responsible for#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈspɑnsəbəl fɔr/ · rih-SPON-suh-bull for · weak /rɪˈspɑnsəbəl fər/
Русский
ответственный за; виновник чего-л.; responsible to — подотчётный кому-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after for (responsible for closing up) — never a to-infinitive after for. ✗ responsible to close up changes the meaning entirely, because responsible to takes a person you answer to.
Pattern
be responsible for [duty / outcome / verb-ing] · be responsible to [person or body one answers to] · hold [someone] responsible for [noun] · personally responsible for [noun] · responsibility for [noun] — the noun keeps for
Variants
accountable for a result and accountable to a person split exactly the same way; in charge of covers the duty sense; to blame for covers the fault sense.
Contrast
Responsible for names the job or the fault; responsible to names the boss. She's responsible for scheduling* is her duty; She's responsible to the board is her reporting line. Mixing them produces a real misunderstanding rather than a stylistic slip. Note the two senses of the for frame — duty (responsible for the night shift) and culpability (responsible for the leak) — which are distinguished only by context, and the bare adjective meaning trustworthy (a responsible adult), which takes no preposition at all.

Having a duty for something, having caused something, or answering to someone.

  1. She's responsible for everything that leaves the loading dock.
  2. Faulty wiring was responsible for the outage.
  3. The director is responsible to a seven-member board.
  4. Each department is responsible for keeping its own inventory.
  5. Nobody wanted to be held responsible for the decision afterward.
See also
guilty of, innocent of, subject to, for

rich in#

Pronunciation
/rɪtʃ ɪn/ · RICH in · weak /rɪtʃ ən/
Русский
богатый чем-л., с высоким содержанием чего-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; common in nutrition, geology, and description
Complement
a mass noun naming what there is a lot of (rich in iron, rich in detail) — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
be rich in [substance / quality] · [noun] rich in [noun] · [noun]-rich: protein-rich, nutrient-rich · rich with [something abundant and sensory] · rich alone: a rich family
Variants
high in is the everyday nutrition version (high in sodium); abundant in and replete with are the formal ones; loaded with is the informal one.
Contrast
Rich in is the standard; rich with is possible when the abundance is sensory or atmospheric — air rich with woodsmoke* — and it is the more literary choice. ✗ rich of and ✗ rich on are errors. The exact opposite is poor in (soil poor in nitrogen), which is worth learning as a pair, since the antonym keeps the same preposition. Note that the plain sense of rich — having money — takes no preposition.

Containing a great deal of something.

  1. Kale is rich in vitamin K and nobody is happy about it.
  2. The valley is rich in iron ore and short of everything else.
  3. Her account is rich in the kind of detail that can't be invented.
  4. The soil along the creek is rich in organic matter.
  5. The town's history is rich in feuds and thin on documents.
See also
full of, poor at, crowded with, lacking in, in

rude to#

Pronunciation
/rud tu/ · ROOD too · weak /rud tə/
Русский
грубый с кем-л., невежливый
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person after to — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive. The judgment frame takes of plus a doer and an infinitive: It was rude of her to interrupt.
Pattern
be rude to [person] · it is rude to [verb] · it was rude of [someone] to [verb] · rude about [something] — insulting concerning
Variants
impolite to, discourteous to, and disrespectful toward are the formal ladder; nasty to and short with are the informal ones — note that short with takes with.
Contrast
Rude to the person, rude about the subject. She was rude to the waiter* is how she treated him; She was rude about the food is what she said concerning it. The of-frame assigns blame to the doer: It was rude of him to leave without saying goodbye. ✗ rude with is an error, though short with and sharp with, which mean something close, both take with.

Behaving in a way that offends ordinary good manners.

  1. He was rude to the receptionist and wondered why the appointment slipped.
  2. It's rude to check your phone while someone is answering your question.
  3. She was rude about the neighbors within earshot of the neighbors.
  4. It was rude of them to leave before the toast.
  5. Nobody there was rude to us; they were just impossible to reach.
See also

sad about#

Pronunciation
/sæd əˈbaʊt/ · SAD uh-BOWT · weak /sæd əˈbaʊt/
Русский
огорчённый чем-л., которому грустно из-за
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about (sad about leaving) — never a to-infinitive after about. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (sad to see it go) and a that-clause (sad that nobody came).
Pattern
be sad about [noun / verb-ing] · be sad to [verb] · be sad (that) [clause] · be sad for [someone] · sad alone, of a thing: a sad story
Variants
upset about is the more agitated version; sorry about the apologetic one; heartbroken over the intensified one, which takes over.
Contrast
Sad about names the situation; sad for names the person you feel it on behalf of; sad to + verb reports the reaction as it happens. Sad about the closing* is the fact; sad for the staff is on their behalf; sad to hear it is the moment of learning. ✗ sad of and ✗ sad with are errors. Note the useful distinction from sorry about, which carries either apology or sympathy — I'm sorry about your father is condolence, while I'm sad about your father would be an odd thing to say to a mourner, because it centers the speaker.

Unhappy about something.

  1. She's sad about leaving the house more than about leaving the town.
  2. Everyone was sad about the way the season ended.
  3. I'm sad for him; he put four years into that program.
  4. It was sad to see the place boarded up.
  5. He's sad about not having asked more questions while he could.

safe from#

Pronunciation
/seɪf frʌm/ · SAYF from · weak /seɪf frəm/
Русский
защищённый от, вне досягаемости опасности
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun naming the danger escaped after from — a gerund is possible but rare. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive in a different frame (safe to drink, safe to assume), which describes the thing rather than the person. Never a to-infinitive after from.
Pattern
be safe from [danger] · be safe to [verb] · keep [someone] safe from [danger] · safe for [a use or a group]: safe for kids · safe with [a person entrusted]: the secret is safe with me
Variants
secure from and protected from / against are the formal equivalents; out of reach of is the concrete one.
Contrast
Four frames, four jobs. Safe from the threat; safe for the user or purpose (safe for pets); safe with the person you trusted; safe to + verb about what may be done (safe to eat). Substituting one changes the sentence: safe for children means it will not hurt them, while safe from children means they cannot get at it — and both sentences occur in real life. ✗ safe of is an error.

Out of reach of some danger.

  1. The files are safe from anything short of a fire.
  2. Nothing on that block was safe from the reassessment.
  3. Keep the seedlings safe from frost for another two weeks.
  4. It's safe to assume the meeting will run long.
  5. Your secret is safe with me, which people say and rarely mean.

satisfied with#

Pronunciation
/ˈsætɪsfaɪd wɪθ/ · SAT-iss-fyd with · weak /ˈsætɪsfaɪd wəθ/
Русский
удовлетворённый чем-л.; в офиц. смысле — убеждённый в чём-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after with (satisfied with letting it stand) — never a to-infinitive after with. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective in the "convinced" sense: satisfied that the accounts were in order.
Pattern
be satisfied with [thing assessed] · be satisfied (that) [clause] · be satisfied by [the thing that satisfies] · satisfy [someone] — the verb, no preposition · satisfaction with [noun] — the noun keeps with
Variants
happy with and pleased with are the everyday twins; content with is the quieter one; okay with is the conversational one.
Contrast
Satisfied with means content; satisfied that means convinced. The second sense is the language of inspectors and judges: satisfied that the requirements have been met* is a finding, not a mood. Satisfied by points at the cause, and it is also the frame used for requirements: the condition was satisfied by the second payment. ✗ satisfied of is an error in modern American English, though satisfied of the truth of it survives in legal prose.

Content with what was received or done — or, in the formal sense, convinced.

  1. The customer was satisfied with the replacement and dropped the complaint.
  2. She wasn't satisfied with the first three explanations.
  3. The inspector was satisfied that the repairs met code.
  4. He seems satisfied with the arrangement, or at least done arguing about it.
  5. Nobody is ever entirely satisfied with a compromise, which is how you know it worked.

scared of#

Pronunciation
/skɛrd ʌv/ · SKAIRD uv · weak /skɛrd əv/
Русский
боящийся, испуганный чем-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; the everyday American word
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (scared of falling) after of; a noun after by — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (scared to look), a separate frame.
Pattern
be scared of [noun / verb-ing] · be scared by [the event that caused it] · be scared to [verb] · be scared for [someone] · scared stiff / scared to death · scare [someone] (no preposition on the object)
Variants
afraid of is the neutral twin; frightened of the slightly formal one; terrified of and petrified of the intensified ones.
Contrast
Scared of is the standing fear, scared by the single event, scared to the hesitation to act. Scared of the dark* is general; scared by the noise was one moment; scared to open it is not doing something. All three are correct and they are not interchangeable. ✗ scared from is an error. Register is the only thing separating scared of from afraid of: the first is what Americans say, the second is what they write.

Feeling fear.

  1. He's scared of heights and took the job on the ninth floor anyway.
  2. She was scared by how quickly the water came up.
  3. I was scared to ask what the estimate was.
  4. They were scared for the crew still out on the water.
  5. Everybody's a little scared of the first day.

seized of, seised of#

Pronunciation
/sizd ʌv/ · SEEZD uv · weak /sizd əv/ — both spellings are pronounced the same
Русский
юр. владеющий недвижимостью на законном праве; о суде — принявший дело к рассмотрению
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
legal and archaic-legal; the seised spelling is the traditional one in real-property law, and an American meets either only in a deed, a title opinion, or a court's statement that it has a matter in hand
Complement
a noun phrase naming land, an estate, or a matter before a tribunal — never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
be seized of [land / estate] — the property sense · be seised of [an estate in fee simple] — the conveyancing spelling · the court is seized of [the matter] — the jurisdiction sense, borrowed from international practice · seized with [a matter] — a variant of the second sense
Variants
seised of is the same phrase in the older spelling kept by property lawyers to distinguish it from ordinary seizing; possessed of is the neighboring formal phrase; in possession of is the modern equivalent; have jurisdiction over replaces the tribunal sense in American courts, which more often say the matter is before the court.
Contrast
Seized of means lawfully holding, which is nearly the opposite of what the everyday verb suggests — police seize property from you, but a landowner is seized of an estate in it. The tribunal sense (the court is seized of the matter*) is common in international and European practice and is used in America chiefly by arbitrators and by writers with a foreign audience. Against possessed of, which covers qualities as well as property, seized of is confined to estates in land and to matters before a tribunal. ✗ seized to is not English, and seized by means the ordinary taking.

Lawfully holding an estate in land — or, of a tribunal, having a matter properly in hand.

  1. The grantor was seized of an estate in fee simple at the time of the conveyance.
  2. Title showed the decedent seised of the eastern half only.
  3. The tribunal is seized of the dispute and has set a briefing schedule.
  4. Anyone seized of land subject to the covenant takes it with the burden.
  5. The abstract recites that she died seized of the property.
See also
possessed of, vested with, appurtenant to, entitled to, of

Pronunciation
/ˈsɛnsətɪv tu/ · SEN-suh-tiv too · weak /ˈsɛnsətɪv tə/
Русский
чувствительный к — физически, эмоционально или деликатный по отношению к
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun after to; noun phrase or gerund after about (sensitive about being asked her age) — never a to-infinitive after either.
Pattern

Variants
responsive to covers the neutral technical sense; touchy about is the informal version of sensitive about; allergic to is the medical one.
Contrast
Sensitive to points outward; sensitive about points inward. Sensitive to criticism* means it affects you; sensitive about your accent means you don't want it mentioned. A third sense is purely physical — sensitive to light, sensitive to penicillin — and a fourth is approving: sensitive to the concerns of the neighborhood means considerate, not fragile. ✗ sensitive of is an error, though sensitive of you to notice appears occasionally and is better rendered thoughtful of you.

Reacting readily to something — physically, emotionally, or considerately.

  1. The film is sensitive to even a few seconds of daylight.
  2. He's sensitive about how long it took him to finish school.
  3. The proposal is sensitive to small changes in the discount rate.
  4. She's sensitive to the fact that half the staff has been here longer.
  5. Some patients are sensitive to the dye used in the scan.

serious about#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɪriəs əˈbaʊt/ · SEER-ee-uss uh-BOWT · weak /ˈsɪriəs əˈbaʊt/
Русский
всерьёз настроенный на что-л., не шутящий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (serious about going back to school) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ serious to go back is an error.
Pattern

Variants
in earnest about is the formal one; committed to takes to and a gerund; not kidding about is the conversational one.
Contrast
Serious about + -ing versus determined to + verb is the usual pairing: same idea, opposite complements. Note the relationship sense — serious about her, meaning committed romantically — which is one of the few cases where the object of about is a person. Do not confuse the adjective with serious meaning grave (a serious injury), which takes no preposition at all. ✗ serious in is an error, though serious in tone and serious in intent are correct phrases where in names the respect.

Genuinely intending something, and not joking about it.

  1. She's serious about moving the whole operation to the coast.
  2. If you're serious about the offer, put it in writing.

shocked at#

Pronunciation
/ʃɑkt æt/ · SHOKT at · weak /ʃɑkt ət/
Русский
потрясённый, шокированный чем-л. неожиданным
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at and by (shocked at finding the door open) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (shocked to learn) and a that-clause (shocked that nobody objected).
Pattern
be shocked at [noun / verb-ing] · be shocked by [the cause] · be shocked to [verb] · be shocked (that) [clause] · shocked into [verb-ing] · shock [someone] (no preposition on the object)
Variants
appalled at / by and horrified at / by are the stronger versions with the same split; taken aback by is the milder one.
Contrast
Same at / by split as surprised and astonished: at frames it as your reaction, by frames it as the thing acting on you. Shocked at the price is a judgment; shocked by the news is an effect. Shocked to learn reports the moment. The literal electrical sense takes by only (shocked by a live wire), which is a useful anchor for remembering which preposition means "acted on by." ✗ shocked of and ✗ shocked with are errors.

Startled and upset by something unexpected, usually something bad.

  1. Neighbors were shocked at how fast the building came down.
  2. She was shocked by the tone of the letter.
  3. I was shocked to find out he'd been gone since March.
  4. The board was shocked at the size of the shortfall.
  5. He was shocked into silence, which for him was remarkable.

short of#

Pronunciation
/ʃɔrt ʌv/ · SHORT uv · weak /ʃɔrt əv/
Русский
испытывающий нехватку чего-л.; во втором значении — кроме, разве что не (+ герундий)
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase after of in the "lacking" sense; noun phrase or gerund in the "except" sense (short of rebuilding the whole thing) — never a to-infinitive. After on, a noun naming the supply that is low.
Pattern
be short of [something needed] · be short on [supply] · short of [verb-ing] — meaning "except by" · fall short of [standard] · nothing short of [noun] · stop short of [verb-ing]
Variants
low on and out of are the everyday American versions of short on and short of; wanting in and deficient in are the formal ones and take in.
Contrast
Short of has two distinct senses in the same shape. The first is lacking: short of cash*. The second is "anything except": Short of selling the building, I don't see a way out — and this second sense takes a gerund and is genuinely useful. Short on is the near-twin used with supplies and qualities: short on patience. ✗ short in is an error for both senses, though short in stature is a fixed phrase about height. The idioms fall short of, stop short of, and nothing short of all keep of.

Not having enough of something — or, in the second sense, anything other than.

  1. We're short of volunteers for the Saturday shift.
  2. He's short on patience and long on opinions.
  3. Short of rebuilding the retaining wall, there's no permanent fix.
  4. The count came up two short of what the manifest said.
  5. The performance fell short of what the reviews had promised.

/sɪk ʌv/ · SIK uv · weak /sɪk əv/

Type

Register

Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (sick of hearing about it) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern

Variants
tired of is the calmer twin; fed up with the equivalent with a different preposition; done with the flat American one.

Thoroughly tired of something and out of patience with it.

  1. I'm sick of explaining the same policy every August.

similar to#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɪmələr tu/ · SIM-uh-lur too · weak /ˈsɪmələr tə/
Русский

Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (similar to working from home, but with worse coffee) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. A free relative works: similar to what we saw last year.

[X] is similar to [Y] · broadly / strikingly similar to [noun] · similar in [respect] to [noun] · similarly [adjective] · similarity to / between — the noun takes both

skilled at#

Pronunciation
/skɪld æt/ · SKILD at · weak /skɪld ət/
Русский
умелый в чём-л., владеющий навыком
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (skilled at defusing arguments) and after in (skilled in negotiation) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ skilled to negotiate is an error.
Pattern
be skilled at [practice / verb-ing] · be skilled in [field or discipline] · highly skilled at [verb-ing] · skilled labor (no preposition)
Variants
proficient in, adept at, practiced at, accomplished in, and versed in fill out the same range; good at is the plain everyday version.
Contrast
At takes the practice, in takes the field. Skilled at soldering* is what you do with your hands; skilled in electrical engineering is a body of knowledge. The distinction is soft — both are correct in most sentences — but in sounds credentialed and at sounds practical, which is why résumés prefer in and recommendations prefer at. ✗ skilled on and ✗ skilled of are errors. The parallel adjectives expert, good, bad, hopeless, and terrible all take at for practices.

Having developed ability in some practice or field.

  1. She's skilled at getting two departments to agree on a date.
  2. He's skilled in both civil and criminal procedure.
  3. The crew is skilled at working around weather delays.
  4. We need someone skilled in structural inspection, not just licensed.
  5. He's skilled at the part of the job nobody writes down.

slow at#

Pronunciation
/sloʊ æt/ · SLOH at · weak /sloʊ ət/
Русский
медлительный в чём-л. (о навыке); slow to — не спешащий сделать что-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (slow at reading music) and after in (slow in responding, the formal frame). The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive meaning "not quick to" (slow to anger, slow to respond), which is the commoner frame in writing. Never a to-infinitive after at.
Pattern
be slow at [skill / verb-ing] · be slow to [verb] · be slow in [verb-ing] · slow on the uptake · slow alone, of business: sales are slow
Variants
not quick to is the plain paraphrase of slow to; behind on covers the schedule sense.
Contrast
Slow at names a skill you perform slowly; slow to names a reaction you are late in having. Slow at arithmetic* is about ability; slow to complain is about temperament, and it is one of the small set of adjectives — with quick to, ready to, eager to — where an infinitive follows directly. Slow in + -ing is the formal alternative to slow to: slow in acknowledging receipt. ✗ slow of is an error except in the fossil slow of speech.

Taking longer than others do — at a task, or in reacting.

  1. He's slow at mental arithmetic and unbeatable on paper.
  2. She's slow to take offense, which is why people tell her things.
  3. The office was slow in processing the refund.
  4. I'm slow at anything involving a keyboard shortcut.
  5. The county has been slow to release the inspection reports.

sorry for#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɑri fɔr/ · SAH-ree for · weak /ˈsɑri fər/
Русский
виноватый, сожалеющий о своём поступке; sorry for someone — жалеющий кого-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a person after for in the pity sense; a noun phrase or gerund after for and about in the apology sense (sorry for keeping you waiting) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (sorry to bother you) and a that-clause (sorry that it came to this).
Pattern
be sorry for [person] — pity · be sorry for [verb-ing] — apology · be sorry about [thing] · be sorry to [verb] · be sorry (that) [clause] · feel sorry for [person] · sorry alone, as an apology
Variants
apologize for + gerund is the formal verb frame; regret takes a direct object or a gerund with no preposition at all (regret saying it).
Contrast
The three frames say three different things, and mixing them is a genuine social error. Sorry for you is pity — often unwelcome. Sorry about your father is condolence. Sorry for interrupting is an apology for something you did. And sorry to hear that is a reaction to news. So I'm sorry for you and I'm sorry about that are not interchangeable, and the first can insult where the second consoles. ✗ sorry of is an error; sorry for + gerund and sorry about + gerund are both fine for apologies, with for slightly the more contrite.

Regretful about something you did, or sympathetic about something that happened.

  1. I'm sorry for keeping you waiting; the earlier meeting ran long.
  2. She was sorry about the whole misunderstanding.
  3. Don't feel sorry for him; he chose every part of this.
  4. I'm sorry to hear about the diagnosis.
  5. He was sorry that he'd ever mentioned it.
See also
ashamed of, guilty of, sad about, grateful to, for

/spɪˈsɪfɪk tu/ · spuh-SIF-ik too · weak /spəˈsɪfɪk tə/
Русский
характерный только для данного случая, специфичный для
