neutral to technical; the modern workhorse for "belonging only to," common in medicine, software, and policy writing
Complement
a noun phrase naming the group, place, context, or case — or a gerund where the context is an activity (specific to running the night shift). Never a to-infinitive: ✗ specific to run the night shift.
Pattern
be specific to [group / context] · [noun] specific to [noun] · not specific to [noun] — the common denial · site-specific, species-specific, context-specific — the hyphenated compounds, where the complement moves in front · be specific about [noun] — the unrelated sense, "give details"
Variants
peculiar to is the formal equivalent; unique to is the strongest; particular to is a close synonym; restricted to and confined to say the same thing about range.
Contrast
Two different adjectives share this spelling. Specific to + noun means "belonging only to": a symptom specific to the disease. Specific about + noun means "giving detail": be specific about the dates. The prepositions keep them apart and are not interchangeable — ✗ be specific to the dates asks for something else entirely. Against peculiar to, which is formal and slightly literary, specific to is the neutral modern choice. In medicine the pair sensitive and specific are terms of art about tests, and there specific takes no complement at all.

Belonging to one context, group, or case and not to others.

  1. The error is specific to the Windows build and doesn't appear on a Mac.
  2. That rule is specific to districts with fewer than five hundred students.
  3. The training is specific to the machines on this floor.
  4. Symptoms like these are not specific to any one condition.
  5. The permit conditions are specific to the parcel and don't travel.
See also
peculiar to, native to, endemic to, common to, to

stuck with, stuck in, stuck on#

Pronunciation
/stʌk wɪθ, stʌk ɪn, stʌk ɑn/ · STUCK with, STUCK in, STUCK on · weak /stʌk wəθ/, /stʌk ən/, /stʌk ən/ — in and on both reduce to /ən/, so stuck in traffic and stuck on a problem sound alike in the unstressed syllable and are told apart by the noun
Русский
застрявший — with с обузой, in в месте, on на задаче
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; thoroughly American conversational English, and common in writing that wants a spoken texture
Complement
after with, a noun phrase naming the burden you cannot get rid of — and a gerund (stuck with paying for it). After in, a noun naming the place or condition you cannot get out of, or a gerund (stuck in waiting for approval is possible but marginal). After on, a noun naming the problem you cannot get past or the person you are infatuated with. No to-infinitive after any of the three: ✗ stuck with to pay. The bare frame stuck + -ing with no preposition (stuck waiting an hour) is also standard.
Pattern
be stuck with [bill / responsibility / verb-ing] · be stuck in [traffic / a job / a rut] · be stuck on [a problem / question / person] · get stuck [verb-ing] — no preposition · be stuck at [a number] — a fourth, quantitative frame
Variants
saddled with and left holding the bag replace stuck with; bogged down in replaces stuck in; hung up on is the near twin of stuck on in both its senses.
Contrast
The three prepositions carve up the idea cleanly. Stuck with is about a burden you did not want (stuck with the bill); stuck in is about a place or state you cannot leave (stuck in traffic, stuck in a bad contract); stuck on is about a point you cannot get past (stuck on question four) or, in the older slang, a person you cannot stop thinking about (he's been stuck on her since high school). Note that stuck with takes people too, and then it is rude: stuck with my brother-in-law for the weekend. ✗ stuck to means physically adhering (gum stuck to the seat) and is a different pattern altogether.

Unable to get free of something — a burden, a place, or a problem, depending on the preposition.

  1. We got stuck with the catering bill when the sponsor pulled out.
  2. She's been stuck in traffic on the bridge for forty minutes.
  3. I'm stuck on the third question and the rest went fine.
  4. He's stuck in a job he outgrew four years ago.
  5. They were stuck with explaining the whole thing to the board twice.
See also
fed up with, tired of, involved in, encumbered by, encumbered with, with

subject to#

Pronunciation
/ˈsʌbdʒɪkt tu/ · SUB-jikt too · weak /ˈsʌbdʒɪkt tə/ — the adjective stresses the first syllable, unlike the verb subject /səbˈdʒɛkt/
Русский
при условии чего-л.; подверженный чему-л.; подчинённый чему-л.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; the standard idiom of contracts, notices, and regulation
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (subject to being called back) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ subject to be reviewed is the error; write subject to review or subject to being reviewed.
Pattern
be subject to [condition / authority / liability] · [noun], subject to [condition] · subject to change without notice · subject [someone] to [something] — the verb, stressed on the second syllable
Variants
contingent on and conditional on cover the "depending on" sense and take on; liable to covers the "likely to suffer" sense; governed by covers the authority sense.
Contrast
Subject to carries three related senses, all with the same preposition. Conditional: the offer is subject to financing*. Liable to undergo: prices subject to change, subject to delays. Under the authority of: subject to state law. Distinguish the verb, which is spelled the same and stressed differently: they subjected him to three hours of questions. ✗ subject of is a different word entirely — the noun, meaning topic.

Conditional on something, liable to undergo it, or under its authority.

  1. All prices are subject to change without notice.
  2. The sale is subject to a satisfactory inspection.
  3. Flights out of that airport are subject to fog delays all spring.
  4. Contractors are subject to the same background checks as employees.
  5. The award is subject to being reviewed after the first year.
See also
dependent on, based on, responsible for, vulnerable to, to

subordinate to#

Pronunciation
/səˈbɔrdənɪt tu/ · suh-BOR-duh-nit too; the verb is suh-BOR-duh-nayt · weak /səˈbɔrdənət tə/
Русский
подчинённый чему-л., стоящий ниже по власти или значимости
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; standard in organizational writing, in finance (subordinated debt), and in grammar (a subordinate clause)
Complement
a noun phrase naming what ranks higher — a person, a body, an interest, a lien. A gerund is possible where the higher thing is an activity (subordinate to keeping the lights on), and a to-infinitive is impossible: ✗ subordinate to keep the lights on.
Pattern
be subordinate to [person / interest] · make [something] subordinate to [noun] · subordinate [a lien] to [another] — the verb, in finance · subordinated debt / a subordination agreement — the terms of art
Variants
secondary to is the neutral equivalent; inferior to ranks quality rather than authority; junior to is the finance and military word for priority; beneath and under are the plain prepositions.
Contrast
Against inferior to, which judges quality (an inferior copy), subordinate to describes rank and authority and implies no judgment about worth. In American lending the term is precise: a subordinated lien is paid after the senior one, and a subordination agreement is what makes it so — which is why a second mortgage is subordinate to the first regardless of when it was recorded. Note that in grammar a subordinate clause is one that cannot stand alone, another use with no preposition. ✗ subordinate of is an error, though a subordinate of the director exists as a noun.

Ranking below something else in authority, priority, or importance.

  1. The regional offices are subordinate to the state director.
  2. The second lien is subordinate to the bank's mortgage.
  3. Everything else in the plan is subordinate to keeping the plant open.
  4. She refuses to be subordinate to a committee that never meets.
  5. In this design, style is subordinate to legibility.
See also
inferior to, ancillary to, incidental to, superior to, to

successful in#

Pronunciation
/səkˈsɛsfəl ɪn/ · suk-SESS-full in · weak /səkˈsɛsfəl ən/
Русский
добившийся успеха в чём-л., достигший задуманного
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after in (successful in raising the money) and after at (successful at getting people to show up) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ successful to raise the money is an error; use succeeded in raising or managed to raise.
Pattern
be successful in [field / verb-ing] · be successful at [practice / verb-ing] · be successful with [a particular case or group] · succeed in [verb-ing] — the verb keeps in · success in / at — the noun follows suit
Variants
effective in / at, productive in, and does well at cover the same ground; manage to + verb is the everyday paraphrase with an infinitive.
Contrast
In leans toward the field or the endeavor; at leans toward the repeated practice. Successful in business* names a domain; successful at raising money names something done more than once. Both are correct with a gerund. The verb takes only in: ✓ succeeded in convincing them, ✗ succeeded to convince them — a very common error, since manage and try both take infinitives. ✗ successful of is an error.

Achieving what was intended.

  1. She was successful in getting the deadline moved twice.
  2. The clinic has been successful at reaching families the old program missed.
  3. He's been successful in every business he's touched except restaurants.
  4. The campaign was successful in raising the money and useless at spending it.
  5. They were successful with the first group and are repeating it in the fall.
See also
skilled at, good at, expert at, in

suggestive of#

Pronunciation
/səˈdʒɛstɪv ʌv/ · sug-JESS-tiv uv · weak /səˈdʒɛstəv əv/
Русский
наводящий на мысль о — намекающий, но не доказывающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; the natural home is a radiology report or a clinical note (findings suggestive of pneumonia), and by extension any careful analytical writing
Complement
a noun phrase naming what is hinted at, or a gerund (suggestive of having been repaired before) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ suggestive of to be repaired.
Pattern
[finding] be suggestive of [condition] · strongly / only mildly suggestive of · suggestive — used alone, where the modern meaning is risqué · suggest [conclusion] — the verb, direct object, no preposition
Variants
indicative of is the stronger word; consistent with is the radiologist's hedge that claims even less; symptomatic of names the condition outright; reminiscent of is the aesthetic cousin (a style reminiscent of the thirties).
Contrast
A ladder of confidence runs consistent with → suggestive of → indicative of → diagnostic of, and American clinicians choose among them deliberately: findings consistent with pneumonia would fit it, findings suggestive of pneumonia point toward it, findings diagnostic of it settle the matter. Watch the bare adjective: suggestive with no complement now means sexually suggestive, so the photograph was suggestive and the photograph was suggestive of an earlier repair are two different sentences and only the second is this pattern. ✗ suggestive to and ✗ suggestive for are errors.

Pointing toward a conclusion without establishing it.

  1. The films show changes suggestive of an old fracture.
  2. The pattern of withdrawals is suggestive of something other than payroll.
  3. Her account is suggestive of a much longer delay than the log shows.
  4. Wear on the threshold is suggestive of daily use, not occasional.
  5. The findings are suggestive of, though not diagnostic of, the disorder.
See also
indicative of, symptomatic of, illustrative of, predictive of, of

suitable for#

Pronunciation
/ˈsutəbəl fɔr/ · SOO-tuh-bull for · weak /ˈsutəbəl fər/
Русский
подходящий для — для цели, применения или аудитории
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a noun naming a purpose, use, or group after for; a gerund is possible (suitable for freezing) — never a to-infinitive after for. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive in the passive-ish frame suitable to serve, which is rarer.
Pattern
be suitable for [purpose / group] · be suitable to [a standard or occasion] · suitable for framing · not suitable for [group]
Variants
appropriate for and right for are the everyday equivalents; fit for is the blunter one; suited to is the close twin and takes to.
Contrast
Suitable for a purpose; suited to a role or a person. A car suitable for city driving* and a candidate well suited to the job — the pair differs by preposition and by what fills the slot, and ✗ suitable to city driving and ✗ suited for the job both drift. Note the fixed phrases: suitable for all ages, not suitable for children, suitable for framing. ✗ suitable of is an error.

Right for a particular purpose, use, or audience.

  1. The lot isn't suitable for anything heavier than a shed.
  2. The material is suitable for outdoor use in freezing weather.
  3. The film is not suitable for younger viewers.
  4. That soil is suitable for almost nothing but blueberries.
  5. She's better suited to the research side than the client side.
See also
available to, useful for, essential to, ready for, for

superior to#

Pronunciation
/səˈpɪriər tu/ · suh-PEER-ee-ur too · weak /səˈpɪriər tə/
Русский
превосходящий что-л. — выше по качеству или рангу
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
[X] is superior to [Y] · superior in [respect] to [noun] · vastly / demonstrably superior to [noun] · a superior [noun] — attributive, no preposition · feel superior to [person]
Variants
better than is the everyday equivalent and takes than, which is exactly the source of the error this entry warns about.
Contrast
Superior takes to*, never than. ✗ superior than the original* is a persistent error, and it happens because the word means "better" while behaving like a Latin comparative. The same rule covers inferior to, senior to, junior to, prior to, preferable to, anterior to, and posterior to — a family worth learning together. Note also that superior takes no more: ✗ more superior to is redundant, though it is common in speech.

Higher in quality or rank than something else.

  1. The older tooling is superior to anything available now at that price.
  2. She was superior to every other applicant on paper and in person.
  3. The second route is superior in every respect to the one we took.
  4. He has a way of sounding superior to the people he's asking for help.
  5. Nothing about the newer model is superior to the one it replaced.
See also
inferior to, equal to, similar to, than, to

sure of#

Pronunciation
/ʃʊr ʌv/ · SHOOR uv · weak /ʃʊr əv/, and in fast speech /ˈʃʊrə/
Русский
уверенный в чём-л.; в инфинитивных оборотах — непременно случится или ожидается
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral; the everyday twin of certain
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, gerund (sure of getting the spot), or a wh-clause after about (sure about what she wants) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective (sure that he'd locked it), and a bare to-infinitive attaches directly with a different meaning: sure to happen means bound to happen.
Pattern
be sure of [noun / verb-ing] · be sure about [matter] · be sure (that) [clause] · be sure to [verb] — an instruction or a prediction · make sure of [noun] · for sure
Variants
certain of / about is the more formal twin and covers every frame; positive about is the emphatic conversational one.
Contrast
Sure of is confidence; sure to is inevitability or instruction. He's sure of a spot* means he feels confident; He's sure to get a spot means it will happen; Be sure to lock up is an order. Three different things from one adjective. Of takes a fact or an outcome, about takes a matter under consideration, and about is the one that takes wh-clauses. ✗ sure for is an error.

Free of doubt — or, in the infinitive frames, bound to happen or expected to be done.

  1. I'm sure of the date; the receipt is right here.
  2. She wasn't sure about moving the whole family in January.
  3. Make sure of the measurement before you cut.
  4. Be sure to turn off the water at the street.
  5. That corner is sure to flood again in the spring.
See also
certain of, confident of, aware of, of

surprised at#

Pronunciation
/sərˈpraɪzd æt/ · sur-PRYZD at · weak /sərˈpraɪzd ət/
Русский
удивлённый чем-л. неожиданным
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at and by (surprised at finding it unlocked) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (surprised to learn) and a that-clause (surprised that anyone remembered).
Pattern
be surprised at [noun / verb-ing] · be surprised by [the cause] · be surprised to [verb] · be surprised (that) [clause] · surprise [someone] (no preposition on the object) · take [someone] by surprise
Variants
surprised at and surprised by are both standard American English; by is the commoner of the two in writing and points harder at the cause.
Contrast
At frames the reaction; by frames the cause. Surprised at how little it cost* is your reaction to a figure; surprised by the phone call is the call acting on you — which is why an event that arrives takes by and a fact you consider takes at. The infinitive frame is different again: surprised to see her there reports the moment. Note the reproachful idiom I'm surprised at you, which is a mild rebuke and cannot be rebuilt with by. ✗ surprised of and ✗ surprised from are errors.

Reacting to something you did not expect.

  1. I was surprised at how empty the place was on a Friday.
  2. She was surprised by the second call, which came at midnight.
  3. He was surprised to find the office already unlocked.
  4. Nobody was surprised that the vote was postponed again.
  5. I'm surprised at you — you know better than that.
See also
astonished at, shocked at, impressed with, at

susceptible to, susceptible of#

Pronunciation
/səˈsɛptəbəl tu, səˈsɛptəbəl ʌv/ · suh-SEP-tuh-bul too, suh-SEP-tuh-bul uv · weak /səˈsɛptəbəl tə/ and /səˈsɛptəbəl əv/
Русский
подверженный чему-л. (to); допускающий что-л., поддающийся (of); книжн.
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
susceptible to is neutral and everyday in medicine and engineering; susceptible of is formal and now largely legal and literary
Complement
after to, a noun phrase naming the harm or influence you are open to, or a gerund (susceptible to being talked into things). After of, a noun naming what the thing admits — susceptible of proof, susceptible of two readings — and a gerund is not used there. Never a to-infinitive after either: ✗ susceptible to be persuaded.
Pattern
be susceptible to [disease / influence / damage] · be susceptible of [proof / interpretation / improvement] · particularly / especially susceptible to · susceptibility to [noun] — the noun frame keeps to
Variants
vulnerable to and prone to replace the to frame; open to and capable of replace the of frame; liable to covers both loosely; at risk of is the public-health phrase.
Contrast
To for what can happen to it; of for what it will admit. Old pipe is susceptible to corrosion (it can be attacked); a clause is susceptible of two readings (it will bear them). The of* frame is the older and rarer one, and it is nearly always followed by an abstract noun like proof, interpretation, division, or improvement. Against vulnerable to, which stresses harm and defenselessness, susceptible to is neutral enough for engineering and for personality (susceptible to flattery). ✗ susceptible for is an error.

Open to being affected by something, in the to frame; capable of admitting something, in the of frame.

  1. Older patients are more susceptible to the complication than the trial suggested.
  2. Galvanized fittings are susceptible to corrosion where they meet copper.
  3. He's susceptible to flattery from anyone who mentions the boat.
  4. The sentence is susceptible of at least two readings.
  5. The claim is not susceptible of proof one way or the other.
See also
vulnerable to, immune from, immune to, amenable to, sensitive to, to

suspicious of#

Pronunciation
/səˈspɪʃəs ʌv/ · suh-SPISH-uss uv · weak /səˈspɪʃəs əv/
Русский
относящийся с подозрением к, не доверяющий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (suspicious of being followed) — never a to-infinitive. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective only in the sense "suspecting": suspicious that something had been moved.
Pattern
be suspicious of [person / thing] · be suspicious about [a matter] · grow / become suspicious of [noun] · look suspicious (no complement) · suspicion of [noun] — the noun keeps of
Variants
wary of and leery of are the cautious versions; skeptical of / about is the intellectual one; distrustful of is the formal one.
Contrast
Suspicious of is what you feel about someone; suspicious about is what you feel about a matter; and suspicious alone means looking guilty. The last is the ambiguity worth naming: He was suspicious* can mean either that he suspected something or that he seemed suspect, and only context decides — the second sense usually appears as He looked suspicious. ✗ suspicious to and ✗ suspicious from are errors.

Not trusting someone or something, and having reasons.

  1. The bank was suspicious of three transfers in one afternoon.
  2. She's suspicious of anyone who volunteers to take the minutes.
  3. Investigators grew suspicious about the timing of the sale.
  4. He was suspicious of being sent on an errand at that hour.
  5. Everyone was suspicious of the offer and nobody said so.
See also

sympathetic to#

Pronunciation
/ˌsɪmpəˈθɛtɪk tu/ · sim-puh-THET-ik too · weak /ˌsɪmpəˈθɛtɪk tə/
Русский
сочувствующий кому-л.; благосклонный к делу, идее
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a noun naming a person, group, or cause after to and toward — never a gerund and never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
be sympathetic to [cause / person] · be sympathetic toward [person] · be sympathetic with [a position] — less common · sympathize with [person] — the verb takes with
Variants
toward is the American spelling and towards the British; both are read as ordinary in the United States, with toward the house style of most American publishers. Sympathetic to is the commoner of the two in American writing.
Contrast
The adjective takes to* or toward; the verb takes with. ✓ sympathetic to the strikers*, ✓ sympathize with the strikers, ✗ sympathize to the strikers. That split is the whole difficulty. A second sense of the adjective means favorably disposed rather than compassionate — a judge sympathetic to the defense — and it is the one used in political writing. The unrelated technical sense (the sympathetic nervous system) takes no preposition.

Feeling or showing understanding for someone, or favorably disposed toward a cause.

  1. The committee was sympathetic to the tenants but bound by the ordinance.
  2. She's sympathetic toward anyone trying to get a permit out of that office.
  3. The paper has always been sympathetic to the union's position.
  4. He was sympathetic to the argument without agreeing with the conclusion.
  5. I sympathize with the family — the verb, with with.
See also
sensitive to, kind to, sorry for, to

symptomatic of#

Pronunciation
/ˌsɪmptəˈmætɪk ʌv/ · simp-tuh-MAT-ik uv · weak /ˌsɪmptəˈmædək əv/, with the t flapped: "simp-tuh-MAD-ik-uv"
Русский
симптоматичный для — служащий признаком более глубокой причины
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal; medical in origin and now at least as common in commentary about institutions (symptomatic of a deeper problem)
Complement
a noun phrase naming the underlying condition or problem, or a gerund (symptomatic of having grown too fast) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ symptomatic of to grow too fast.
Pattern
[sign] be symptomatic of [condition] · symptomatic of a larger / deeper / wider [noun] — the commentary cliché · symptomatic — the clinical bare use, meaning showing symptoms (symptomatic patients) · asymptomatic — the negative, a word every American learned in 2020
Variants
indicative of is the neutral evidential word; a symptom of is the noun phrase and takes of too; emblematic of is the loaded version; a sign of is the plain one.
Contrast
Symptomatic of names the sign of a condition that lies underneath, which is why the figurative use is so common and so lazy: symptomatic of a deeper problem* is a way of not naming the problem. Against indicative of, which points at any conclusion, symptomatic of implies a whole disease behind the sign. Note the clinical bare use, where symptomatic means "having symptoms" and takes no complement at all — symptomatic patients were tested first — which is a different construction from this one. ✗ symptomatic to and ✗ symptomatic for are errors.

Serving as a sign of a larger condition lying underneath.

  1. The rash is symptomatic of the reaction, not of the infection itself.
  2. Three resignations in a month are symptomatic of something nobody wants to name.
  3. The backlog is symptomatic of a system built for half the volume.
  4. His answer was symptomatic of how the department handles complaints.
  5. Late shipments were symptomatic of a supplier who had already given up.
See also
indicative of, suggestive of, illustrative of, characteristic of, of

synonymous with#

Pronunciation
/sɪˈnɑnəməs wɪθ/ · sih-NON-uh-mus with · weak /səˈnɑnəməs wəθ/ — note that the adjective stresses the second syllable while the noun synonym stresses the first
Русский
синонимичный чему-л.; об имени — ставший символом какого-л. качества
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; a linguistic term and, far more often, a journalistic idiom about reputations
Complement
a noun phrase, or a gerund (synonymous with cutting corners) — never a to-infinitive: ✗ synonymous with cut corners.
Pattern
be synonymous with [word / thing] · practically / virtually / now synonymous with · a synonym for [word] — the noun, which switches to for
Variants
interchangeable with is the strict linguistic claim; equivalent to claims equal value; identified with is the reputational sense stated plainly; a byword for is the idiom for the journalistic use.
Contrast
Adjective with, noun for. ✓ synonymous with, ✓ a synonym for, ✗ a synonym with, ✗ synonymous for. In strict use the word claims two expressions mean the same thing; in the commoner journalistic use it claims that a name has come to stand for a quality — a town synonymous with steel. Against equivalent to, which is quantitative, synonymous with is about meaning and reputation. ✗ synonymous to is an error and a frequent one.

Meaning the same thing as something else — or, of a name, so associated with a quality that it stands for it.

  1. In everyday speech, begin is synonymous with start, though the registers differ.
  2. The family name became synonymous with the mill and closed with it.
  3. That neighborhood was for decades synonymous with cheap rent.
  4. Speed is not synonymous with haste, whatever the schedule says.
  5. His byline is synonymous with stories nobody else would take.
See also
equivalent to, identical to, tantamount to, similar to, with

tangent to#

Pronunciation
/ˈtændʒənt tu/ · TAN-junt too · weak /ˈtændʒənt tə/
Русский
касательный к — мат., касающийся кривой в одной точке
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
technical — geometry and calculus, where a line tangent to a curve touches it at exactly one point; the figurative work is done by the noun tangent and by tangential to
Complement
a noun phrase naming the curve or surface touched — never a gerund and never a clause.
Pattern
[line] be tangent to [curve] · a plane tangent to [surface] · the tangent line at [point] · go off on a tangent — the idiom, with no complement · tangential to [noun] — the figurative adjective
Variants
tangential to is the form for the figurative sense, meaning only slightly connected (a question tangential to the motion); touching is the literal paraphrase; osculating is the specialist's word for a closer contact in differential geometry.
Contrast
Tangent to stays mathematical; tangential to carries the figurative sense. A line is tangent to a circle; a remark is tangential to the discussion, and using tangent to* for the second reads as an error to anyone who took calculus. Against perpendicular to and parallel to, which describe fixed relations between straight lines, tangent to describes contact at a single point on a curve. The related American idiom go off on a tangent keeps the image: you leave the curve along the line that just touched it.

Touching a curve or surface at exactly one point without crossing it.

  1. Draw the line tangent to the circle at the point where they meet.
  2. The derivative gives the slope of the line tangent to the curve.
  3. A plane tangent to the sphere touches it at a single point.
  4. The road runs tangent to the reservoir for a quarter mile.
  5. His question was tangential to the agenda, which is the figurative form.
See also
perpendicular to, parallel to, orthogonal to, adjacent to, to

tantamount to#

Pronunciation
/ˈtæntəˌmaʊnt tu/ · TAN-tuh-mownt too · weak /ˈtænəˌmaʊnt tə/ — the first t after n often drops in speech: "TAN-uh-mownt"
Русский
равносильный чему-л. — книжн., по сути то же, но названное мягче
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
formal to journalistic; almost always used to characterize an act as something worse than it was called
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund — tantamount to admitting the whole thing ✓ — and never a to-infinitive: ✗ tantamount to admit the whole thing. The gerund is the commoner complement, which makes this one of the highest-value entries in the file for the -ing rule.
Pattern
be tantamount to [noun / verb-ing] · amount to [noun / verb-ing] — the near-identical verb · tantamount to a [confession / declaration / admission] — the standard collocations
Variants
amounts to is the plain verb and does the same work in ordinary prose; equivalent to is neutral; in effect is the adverbial paraphrase (in effect a confession); no better than is the blunt version.
Contrast
Tantamount to is a judgment, not a measurement. Where equivalent to says two things are equal in value, tantamount to* says an act has the force of something graver — which is why the complement is nearly always negative: tantamount to fraud, tantamount to a threat, tantamount to giving up. The word has no attributive use: ✗ a tantamount admission is not English. And the to is a preposition every time, so the -ing form is obligatory: ✗ tantamount to say nothing, ✓ tantamount to saying nothing.

Equal in effect to something graver than what it was called.

  1. Saying nothing at that point was tantamount to agreeing.
  2. Refusing the inspection is tantamount to an admission.
  3. The transfer was tantamount to a gift, whatever the paperwork called it.
  4. Cutting the route is tantamount to stranding half the riders.
  5. Her memo was tantamount to accusing the vendor of padding the invoices.
See also
equivalent to, synonymous with, equal to, amenable to, to

terrible at#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɛrəbəl æt/ · TAIR-uh-bull at · weak /ˈtɛrəbəl ət/
Русский
ужасно плохой в чём-л. (at); terrible for вредный, terrible with не умеющий обращаться с
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; emphatic
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after at (terrible at keeping secrets) — never a to-infinitive. After for, a person or thing harmed (terrible for morale). After with, a category handled badly.
Pattern
be terrible at [skill / verb-ing] · be terrible for [person / thing] · be terrible with [category] · be terrible about [verb-ing] — chronically negligent · terrible alone: a terrible mistake
Variants
awful at, horrible at, bad at, and hopeless at are the same word at different volumes; no good at is the flat one.
Contrast
Terrible at / for / with splits exactly as good at / for / with and bad at / for / with do — skill, harm, and category respectively — so learning one of the three learns all three. ✗ terrible in math* is an error in American English. Note the about frame, which reports a chronic failure to do something rather than a lack of skill: terrible about returning calls means you can, but don't.

Very bad at something, or very bad for something.

  1. I'm terrible at estimating how much paint a room takes.
  2. That schedule is terrible for anyone with a second job.
  3. He's terrible with names and excellent with faces.
  4. She's terrible about checking the shared calendar.
  5. We're both terrible at saying no to a weekend project.
See also
bad at, hopeless at, poor at, good at, at

thankful for#

Pronunciation
/ˈθæŋkfəl fɔr/ · THANK-full for · weak /ˈθæŋkfəl fər/
Русский
благодарный за, с облегчением радующийся тому, что
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to warm
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after for (thankful for having somewhere to go) — never a to-infinitive after for. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (thankful to be home) and a that-clause (thankful that nobody was hurt).
Pattern
be thankful for [thing] · be thankful to [verb] · be thankful (that) [clause] · be thankful to [a benefactor] — restricted · thankfully as a sentence adverb
Variants
grateful for is the close twin and additionally takes a person with to; appreciative of is the formal one.
Contrast
Thankful takes for, and, unlike grateful, it does not comfortably take a person. ✓ grateful to the neighbors*, and ✗ thankful to the neighbors is odd — the exception is thankful to God, a fixed religious phrase. That is the whole difference between the two words grammatically; in tone, thankful is warmer and more personal, grateful more likely to acknowledge a specific debt. ✗ thankful of and ✗ thankful about are errors.

Feeling relief and gratitude that something is the case.

  1. We're thankful for every dry day this month.
  2. She's thankful for having had those last two summers with him.
  3. I'm thankful to be back in a house with working heat.
  4. They were thankful that the storm turned before the harbor.
  5. Be thankful for small mercies and a short agenda.
See also
grateful to, glad about, happy about, for

tired of#

Pronunciation
/ˈtaɪərd ʌv/ · TY-urd uv · weak /ˈtaɪərd əv/
Русский
которому надоело что-л.; tired from — физически утомлённый чем-л.
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund after of (tired of waiting) and after from (tired from standing all day) — never a to-infinitive after either. ✗ tired to wait is an error; too tired to wait is a different, correct construction.
Pattern
be tired of [noun / verb-ing] — fed up · be tired from [noun / verb-ing] — physically worn out · get tired of [noun] · sick and tired of [noun] · too tired to [verb]
Variants
sick of and fed up with are the impatience versions; worn out from and exhausted from are the physical ones.
Contrast
Tired of is impatience; tired from is exhaustion. Tired of the drive* means you'd rather not do it again; tired from the drive means you need to sit down. The two are constantly confused and the difference is total. A third frame, too tired to + verb, uses an infinitive and belongs to too … to, not to this adjective. ✗ tired with is an error in American English, though it appears in older British prose.

Out of patience with something, or physically worn out by it.

  1. She's tired of explaining why the office closes at four.
  2. He was tired from three days of moving furniture.
  3. I'm tired of being the only one who reads the agenda.
  4. They were tired from the flight and useless until noon.
  5. Voters are tired of hearing about a bridge that never gets built.
See also
sick of, fed up with, bored with, of, from

typical of#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɪpɪkəl ʌv/ · TIP-ih-kull uv · weak /ˈtɪpɪkəl əv/
Русский
типичный для, характерный для — часто со вздохом: «как всегда»
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun; a gerund where a behavior is meant (typical of showing up unannounced is possible but heavy) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause. The it-frame takes an infinitive: It's typical of him to volunteer.
Pattern
be typical of [person / group / period] · [noun] typical of [noun] · it is typical of [someone] to [verb] · typically [adjective / verb] · That's typical.
Variants
characteristic of is the neutral formal twin; representative of is the statistical one; par for the course is the idiomatic complaint.
Contrast
Typical of is very often a complaint; characteristic of almost never is. That's typical of them* usually means they have let you down again, and the bare That's typical is a pure grumble. Note also the difference from representative of: a typical case is an ordinary one, while a representative sample is one chosen to stand for the whole. ✗ typical for is an error in this sense, though typical for this time of year is correct and means normal for that period — a genuinely different frame worth knowing.

Showing the usual qualities of a person, group, or kind — often said with a sigh.

  1. The long lead-in is typical of his later work.
  2. Rain like this is typical of early spring here.
  3. It's typical of her to have read the whole file before the meeting.
  4. That kind of answer is typical of the whole department.
  5. Snow in October is typical for this elevation, if not for this state.
See also
characteristic of, similar to, known for, of

upset about#

Pronunciation
/ʌpˈsɛt əˈbaʊt/ · up-SET uh-BOWT · weak /ʌpˈsɛt əˈbaʊt/ — the adjective stresses the second syllable, unlike the noun upset /ˈʌpsɛt/
Русский
расстроенный, огорчённый чем-л. — от лёгкой досады до настоящего горя
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about, over, and by (upset about being left out) — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. A person follows with. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: upset that nobody called.
Pattern
be upset about [situation / verb-ing] · be upset with [person] · be upset by [the cause] · be upset over [situation] · be upset (that) [clause] · get upset about [noun]
Variants
bothered by, rattled by, and thrown by are the milder ones; distraught over is the intensified one and prefers over.
Contrast
The familiar split, with one extra option. About and over take the situation, with takes the person, by takes the cause acting on you. Upset with her sister about the will fills two slots at once. Over is slightly more emotional than about and is the newspaper's choice for grief and grievance. ✗ upset of is an error, and the medical an upset stomach is a different use with no preposition at all.

Distressed or annoyed — the general-purpose word covering everything from mild irritation to real grief.

  1. Parents are upset about how late the schedule was announced.
  2. She's upset with her brother and won't say why.
  3. He was visibly upset by the question.
  4. The whole neighborhood is upset over the tree removal.
  5. Don't get upset about something you can't change before Friday.
See also
angry with, annoyed with, sad about, worried about, about

used to#

Pronunciation
/just tu/ · YOOST too · weak /just tə/, in ordinary speech /ˈjustə/ — note the adjective is /just/ with the s of loose, not /juzd/ with the z of used a pencil
Русский
привыкший к (прилагательное, дальше герундий); либо «раньше, бывало» — о прошлой привычке
Type
adjective + preposition — and, spelled identically, a semi-modal of the habitual past
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (used to working nights) — never a to-infinitive after the adjective. ✗ I'm used to work nights is the error. The semi-modal used to, by contrast, takes a bare infinitive and no be: I used to work nights, meaning I no longer do.
Pattern
be / get / become used to [noun / verb-ing] · used to [bare verb] — habitual past · didn't use to [verb] · never used to [verb] · not used to [noun / verb-ing]
Variants
accustomed to is the formal equivalent of the adjective and behaves identically. Would covers part of the habitual-past sense, but only for repeated actions, not for states: ✓ We used to live there, ✗ We would live there.
Contrast
This is the most consequential ambiguity in the file. Look at what precedes and what follows. Preceded by be*, get, or become, and followed by a noun or -ing: the adjective, meaning accustomed — I'm used to the noise*, She got used to driving in it. *
Preceded by a bare subject and followed by a bare verb
the semi-modal, meaning a discontinued habit — I used to smoke*. The negative and interrogative of the semi-modal drop the d in American writing: didn't use to, did he use to — though never used to is commoner in speech. And there is no present tense of the semi-modal: ✗ I use to smoke for a current habit is an error; say I usually smoke.

Accustomed to something — or, in the identical-looking semi-modal, in the habit of doing something in the past.

  1. She's used to the noise from the interstate by now.
  2. I'm not used to getting up before the alarm.
  3. He got used to driving on the left in about a week.
  4. We used to eat there every Friday before it closed.
  5. There didn't use to be a light at that intersection.
See also
accustomed to, familiar with, comfortable with, look forward to, to

useful for#

Pronunciation
/ˈjusfəl fɔr/ · YOOS-full for · weak /ˈjusfəl fər/
Русский
полезный для (цели); useful to — полезный кому-л., useful in — в какой-л. обстановке
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a noun naming a purpose, or a gerund (useful for getting into tight corners) after for; a person after to — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive in the frame useful to know, which describes what may be done, not who benefits.
Pattern
be useful for [purpose / verb-ing] · be useful to [person] · be useful in [a situation or process] · it is useful to [verb] · make oneself useful
Variants
helpful for / to splits the same way; handy for is the informal one; of use to is the formal noun frame.
Contrast
For names the purpose; to names the person who benefits; in names the setting. Useful for removing rust*, useful to anyone with an old car, useful in a pinch. The infinitive frame is a fourth thing: It's useful to know the code means knowing it helps, and its subject is the impersonal it, not a person. ✗ useful of is an error.

Serving a purpose, or being of help to someone.

  1. That attachment is useful for getting into corners.
  2. Her notes were useful to everyone who came after her.
  3. The old map turned out to be useful in ways nobody expected.
  4. It's useful to know which door they actually unlock at seven.
  5. A second set of hands is useful for about an hour of this job.

vested with#

Pronunciation
/ˈvɛstɪd wɪθ/ · VEST-id with · weak /ˈvɛstəd wəθ/
Русский
наделённый чем-л. — полномочием, властью, правом; юр./офиц.
Type
adjective (past participle) + preposition
Register
formal and legal; the natural home is a statute, a charter, or a ceremony — by the power vested in me
Complement
a noun phrase naming the power, authority, or interest conferred — never a gerund and never a clause. The recipient goes in the subject, and the source, if named, in a by-phrase.
Pattern
be vested with [authority / power] · vest [power] in [person] — the converse frame, where the power is the object and the person takes in · by the power vested in me by [authority] — the wedding formula · a vested interest in [noun] · fully vested — the retirement-plan sense, with no complement
Variants
endowed with covers qualities and funds; invested with is the same word in its longer form and is used of dignity and symbolism (invested with meaning); granted and conferred on are the plain verbs.
Contrast
Vested with takes the power; vested in takes the person. The board is vested with authority to set rates; authority to set rates is vested in the board — same fact, mirrored. Against endowed with, which suits qualities and gifts, vested with* suits offices and legal powers. Note the American benefits sense, where an employee becomes vested after a period of service and takes no complement at all, and the idiom a vested interest in, which means a stake worth protecting.

Formally granted a power, an authority, or an interest.

  1. The commission is vested with authority to subpoena records.
  2. By the power vested in me by the state — the converse frame, with in.
  3. The trustee is vested with discretion over distributions.
  4. No single office is vested with the power to waive the requirement.
  5. The council is vested with everything the charter took from the mayor.
See also

vulnerable to#

Pronunciation
/ˈvʌlnərəbəl tu/ · VUL-nur-uh-bull too · weak /ˈvʌlnərəbəl tə/
Русский
уязвимый для, беззащитный перед
Type
adjective + preposition
Register

Complement

Vulnerable to names what can hurt you; at risk of names what might happen to you. Open to being harmed by something.

  1. Older homes on that street are vulnerable to basement flooding.
  2. The system was vulnerable to a fairly obvious attack for two years.

worried about#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɜrid əˈbaʊt/ · WUR-eed uh-BOWT · weak /ˈwɜrid əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈwɜrid ˈbaʊt/
Русский
беспокоящийся о том, что может пойти не так
Type
adjective (participial) + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after about (worried about losing the deposit) and after by — never a to-infinitive after a preposition. A that-clause attaches to the bare adjective: worried that the check hadn't cleared.
Pattern
be worried about [noun / verb-ing] · be worried by [the thing causing the worry] · be worried for [someone's welfare] · be worried (that) [clause] · get worried about [noun] · worry [someone] (no preposition on the object)
Variants
concerned about is the more formal twin; anxious about the more clinical one; nervous about the version reserved for a specific upcoming event.
Contrast
About names the topic of the worry; by names what produced it; for names the person you fear for. Worried about the results* is ordinary; worried by the results means the results themselves disturbed you and is what a doctor says; worried for her is on her behalf. The by frame is the one that reports an active cause, exactly as with annoyed by and bored by. ✗ worried of and ✗ worried with are errors.

Anxious about something that might go wrong.

  1. She's worried about the second mortgage more than the first.
  2. Doctors were worried by the pattern in the second scan.
  3. He's worried for his parents, who haven't left the house all winter.
  4. I'm worried about signing anything before the survey comes back.
  5. Nobody was worried that the vote would fail; they were worried about the margin.

worthy of#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɜrði ʌv/ · WUR-thee uv · weak /ˈwɜrði əv/, and before a consonant often /ˈwɜrði ə/ — "WUR-thee-uh-note"
Русский
достойный чего-л., заслуживающий
Type
adjective + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; ordinary in reviews, recommendations, and argument
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (worthy of being taken seriously) — never a to-infinitive after of: ✗ worthy of to be considered. A separate and much older frame takes a bare infinitive with no of at all (worthy to be called a son), which survives in scripture and in deliberate archaism.
Pattern
be worthy of [noun / verb-ing] · hardly / richly / well worthy of · worthy of note / worthy of mention / worthy of consideration — the fixed collocations · worth [noun / verb-ing] — the different adjective, with no preposition
Variants
deserving of is the close twin and stresses what conduct has earned; meriting is the participial version; worth replaces the whole phrase in ordinary speech and takes no of.
Contrast
Worthy of takes of; worth takes nothing. ✓ worth a look*, ✓ worthy of a look, ✗ worth of a look, ✗ worthy a look except in the frozen literary worthy a second glance. That is the trap, and it catches native writers. Against deserving of, which is about desert earned by conduct, worthy of can simply weigh quality: a sequel worthy of the original has met a standard, not earned a reward. Note the common compound suffix -worthy — newsworthy, trustworthy, seaworthy — where the complement has moved in front and the of disappears.

Good enough or important enough to deserve something.

  1. The proposal is worthy of a closer look than it got.
  2. Two findings in the audit are worthy of mention in the cover letter.
  3. That's a performance worthy of the room it was played in.
  4. Her record is worthy of being read aloud at the ceremony.
  5. Nothing in the complaint is worthy of the attention it has drawn.
